The Denotational Semantics of SSA
Jad Ghalayini, Neel Krishnaswami

Abstract
. Static single assignment form, or SSA, has been the dominant compiler intermediate representation for decades. In this paper, we give a type theory for a variant of SSA, including its equational theory, which are strong enough to validate a variety of control and data flow transformations. We also give a categorical semantics for SSA, and show that the type theory is sound and complete with respect to the categorical axiomatization. We demonstrate the utility of our model by exhibiting a variety of concrete models satisfying our axioms, including in particular a model of TSO weak memory. The correctness of the syntactic metatheory, as well as the completeness proof has been mechanized in the Lean proof assistant.

This paper is dedicated to the memory of Alan Jeffrey, who
taught us about both premonoidal categories and the semantics of weak memory, and
who never shied away from either theory or implementation.

1.
Introduction

Static single assignment form, or SSA form, has been the dominant compiler intermediate
representation since its introduction by
Alpern et al. (1988)
and
Rosen et al. (1988)

in the late 1980s. Most major compilers – GCC, Clang, MLIR, Cranelift – use this representation,
because it makes many optimizations much easier to do than traditional 3-address code IRs.

The key idea behind SSA is to adapt an idea from functional programming: namely, every variable is
defined only once. This means that substitution is unconditionally valid, without first requiring a
dataflow analysis to compute where definitions reach. Unlike in functional programming, though,
scoping of definitions in SSA is traditionally not lexical. Instead, scoping is determined by

dominance
: every variable occurrence must be dominated by a single assignment in the control
flow graph.

The semantics of SSA has traditionally been handled quite informally, because conceptually, it is a
simple first-order imperative programming language. As a result, whether a rewrite is sound or not
is usually obvious, without having to do a complex correctness argument.

Unfortunately, computers are no longer as simple as they were in the late 1980s. Modern computers
are typically multicore, and feature many levels of caching, and as a result the semantics of memory
is no longer correctly modelled as a big array of bytes. Finding good semantics for modern weak
memory systems remains an ongoing challenge.

As a result, it is not correct to justify compiler optimizations in terms of a simple imperative
model, and it is an open question which equations should hold of an SSA program. This is a
particularly fraught question, because it is also unclear which equations weak memory models should
satisfy.

What we would like to know is which equations any SSA representation should satisfy. This would let
us establish a contract between compiler writers and hardware designers. The compiler writers could
rely upon the equational theory of SSA when justifying optimizations, without needing to know all
the details of the memory model at all times. Conversely, memory models could be validated by
seeing if they satisfy the equations of SSA, without needing to study every possible compiler
optimization.

Concretely, our contributions are as follows:

•

First, we give a type-theoretic presentation of SSA, with both typing rules (in
Section
3
) and an equational theory (in Section
4
) for
well-typed terms. We also prove the correctness of suitable substitution properties for this
calculus.

•

Next, in Section
5
, we give a categorical semantics for this type theory, in
terms of distributive Elgot categories. We show that any denotational model with this categorical
structure is also a model of SSA. This shows that all of the equations we give are sound with
respect to the categorical structure.

•

We also show, in Section
5.5
, that syntax quotiented by the equational
theory yields the initial distributive Elgot category. This establishes that our set of syntactic
equations is complete, and that there are no equations which the denotational semantics validates,
but which cannot be proved syntactically.

•

We proceed in Section
6
to show that this denotational axiomatization is
useful in practice, by giving a variety of concrete models, including a model of TSO weak memory
based on
Kavanagh and Brookes (2018)
in Section
6.3
. This demonstrates that it is possible to give
realistic weak memory models which do not disturb the structure of SSA in fundamental ways.

•

Finally, we have substantially mechanized our proofs using the Lean 4 proof assistant. We have
mechanized proofs of substitution for our type theory, as well as proofs that the syntax forms the
initial model, and that the SPARC TSO semantics forms a valid model of SSA. The denotational
semantics and its proof of the soundness of substitution are done on paper.

2.
Static Single Assignment Form

In this section, we describe SSA form and the isomorphism between the standard
\phi
-node-based
presentation and the more functional
basic blocks with arguments
format. We then discuss
standard dominance-based scoping and how it can be recast as lexical scoping to make it more
amenable to standard type-theoretic treatment. We further generalize this format to allow branching
to arbitrary code rather than only labels, obtaining
A-normal form

(ANF)
(
Flanagan et al., 1993
)
, analogously to the transformation described by

Chakravarty et al. (2003)
. Finally, to allow for substitution, we relax our syntax to
permit arbitrary expression nesting and
\mathsf{let}
-expressions, resulting in
type-theoretic
SSA
, or
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
, which will be the focus of the rest of this paper. A straightforward
argument shows that these extensions add no expressive power; we give a more formal version of this
argument, as well as algorithms to interconvert between
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
, standard SSA, and ANF, in
Section
4.4
.

As a running example, consider the simple imperative program to compute
10!
given in
Figure
1
. Operating directly on an imperative language can be challenging,
since having a uniform representation of code friendly to mechanized optimization and analysis is
often in tension with features designed to improve readability and programmer productivity, such as
syntactic sugar. Early work on compiler intermediate representations, notably by Frances
Allen
(
Allen, 1970
)
, introduced
three-address code
, also known as
register
transfer language (RTL)
, to normalize programs into a form more suitable for analysis and
optimization. We can normalize our code into 3-address code, as in Figure
1(b)
, by:

•

Converting structured control flow (e.g.,
\mathsf{while}
) into unstructured jumps between basic
blocks labelled
\mathsf{start}
,
\mathsf{loop}
, and
\mathsf{body}
.

•

Replacing subexpressions like
i+1
in
a*(i+1)
with
\mathsf{let}
-bindings so that every
expression in our program is atomic.

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{let\;mut}\;i=1;

\displaystyle\mathsf{let\;mut}\;a=1;

\displaystyle\mathsf{while}\;i_{0}<n\;\{

\displaystyle\quad a=a*(i+1)

\displaystyle\quad i=i+1;

\displaystyle\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{ret}\;a

(a)

As an imperative program

\displaystyle\mathsf{start}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{let\;mut}\;i=1;

\displaystyle\mathsf{let\;mut}\;a=1;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}

\displaystyle\mathsf{loop}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{if}\;i<n\;\{\;\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{body}\;\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{\;\mathsf{ret}\;a\;\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{body}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;t=i+1;

\displaystyle a=a*t;

\displaystyle i=i+1;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}

(b)

As 3-address code

Figure 1
.

A simple, slightly suboptimal program to compute
10!
via multiplication in a loop, represented
as typical imperative code and in 3-address code.

While functional languages typically rely on
lexical scoping
, where the scope of a variable
is determined by its position within the code’s nested structure, 3-address code uses a different
scoping mechanism based on
dominance
. In particular, a variable
x
is considered to be in
scope at a specific point
P
if and only if all execution paths from the program’s entry point to

P
pass through a definition
D
for
x
. In this case, we say that the definition
D

strictly dominates

P
. The relation on basic blocks “
A
strictly dominates
B
"
intersected with “
A
is a
direct predecessor
of
B
" forms a tree called the

dominance tree
of the CFG – this can be computed in nearly linear time

(
Cytron et al., 1991
)
.

Despite this normalization, many optimizations remain difficult to express in this format because a
variable’s value may be set by multiple definitions throughout the program’s execution. To improve
our ability to reason about programs, we introduce the
static single assignment
restriction,
originally proposed by
Alpern et al. (1988)
, which states that every variable must be
defined at exactly one point in the program. We can intuitively represent this as every variable
being given by an immutable
\mathsf{let}
-binding. In particular, given a variable
x
, which we can now
associate to its unique definition
D_{x}
,
x
is in scope at a point
P
if and only if
D_{x}

strictly dominates
P
.

One might attempt to convert programs to SSA form by numbering each definition of a variable,
effectively changing references to
x
to references to
x_{t}
, i.e. “
x
at time
t
.” For
example, we could rewrite

(1)

\mathsf{let}\;x=3y+5;\;\mathsf{let}\;x=3x+2;\;\mathsf{let}\;x=3x+1;\;\mathsf{ret}\;x\\
\approx\mathsf{let}\;x_{0}=3y+5;\;\mathsf{let}\;x_{1}=3x_{0}+2;\;\mathsf{let}\;x_{2}=3x_{1}+1;\;\mathsf{ret}\;x_{2}

This transformation enables algebraic reasoning about expressions involving each
x_{t}
. However,
since we can only define a variable once in SSA form, expressing programs with loops and branches
becomes challenging. For example, naïvely trying to lower the program in
Figure
1(b)
into SSA form would not work, since the
i
definition
i=i+1
can
refer to
either
the previous value of
i
from the last iteration of the loop
or
the
original value
i=1
. The classical solution is to introduce
\phi
-nodes
, which select a
value based on the predecessor block from which control arrived. We give the lowering of our program
into SSA with
\phi
-nodes in Figure
2(b)
.

Cytron et al. (1991)
introduced the first efficient algorithm to lower a program in 3-address
code to valid SSA while introducing a minimum number of
\phi
-nodes, making SSA practical for
widespread use as an intermediate representation. Unfortunately,
\phi
-nodes do not have an obvious
operational semantics. Additionally, they require us to adopt more complex scoping rules than simple
dominance-based scoping. For example, in basic block
\mathsf{loop}
in Figure
2(b)
,
i_{0}

evaluates to 1 if we came from
\mathsf{start}
and to
i_{1}
if we came from
\mathsf{body}
. Similarly,
a_{0}

evaluates to either 1 or
a_{1}
based on the predecessor block. This does not obey dominance-based
scoping, since
i_{0}
and
i_{1}
are defined
after
the
\phi
-nodes
i_{0}
,
a_{0}
that reference
them, which seems counterintuitive – after all, variables are typically used after they are
defined. In fact, since the value of a
\phi
-node is determined by which basic block is our
immediate predecessor, we instead need to use the rule that expressions in
\phi
-node branches with
source
S
can use any variable
y
defined at the
end
of
S
. Note that this is a strict
superset of the variables visible for a normal instruction
x
, which can only use variables
y

which
dominate

x
– i.e., such that
every
path from the entry block to the
definition of
x
goes through
y
, rather than only those paths which also go through
S
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{start}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{let\;mut}\;i=1;

\displaystyle\mathsf{let\;mut}\;a=1;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}

\displaystyle\mathsf{loop}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{if}\;i<n\;\{\;\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{body}\;\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{\;\mathsf{ret}\;a\;\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{body}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;t=i+1;

\displaystyle a=a*t;

\displaystyle i=i+1;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}

(a)

3-address code

\displaystyle\mathsf{start}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}

\displaystyle\mathsf{loop}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;i_{0}=\phi(\mathsf{start}:1,\mathsf{body}:i_{1})

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;a_{0}=\phi(\mathsf{start}:1,\mathsf{body}:a_{1})

\displaystyle\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{\;\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{body}\;\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{\;\mathsf{ret}\;a_{0}\;\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{body}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;t=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;a_{1}=a_{0}*t

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;i_{1}=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}

(b)

Converted to SSA form

Figure 2
.

Conversion of three address code for the program in Figure
1
to SSA
form, requring the insertion of
\phi
-nodes for
i
and
a
due to control-flow dependent
updates. Note how SSA-form can be viewed as “three address code in which all

\mathsf{let}
-bindings are immutable.”

While this rule can be quite confusing, and in particular makes it non-obvious how to assign an
operational semantics to
\phi
-nodes, the fact that the scoping for
\phi
-node branches is based
on the source block, rather than the block in which the
\phi
-node itself appears, hints at a
possible solution. By
moving
the expression in each branch to the
call-site
, we can
transition to an isomorphic syntax called basic blocks with arguments (BBA), as illustrated in
Figure
3(b)
. In this approach, each
\phi
-node – since it lacks side effects and has
scoping rules independent of its position in the basic block, depending only on the source of each
branch – can be moved to the top of the block. This reorganization allows us to treat each

\phi
-node as equivalent to an argument for the basic block, with the corresponding values passed
at the jump site. Converting a program from BBA format back to standard SSA form with
\phi
-nodes
is straightforward: introduce a
\phi
-node for each argument of a basic block, and for each branch
corresponding to the
\phi
-node, add an argument to the jump instruction from the appropriate
source block.

This allows us to use standard dominance-based scoping without any special cases for
\phi
-nodes.
When considering basic blocks, this means that a variable is visible within the block
D
where it
is defined, starting from the point of its definition. It continues to be visible in all subsequent
blocks
P
that are strictly dominated by
D
in the control-flow graph (CFG). For example, in
Figure
3(b)
:

•

\mathsf{start}
strictly dominates
\mathsf{loop}
and
\mathsf{body}
; thus, the variable
n
defined in

\mathsf{start}
is visible in
\mathsf{loop}
and
\mathsf{body}
.

•

\mathsf{loop}
strictly dominates
\mathsf{body}
; therefore, the parameters
i_{0}
,
a_{0}
to
\mathsf{loop}

are visible in
\mathsf{body}
without the need to pass them as parameters.

•

\mathsf{body}
does
not
strictly dominate
\mathsf{loop}
, since there is a path from
\mathsf{start}

to
\mathsf{loop}
that does not pass through
\mathsf{body}
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{start}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}

\displaystyle\mathsf{loop}:\quad

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{let}\;i_{0}=\phi(\mathsf{start}:1,\mathsf{body}:i_{1})

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{let}\;a_{0}=\phi(\mathsf{start}:1,\mathsf{body}:a_{1})

\displaystyle\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{\;\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{body}\;\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{else}\;\{\;\mathsf{ret}\;a_{0}\;\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{body}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;t=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;a_{1}=a_{0}*t

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;i_{1}=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}

(a)

With
\phi
-nodes

\displaystyle\mathsf{start}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1})

\displaystyle\mathsf{loop}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{0}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}a_{0}}):\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{\;\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{body}\;\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{else}\;\{\;\mathsf{ret}\;a_{0}\;\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{body}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;t=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;a_{1}=a_{0}*t

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;i_{1}=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}a_{1}})

(b)

Basic-blocks with arguments

Figure 3
.

The program in Figure
1
written in standard SSA (using
\phi
nodes),
like in LLVM
(
Lattner and Adve, 2004
)
, and in basic-blocks with arguments SSA, like in MLIR
(
Lattner et al., 2021
)
and
Cranelift
(
Team, 2023
)
. The arguments
i_{0},a_{0}
corresponding to the
\phi
-nodes
i_{0},a_{0}
are colored in
red
and
blue
, respectively.

An important insight provided by the BBA format, as discussed by
Appel (1998)
and

Kelsey (1995)
, is that a program in SSA form can in this way be interpreted as a collection
of tail-recursive functions, where each basic block and branch correspond to a function and tail
call, respectively. This interpretation offers a natural framework for defining the semantics of SSA
and reasoning about optimizations. However, there is a subtle difference between the scoping rules
in this format and the actual scoping used in traditional SSA, which requires careful consideration.
By topologically sorting the basic blocks in the CFG according to this partial order and inserting
brackets based on the dominance tree, we can convert dominance-based scoping to lexical scoping. In
this arrangement, a variable is in lexical scope if and only if it is in scope under dominance-based
scoping, as shown in Figure
4
. This transformation is straightforward,
and standard SSA can be recovered by removing the inserted
\mathsf{where}
-blocks.

\displaystyle\mathsf{start}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(1,1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{loop}(i_{0},a_{0}):\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{\;\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{body}\;\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{else}\;\{\;\mathsf{ret}\;a_{0}\;\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{body}:\quad

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;t=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;a_{1}=a_{0}*t

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;i_{1}=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{1},a_{1})

(a)

Dominance-based scoping

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(1,1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{where}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{0},a_{0}):\{

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{\;\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{body}\;\}

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{else}\;\{\;\mathsf{ret}\;a_{0}\;\}

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{where}\;\mathsf{body}:\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;t=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;a_{1}=a_{0}*t

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;i_{1}=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{1},a_{1})

\displaystyle\quad\}

\displaystyle\}

(b)

Lexical scoping

Figure 4
.
Conversion of an SSA program from dominance-based scoping to explicit lexical scoping

Lexical scoping allows us to apply many of the techniques developed in theoretical computer science
and functional programming for reasoning about and developing optimizations and analysis passes –
in particular, the result of our conversion to lexical scoping looks a lot like the correspondence
between SSA and CPS described in
Kelsey (1995)
. We can use this correspondence to guide us
in developing an
equational theory
for SSA programs, with the goal of enabling
compositional reasoning about program transformations such as:

•

Control-flow rewrites
, such as jump-threading or fusing two identical branches of
an
\mathsf{if}
-statement

•

Algebraic rewrites
, such as simplifying arithmetic expressions

•

Combinations of the two, such as rewriting
\mathsf{if}\;x>0\;\mathsf{then}\;1-x\;\mathsf{else}\;1+x
to
1+\mathsf{abs}(x)
.

We can work towards making these easier to express by generalizing our syntax to allow the branches
of if-statements to contain arbitrary code, rather than just unconditional branches, as in
Figure
5
. This clearly adds no additional expressive power, since:

•

This syntax clearly generalizes the previous syntax, so no conversion into it is necessary

•

To revert back to the less general syntax, one must simply introduce new anonymous basic
blocks for each branch of the if-statement, likeso:

(2)

\mathsf{if}\;e\;\{s\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{t\}\to(\mathsf{if}\;e\;\{\mathsf{br}\;\ell_{\top}\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{\mathsf{br}\;\ell_{\bot}\})\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell_{\top}:\{s\},\;\ell_{\bot}:\{t\}

What we end up with is something which looks a lot like
administrative normal form
(ANF),
with our transformation analogous to that described in
Chakravarty et al. (2003)
. The
key difference is that, in our format (which is strictly first order), we require an explicit

\mathsf{ret}
instruction (rather than adopting an expression-oriented language), and write
“
\mathsf{let\;rec}\;f(x)=e;t
" as “
t\;\mathsf{where}\;f(x):\{e\}
."

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(1,1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{where}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{0},a_{0}):\{

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{\;\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{body}\;\}

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{else}\;\{\;\mathsf{ret}\;a_{0}\;\}

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{where}\;\mathsf{body}:\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;t=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;a_{1}=a_{0}*t

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;i_{1}=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{1},a_{1})

\displaystyle\quad\}

\displaystyle\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(1,1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{where}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{0},a_{0}):\{

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;t=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;a_{1}=a_{0}*t

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;i_{1}=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{1},a_{1})

\displaystyle\quad\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{ret}\;a_{0}

\displaystyle\quad\}

\displaystyle\}

Figure 5
.
Allowing if-statements to jump to arbitrary instructions, rather than a terminator

ANF, however, lacks a good substitution property, since substituting a value for a variable can take
you out of ANF, making it difficult to express optimizations like
(i+1)-1\to i
as rewrite
rules. To fix this, we can simply relax the restriction that expressions in a program must be
atomic. This can again trivially be seen to add no excessive power, since we can always introduce
temporary variables via
\mathsf{let}
-bindings to make any expression atomic. For full generality, we
will also allow
\mathsf{let}
-bindings and
\mathsf{if}
-statements
inside
expressions, which again
can be eliminated in the obvious manner, such as by taking

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;x=(\mathsf{if}\;e\;\{a\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{b\});t

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{if}\;e\;\{\mathsf{let}\;x=a;t\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{\mathsf{let}\;x=b;t\}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{if}\;e\;\{\mathsf{br}\;\ell(x)\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{\mathsf{br}\;\ell(x)\}\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{t\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(1,1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{where}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{0},a_{0}):\{

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;t=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;a_{1}=a_{0}*t

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;i_{1}=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{1},a_{1})

\displaystyle\quad\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{ret}\;a_{0}

\displaystyle\quad\}

\displaystyle\}

(a)

Program in ANF

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(1,1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{where}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{0},a_{0}):\{

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{0}+1,a_{0}*(i_{0}+1))

\displaystyle\quad\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{ret}\;a_{0}

\displaystyle\quad\}

\displaystyle\}

(b)

Programs
6(a)
and
6(b)
after substitution;
since the result is the same, both programs must be equivalent.

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(1,1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{where}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{0},a_{0}):\{

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;i_{1}=i_{0}+1

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{let}\;a_{1}=a_{0}*i_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{1},a_{1})

\displaystyle\quad\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{ret}\;a_{0}

\displaystyle\quad\}

\displaystyle\}

(c)

Optimized ANF program

Figure 6
.

Adding support for expressions, allowing us to perform substitutions of (pure) expressions.
Optimizations such as common subexpression elimination can be built using substitution as a
building block.

This gives us our final
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
calculus, which we will call
type-theoretic SSA
. In
this language, we can safely perform
substitutions
, like in Figure
6
.
These can then be used to build up optimizations such as
common-subexpression elimination
.
More generally, substitution lets us do
algebra
. For example, since we know that:

\displaystyle(i_{0}+1,a_{0}*(i_{0}+1))

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=(i_{0},a_{0})\;\mathsf{in}\;(x+1,y*(x+1)))

\displaystyle(1,1)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=(0,1)\;\mathsf{in}\;(x+1,y*(x+1)))

we can rewrite the program in Figure
7(a)
to that in Figure
7(b)
.
We can then apply general rewrite rules such as
dinaturality
to rewrite
Figure
7(b)
to Figure
7(c)
. This allows us to build up justifications
for complex optimizations, such as rewriting
7(c)
to
6(c)
, in terms of
simple rewriting steps. In particular, we can do
complex
,
error-prone
loop and
control-flow graph optimizations by breaking them down into closed set of simple algebraic steps,
with each step rigorously justified via our denotational semantics

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(1,1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{where}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{0},a_{0}):\{

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{0}+1,a_{0}*(i_{0}+1))

\displaystyle\quad\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{ret}\;a_{0}

\displaystyle\quad\}

\displaystyle\}

(a)

Substituted program from Figure
6(b)

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}\;(0,1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{where}\;\mathsf{loop}(x,y):\{

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{let}\;(i_{0},a_{0})=(x+1,y*(x+1));

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{0},a_{0})

\displaystyle\quad\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{ret}\;a_{0}

\displaystyle\quad\}

\displaystyle\}

(b)

Equivalent to Figure
7(c)
by
dinaturality

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;n=10;

\displaystyle\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=(0,1);

\displaystyle\quad(x+1,y*(x+1))

\displaystyle)

\displaystyle\mathsf{where}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{0},a_{0}):\{

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=(i_{0},a_{0});

\displaystyle\qquad\quad(x+1,y*(x+1))

\displaystyle\qquad)

\displaystyle\quad\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{ret}\;a_{0}

\displaystyle\quad\}

\displaystyle\}

(c)

Equivalent to Figure
7(a)
by substitution

Figure 7
.

Decomposing multi-block rewrites (from
7(c)
to

7(a)
, and therefore to the more optimal program

6(c)
) into simple algebraic steps. By verifying each step, we can
verify complex optimizations through decomposition.

3.
Type Theory

We now give a formal account of
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
, starting with the types. Our types are first order,
and consists of binary sums
A+B
, products
A\otimes B
, the unit type
\mathbf{1}
, and the
empty type
\mathbf{0}
, all parameterised over a set of base types
X\in\mathcal{T}
. We write our set of
types as
\mathsf{Ty}(X)
. We also parameterise over:

•

A set of effects
\epsilon\in\mathcal{E}
, forming a join-semilattice with bottom element
\bot\in\mathcal{E}

•

For each pair
A,B\in\mathsf{Ty}(X)
and effect
\epsilon\in\mathcal{E}
, a
set of
primitive instructions

f\in\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}(A,B)
, where

\epsilon\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\implies\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}(A,B)\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(A,B)
.

We write
\mathcal{I}(A,B)=\bigcup_{\epsilon}\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}(A,B)
,

\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}=\bigcup_{A,B}\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}(A,B)
, and
\mathcal{I}=\bigcup_{\epsilon}\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}
.

We’ll call a tuple
Sg=(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{I})
of types and instructions over these types an

\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
-signature
.

A (variable )
context

\Gamma
is a list of
typing hypotheses

x:{A}^{\epsilon}
, where
x
is a variable name,
A
is the type of that variable, and

\epsilon
is the effect of using that variable (used when filling holes with effectful
expressions). If
\epsilon=\bot
, we often omit it, writing
x:A
. Similarly, we define a

label-context
to be a list of
labels

\ell(A)
, where
A
is the parameter
type that must be passed on a jump to the label
\ell
.

<
A,B,C
> ::=

X

|
A\otimes B

|
\mathbf{1}

|
A+B

|
\mathbf{0}

<
a,b,c,e
> ::=
x

|
f\;a

|
\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;e

|
()

|
(a,b)

|
\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;\;e

|
\iota_{l}\;{a}

|
\iota_{r}\;{a}

|
\mathsf{abort}\;{a}

|
\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:s,\iota_{r}\;{y}:t\}

<
s,t
> ::=
\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;a

|
\mathsf{let}\;x=a;t

|
\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;t

|
\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:s,\iota_{r}\;{y}:t\}

|
t\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}

<
\Gamma
> ::=
\cdot
|
\Gamma,x:{A}^{\epsilon}

<
\mathsf{L}
> ::=
\cdot
|
\mathsf{L},\ell(A)

Figure 8
.
Grammar for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
, parametrized over an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
signature

As shown in Figure
8
,
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
terms are divided into two syntactic
categories, each of with associated with a judgement:

•

Expressions

a,b,c,e
typed with the judgement
\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}
,
which says that under the typing context
\Gamma
, the expression
a
has type
A
and effect

\epsilon
. We say a term is
pure
if it has effect
\bot
; note that whether an expression
is pure or not depends both on the expression itself and on the purity of the variables used in
the expression; this is to allow reasoning about impure substitutions.

•

Regions

r,s,t
, which recursively define a lexically-scoped SSA program with a
single entry and (potentially) multiple exits. This is typed with the judgement

\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}
, which states that given that
\Gamma
is live at the unique entry
point,
r
will either loop forever or branch to one of the exit labels in
\ell(A)\in\mathsf{L}

with an argument of type
A
.

The typing rules for expressions are given in Figure
9
. In particular,
expressions may be built up from the following fairly standard primitives:

•

A variable
x
in the context
\Gamma
, as typed by
var
. We write
(A,\epsilon)\leq(B,\epsilon^{\prime})\iff A=B\epsilon\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
.

•

An
primitive instruction

f\in\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}(A,B)
applied to an expression

\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}
, typed by
op

•

Unary and binary
let-bindings
, typed by
let
1
and
let
2

respectively

•

A
pair
of expressions
\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}
,

\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}
, typed by
pair
. Operationally, we interpret this as
executing
a
, and then
b
, and returning the pair of their values.

•

An empty tuple
()
, which types in any context by
unit

•

Injections, typed by
inl
and
inr

•

Pattern matching on sum types, typed by
case
. Operationally, we interpret this as
executing
e
, and then, if
e
is a left injection
\iota_{l}\;x
, executing
a
with its value
(
x
), otherwise executing
b
.

•

An operator
\mathsf{abort}\;e
allowing us to abort execution if given a value of the empty type. Since the empty type is a 0-ary sum type,
\mathsf{abort}
can be seen as a
\mathsf{case}
with no branches. Since the empty type is uninhabited, execution can never reach an
\mathsf{abort}
. This can be viewed as a typesafe version of the
unreachable
instruction in LLVM IR.

Traditional presentations of SSA use a boolean type instead of sum types. Naturally, booleans can be encoded with sum types as
\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}
. If-then-else is then a
\mathsf{case}
which ignores the unit payloads, so that

\mathsf{if}\;e_{1}\;\{e_{2}\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{e_{3}\}:=\mathsf{case}\;e_{1}\;\{\iota_{l}\;{()}:e_{2},\iota_{r}\;{()}:e_{3}\}
.

\displaystyle\boxed{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}

\displaystyle{\Gamma\;x\leq(A,\epsilon)}\mathstrut

var

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}x:{A}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{f\in\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}(A,B)}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\mathstrut

op

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}f\;a:{B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\mathstrut

let
1

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;b:{B}\mathstrut

unit

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}():{\mathbf{1}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\mathstrut

pair

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}(a,b):{A\otimes B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A\otimes B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}c:{C}}\mathstrut

let
2

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;\;c:{C}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\mathstrut

inl

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\iota_{l}\;{a}:{A+B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\mathstrut

inr

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\iota_{r}\;{b}:{A+B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{\mathbf{0}}}\mathstrut

abort

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{abort}\;{a}:{A}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A+B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{C}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:A\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{C}}\mathstrut

case

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:a,\iota_{r}\;{y}:b\}:{C}\mathstrut

Figure 9
.
Rules for typing
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
expressions

We now move on to
regions
, which can be built up as follows:

•

A branch to a label
\ell
with pure argument
a
, typed with
br
.

•

Unary and binary
let-bindings
, typed by
let
1
and
let
2

respectively

•

Pattern matching on sum types, typed by
case
. Operationally, we interpret this as
executing the (potentially effectful) expression
e
, and then, if
e
is a left injection

\iota_{l}\;x
, executing
r
with its value (
x
), otherwise executing
s
.

•

\mathsf{where}
-statements
of the form “
r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\})_{i}
",
which consist of a collection of mutually recursive regions
\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\}
and a

terminator region

r
which may branch to one of
\ell_{i}
or an exit label.

We previously described SSA programs as being made up out of
basic blocks
, each of which is
made up of a sequence of instructions followed by a
terminator
and, potentially, a list of
strictly dominated basic blocks this terminator may jump to. Basic blocks are an
implicit

feature of our grammar: we can view each as a list of unary or binary let-bindings, until we reach a
terminator, which is either an unconditional branch, a
\mathsf{case}
-statement or a

\mathsf{where}
-statement, as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{defs}(\mathsf{let}\;x=e;r)=(x,e)::r\qquad\mathsf{defs}(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;r)=((x,y),e)::r

\displaystyle\mathsf{defs}(r)=[]\quad\text{otherwise}

\displaystyle\mathsf{terminator}(\mathsf{let}\;x=e;r)=\mathsf{terminator}(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;r)=\mathsf{terminator}(r)

\displaystyle\mathsf{terminator}(r)=r\quad\text{otherwise}

\displaystyle\mathsf{bb}(r)=(\mathsf{defs}(r),\mathsf{terminator}(r))

Note in particular that the region
r
in a
\mathsf{where}
-statement

r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\})_{i}
is best interpreted as a terminator rather than an
entry-block even if of the form, e.g.,
r=\mathsf{let}\;x=e;s
. The entry block is instead the

implicit
basic-block made up of any let-bindings surrounding the
\mathsf{where}
-statement. The
key difference is that variables defined in
r
are
not
visible in the blocks
t_{i}
, whereas
variables defined in the entry-block are. While at first glance this can be unintuitive, the
additional generality greatly simplifies rewriting, and we can always “normalize away” this
feature, since our equational theory generally admits that,
if
both sides are well-typed and
there is no shadowing,

\displaystyle(\mathsf{let}\;x=e;\;r)\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\})_{i}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{let}\;x=e;\;(r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\})_{i}))

\displaystyle(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;\;r)\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\})_{i}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;\;(r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\})_{i}))

\displaystyle\boxed{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\mathsf{L}\;\ell=A}\mathstrut

br

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;a\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

let
1
-r

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A\otimes B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A,y:B\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

let
2
-r

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;r\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A+B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:B\vdash s\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

case-r

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:r,\iota_{r}\;{y}:s\}\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)_{i}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}\vdash t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j}}\mathstrut

cfg

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

Figure 10
.
Rules for typing
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
regions

3.1.
Metatheory

We can now begin to state the syntactic metatheory of
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
. One of the
most important metatheorems, and a basic sanity check of our type theory, is

weakening
; essentially, if things typecheck in a context
\Delta
, and

\Gamma
contains all the variables of
\Delta
(written
\Gamma\leq\Delta
,
pronounced “
\Gamma

weakens

\Delta
”), then these things should
typecheck in the context
\Gamma
as well. That is, “
\Gamma
typechecks more
terms than
\Delta
”.

Making things more formal, we introduce the rules for weakening
\Gamma\leq\Delta
in the first part of Figure
11
:
wk-nil
says
that the empty context weakens itself,
wk-skip
says that if
\Gamma

weakens
\Delta
, then
\Gamma
with an arbitrary variable added also weakens

\Delta
, and
wk-cons
says that if
\Gamma
weakens
\Delta
and

\epsilon\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
, then
\Gamma
with
x:{A}^{\epsilon}
added
weakens
\Delta,x:{A}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}
. It is easy to see that weakening
defined in this manner induces a partial order on contexts.

We may go further and introduce weakening for
label contexts

\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{K}
analogously, except that we will flip the ordering such that
\mathsf{L}

weakens
\mathsf{K}
if it contains
less
, rather than
more
, labels
than
\mathsf{K}
. This is a bit unconventional, since in this case
\mathsf{L}
types

less
regions than
\mathsf{K}
, but it will make our metatheory and
denotational semantics come out more clearly, since it corresponds to
label-contexts being “on the right” (with variable contexts “on the left”).
In particular, in Figure
11
, we introduce the rules

lwk-nil
, which says that the empty label context weakens itself,

lwk-skip
, which says that if
\mathsf{L}
weakens
\mathsf{K}
, then
\mathsf{L}

weakens
\mathsf{K}
with an arbitrary label added, and
lwk-cons
, which says
that if
\mathsf{L}
weakens
\mathsf{K}
, then
\mathsf{L}
with a label
\ell(A)

added weakens
\mathsf{K}
with the same label added. It is easy to see that this
induces a partial order on label contexts.

\displaystyle\boxed{\Gamma\leq\Delta}

wk-nil

\displaystyle\cdot\leq\cdot\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\leq\Delta}\mathstrut

wk-skip

\displaystyle\Gamma,x:{A}^{\epsilon}\leq\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\leq\Delta}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\epsilon\leq\epsilon^{\prime}}\mathstrut

wk-cons

\displaystyle\Gamma,x:{A}^{\epsilon}\leq\Delta,x:{A}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{K}}

lwk-nil

\displaystyle\cdot\leq\cdot\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{K}}\mathstrut

lwk-skip

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{K},\ell(A)\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{K}}\mathstrut

lwk-cons

\displaystyle\mathsf{L},\ell(A)\leq\mathsf{K},\ell(A)\mathstrut

\displaystyle\boxed{\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta}

sb-nil

\displaystyle\cdot:\Gamma\mapsto\cdot\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A}}\mathstrut

sb-cons

\displaystyle\gamma,x\mapsto e:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta,x:{A}^{\epsilon}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\boxed{\Gamma\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}

ls-nil

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\cdot:\cdot\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{K}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\mathstrut

ls-cons

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\sigma,\ell(x)\mapsto r:\mathsf{L},\ell(A)\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}\mathstrut

Figure 11
.
Rules for typing
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
weakening and substitution

We can now state weakening formally as follows:

Lemma 3.1 (Weakening).

Given
\Gamma\leq\Delta
,
\epsilon\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
, and
\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{K}
, we have that:

(a)

\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}\implies\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon^{\prime}}a:{A}

(b)

\Delta\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}\implies\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{K}

(c)

\sigma:\Delta\mapsto\Xi\implies\sigma:\Gamma\mapsto\Xi

(d)

\Delta\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}\implies\Gamma\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}

Proof.

These are formalized as:

(a)

Term.Wf.wk
in
Typing/Term/Basic.lean

(b)

Region.Wf.wk
in
Typing/Region/Basic.lean

(c)

Follows from
Term.Subst.Wf.comp
in
Typing/Term/Subst.lean

(d)

Follows from
Region.Subst.Wf.vsubst
in
Typing/Region/LSubst.lean

∎

If we look at the proof of variable weakening, we might arrive at the alternate statement that all
the variables in
\Delta
are also available with the same type in
\Gamma
, i.e., if

\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}x:{A}\implies\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}x:{A}
, then anything which can be
typed in
\Delta
can be typed in
\Gamma
. More generally, we might ask if every variable

\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}x:{A}
in
\Delta
can be associated with a term

\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\gamma_{x}:{A}
which is well-typed in
\Gamma
. An assignment of such
variables
\gamma:x\mapsto\gamma_{x}
is called a
substitution
, which we can type with the
judgement
\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta
as per the rules given in Figure

10
. In particular,

•

sb-nil
says that the empty substitution takes every context to the empty context.

•

sb-cons
says that if
\gamma
takes
\Gamma
to
\Delta
and

\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A}
, then
\gamma
with the additional substitution
x\mapsto e

adjoined takes
\Gamma
to
\Delta,x:{A}^{\epsilon}

To
use
a substitution, we simply need to perform standard capture-avoiding substitution, as
made explicit in Figure
12
.

\displaystyle(\gamma,x\mapsto e)(x)=e\qquad(\gamma,y\mapsto e)(x)=\gamma(x)\qquad(\cdot)(x)=x

\displaystyle[\gamma]x=\gamma(x)\qquad[\gamma](\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;e)=\mathsf{let}\;x=[\gamma]a;\;[\gamma]e\qquad[\gamma](a,b)=([\gamma]a,[\gamma]b)\qquad[\gamma]()=()

\displaystyle[\gamma](\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;\;e)=\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=[\gamma]a;\;[\gamma]e\qquad[\gamma](\iota_{l}\;{a})=\iota_{l}\;{[\gamma]a}\qquad[\gamma](\iota_{r}\;{b})=\iota_{r}\;{[\gamma]b}

\displaystyle[\gamma](\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:a,\iota_{r}\;{y}:b\})=\mathsf{case}\;[\gamma]e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:[\gamma]a,\iota_{r}\;{y}:[\gamma]b\}

\displaystyle[\gamma](\mathsf{abort}\;{a})=\mathsf{abort}\;{[\gamma]a}

\displaystyle[\gamma](\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;a)=\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;[\gamma]a\qquad[\gamma](\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r)=\mathsf{let}\;x=[\gamma]a;[\gamma]r

\displaystyle[\gamma](\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;r)=\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=[\gamma]e;[\gamma]r

\displaystyle[\gamma](\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:r,\iota_{r}\;{y}:s\})=\mathsf{case}\;[\gamma]e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:[\gamma]r,\iota_{r}\;{y}:[\gamma]s\}

\displaystyle[\gamma](r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})=[\gamma]r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{[\gamma]t_{i}\},)_{i}

\displaystyle[\gamma](\cdot)=\cdot\qquad[\gamma](\gamma^{\prime},x\mapsto e)=([\gamma]\gamma^{\prime},x\mapsto[\gamma]e)

\displaystyle[\gamma](\cdot)=\cdot\qquad[\gamma](\sigma,\ell(x)\mapsto r)=([\gamma]\sigma,\ell(x)\mapsto[\gamma]r)

Figure 12
.

Capture-avoiding substititon for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
terms, regions, and
(label) substitutions; in particular, we assume bound variables and labels
are
\alpha
-converted so as not to appear in
\gamma
/
\sigma
.

This gives us everything we need to state the
substitution lemma
, which
is as follows:

Lemma 3.2 (Substitution).

Given
\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta
, we have that:

(a)

\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}\implies\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}[\gamma]a:{A}

(b)

\Delta\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}\implies\Gamma\vdash[\gamma]r\rhd\mathsf{L}

(c)

\rho:\Delta\mapsto\Xi\implies[\gamma]\rho:\Gamma\mapsto\Xi

(d)

\sigma\vdash\Gamma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}\implies[\gamma]\sigma\vdash\Delta:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}

Proof.

These are formalized as:

(a)

Term.Wf.subst
in
Typing/Term/Subst.lean

(b)

Region.Wf.vsubst
in
Typing/Region/VSubst.lean

(c)

Term.Subst.Wf.comp
in
Typing/Term/Subst.lean

(d)

Region.Subst.Wf.vsubst
in
Typing/Region/LSubst.lean

∎

Note in particular that this allows us to take the
composition

[\gamma^{\prime}]\gamma:\Gamma^{\prime}\mapsto\Delta
of substitutions
\gamma^{\prime}:\Gamma^{\prime}\mapsto\Gamma

and
\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta
; the composition associates as expected:

[[\gamma_{1}]\gamma_{2}]\gamma_{3}=[\gamma_{1}]([\gamma_{2}]\gamma_{3})
, and has identity
[\mathsf{id}]\gamma=\gamma
, yielding a category of substitutions with variable contexts
\Gamma
as objects.

We will define a “left extension” operation
{\cdot}^{\upharpoonleft}_{\Xi}
yielding

{\gamma}^{\upharpoonleft}_{\Xi}:\Xi,\Gamma\mapsto\Xi,\Delta
which appends the identity substitution
for each variable in
\Xi
in the obvious manner:

(3)

{\gamma}^{\upharpoonleft}_{\cdot}=\gamma\qquad{\gamma}^{\upharpoonleft}_{\Xi,x:{A}^{\epsilon}}=x\mapsto x,{\gamma}^{\upharpoonleft}_{\Xi}

We may similarly define a “right extension” operation
{\cdot}^{\upharpoonright}_{\Xi}
yielding

{\gamma}^{\upharpoonright}_{\Xi}:\Gamma,\Xi\mapsto\Delta,\Xi
as follows:

(4)

{\gamma}^{\upharpoonright}_{\cdot}=\gamma\qquad{\gamma}^{\upharpoonright}_{\Xi,x:{A}^{\epsilon}}={\gamma}^{\upharpoonright}_{\Xi},x\mapsto x

We will usually infer
\Xi
from context; sometimes, for convenience, we may even omit the

{\cdot}^{\upharpoonright},{\cdot}^{\upharpoonleft}
operators entirely, due to the fact that for any
\gamma
, we have

[\gamma]a=[{\Gamma}^{\upharpoonleft}_{\Xi}]a=[{\gamma}^{\upharpoonright}_{\Xi}]a
. The identity substitution on
\Gamma
can
therefore be written as
{\cdot}^{\upharpoonright}_{\Gamma}
. One other particularly important form of
substitution is that of substituting an expression
a
for an individual variable
x
, which we will
write
[a/x]:={(x\mapsto a)}^{\upharpoonleft}
.

Finally, just as we can generalize weakening by substituting expressions for variables via
substitution, we can generalize label weakening by substituting
labels
for

(parametrized) regions
via
label substitution
. In particular, a label-substitution

\sigma\vdash\Gamma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}
maps every label
\ell(A)\in\mathsf{L}
to a region

\Gamma,x:A\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{K}
parametrized by
x:A
. As shown in Figure

13
, we may then define label-substitution recursively in the obvious
manner, mapping
\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;a
to
[a/x]r
as a base case. Composition of label-substitutions is
pointwise. This allows us to state
label substitution
as follows:

Lemma 3.3 (Label substitution).

Given
\Gamma\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}
, we have that

(a)

\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}\implies\Gamma\vdash[\sigma]r\rhd\mathsf{K}

(b)

\Gamma\vdash\kappa:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{J}\implies\Gamma\vdash[\sigma]\kappa:\mathsf{K}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{J}

Proof.

These are formalized as:

(a)

Region.Wf.lsubst
in
Typing/Region/LSubst.lean

(b)

Region.Subst.Wf.comp
in
Typing/Region/LSubst.lean

∎

We may similarly define left and right extensions
\Gamma\vdash{\sigma}^{\upharpoonleft}_{\mathsf{K}}:\mathsf{L},\mathsf{J}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K},\mathsf{J}
and
\Gamma\vdash{\sigma}^{\upharpoonright}_{\mathsf{K}}:\mathsf{L},\mathsf{J}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K},\mathsf{J}
and for label substitutions
\Gamma\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}
in the obvious
manner:

(5)

\displaystyle{\sigma}^{\upharpoonright}_{\cdot}=\sigma\qquad{\sigma}^{\upharpoonright}_{\mathsf{K},\ell(A)}={\sigma}^{\upharpoonright}_{\mathsf{K}},\ell(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;x

(6)

\displaystyle{\sigma}^{\upharpoonleft}_{\cdot}=\sigma\qquad{\sigma}^{\upharpoonleft}_{\mathsf{K},\ell(A)}=\ell(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;x,{\sigma}^{\upharpoonleft}_{\mathsf{K}}

As for variable substitutions, we will often omit
\mathsf{L}
when it is clear from the context, or
even omit the
{\cdot}^{\upharpoonright}
and
{\cdot}^{\upharpoonleft}
operators entirely, due to the fact that for any

\sigma
, we have
[\sigma]r=[{\sigma}^{\upharpoonright}_{\mathsf{K}}]r=[{\sigma}^{\upharpoonleft}_{\mathsf{K}}]r
. We also
define the shorthand
[\ell/\kappa]=[{\kappa(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;x}^{\upharpoonleft}]
.

\displaystyle(\sigma,\ell(x)\mapsto r)(\ell,a)=[a/x]r\qquad(\sigma,\kappa(x)\mapsto r)(\ell,a)=\sigma(\ell,a)\qquad(\cdot)(\ell,a)=\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;a

\displaystyle[\sigma](\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;a)=\sigma(\ell,a)\qquad[\sigma](\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r)=\mathsf{let}\;x=a;[\sigma]r

\displaystyle[\sigma](\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;r)=\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;[\sigma]r

\displaystyle[\sigma](\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:r,\iota_{r}\;{y}:s\})=\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:[\sigma]r,\iota_{r}\;{y}:[\sigma]s\}

\displaystyle[\sigma](r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})=([\sigma]r)\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{[\sigma]t_{i}\},)_{i}

\displaystyle[\sigma](\cdot)=\cdot\qquad[\sigma](\sigma^{\prime},\ell(x)\mapsto r)=([\sigma]\sigma^{\prime},\ell(x)\mapsto[\sigma]r)

Figure 13
.

Capture-avoiding label substititon for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
regions and label substitutions; in
particular, we assume bound variables and labels are
\alpha
-converted so as not to appear in

\sigma
.

4.
Equational Theory

4.1.
Expressions

We can now give an equational theory for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
expressions. In particular,
we will inductively define an equivalence relation

\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{A}

on terms
a,a^{\prime}
for each context
\Gamma
, effect
\epsilon
, and type
A
. For each of the rules
we will present, we assume the rule is valid if and only if
both sides
of the rule are
well-typed. We also assume that variables are
\alpha
-converted as appropriate to avoid shadowing;
our formalization uses de Bruijn indices, but we stick with names in this exposition for simplicity.

The rules for this relation can be roughly split into
rewriting rules
, which denote when two
particular expressions have equivalent semantics, and
congruence rules
, which govern how
rewrites can be composed to enable equational reasoning. In particular, our congruence rules, given
in Figure
14
, consist of:

•

refl
,
symm
,
trans
, which state that

\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\cdot\approx\cdot:{A}
is reflexive, transitive, and symmetric respectively
for each choice of
\Gamma,\epsilon,A
, and therefore an equivalence relation.

•

let
1
,
let
2
,
pair
,
inl
,
inr
,
case
, and

abort
, which state that
\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\cdot\approx\cdot:{A}
is a
congruence

with respect to the corresponding expression constructor, and, in particular, that the expression
constructors are well-defined functions on the quotient of expressions up to
\approx
.

We also include the following
type-directed
rules as part of our congruence relation:

•

initial
, which equates
all
terms in a context containing the empty type

\mathbf{0}
, since we will deem any such context to be
unreachable
by control flow. In
particular, any instruction or function call returning
\mathbf{0}
is assumed to diverge, similarly
to Rust’s “never type” “
!
".

•

terminal
, which equates all
pure
terms of unit type
\mathbf{1}
. Note that

impure
terms may be disequal, since while their result values are the same, their side
effects may differ!

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\mathstrut

refl

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a:{A}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx b:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b\approx c:{A}}\mathstrut

trans

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx c:{A}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx b:{A}}\mathstrut

symm

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b\approx a:{A}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}b\approx b^{\prime}:{B}}\mathstrut

let
1

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;b\approx\mathsf{let}\;x=a^{\prime};\;b^{\prime}:{B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b\approx b^{\prime}:{B}}\mathstrut

pair

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}(a,b)\approx(a^{\prime},b):{A\otimes B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e\approx e^{\prime}:{A\otimes B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}c\approx c^{\prime}:{C}}\mathstrut

let
2

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;\;c\approx\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e^{\prime};\;c^{\prime}:{C}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{A}}\mathstrut

inl

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\iota_{l}\;{a}\approx\iota_{l}\;{a^{\prime}}:{A+B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b\approx b^{\prime}:{B}}\mathstrut

inr

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\iota_{r}\;{b}\approx\iota_{r}\;{b^{\prime}}:{A+B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e\approx e^{\prime}:{A+B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{C}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}b\approx b^{\prime}:{C}}\mathstrut

case

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:a,\iota_{r}\;{y}:b\}\approx\mathsf{case}\;e^{\prime}\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:a^{\prime},\iota_{r}\;{y}:b^{\prime}\}:{C}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{\mathbf{0}}}\mathstrut

abort

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{abort}\;{a}\approx\mathsf{abort}\;{a^{\prime}}:{A}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a^{\prime}:{A}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\exists x,\Gamma\;x=(\mathbf{0},\bot)}\mathstrut

initial

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{A}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{\mathbf{1}}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a^{\prime}:{\mathbf{1}}}\mathstrut

terminal

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{\mathbf{1}}\mathstrut

Figure 14
.
Congruence rules for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
expressions

We may group the rest of our rules according to the relevant constructor, i.e.
\mathsf{let}
(unary and
binary) and
\mathsf{case}
. In particular, for unary
\mathsf{let}
, we have the following rules,
summarized in Figure
15
:

•

let
1
-
\beta
, which allows us to substitute the bound variable in
x
the
let-statement
\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;b
with its definition
a
, yielding
[a/x]b
. Note that we require

\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A}
; i.e.,
a
must be
pure
.

let
1
-
\beta
can be combined with
initial
to derive the more convenient
rule
initial-expr
:

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a^{\prime}:{A}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\exists e,\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}e:{\mathbf{0}}}\mathstrut

initial-expr

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{A}\mathstrut

This states that any context in which a
pure
term of empty type can be constructed equates
all terms. Note in particular that this means divergent instructions must have impure effect!

•

let
1
-
\eta
, which is the standard
\eta
-rule for
\mathsf{let}
. This is included as a
separate rule since, while it follows trivially from
\beta
for pure
a
, we also want to
consider
impure
expressions with some effect
e\neq\bot
.

•

Rules
let
1
-op
,
let
1
-let
1
,
let
1
-let
2
,

let
1
-abort
, and
let
1
-case
which allow us to “pull” a let-statement out of
any of the other expression constructors; operationally, this is saying that the bound expression
we pull out is evaluated before the rest of the
\mathsf{let}
-binding.

For example,
let
1
-case
says that, if both

\mathsf{let}\;z=\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:a,\iota_{r}\;{y}:b\};\;d
and

\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\mathsf{let}\;z=a;\;d,\iota_{r}\;{y}:\mathsf{let}\;z=b;\;d\}{y}
,
are well typed, then both must have the same behaviour:

(1)

Compute
e

(2)

If
e=\iota_{l}\;{e_{l}}
, compute
[e_{l}/x]a
, else, if
e=\iota_{r}\;{e_{r}}
, compute
[e_{r}/y]b
;
store this value as
z

(3)

Compute
d
given our value for
z

Note in particular that, since both sides are well-typed,
d
cannot depend on either
x
or
y
.

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\mathstrut

let
1
-
\beta

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;b\approx[b/x]a:{B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\mathstrut

let
1
-
\eta

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;x\approx a:{A}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{f\in\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}(A,B)}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}c:{C}}\mathstrut

let
1
-op

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;y=f\;a;\;c\approx\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;\mathsf{let}\;y=f\;x;\;c:{C}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}c:{C}}\mathstrut

let
1
-let
1

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;y=(\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;b);\;c\approx\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;\mathsf{let}\;y=b;\;c:{C}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A\times B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A,y:C\vdash_{\epsilon}c:{C}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,z:C\vdash_{\epsilon}d:{D}}\mathstrut

let
1
-let
2

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;z=(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;\;c);\;d\approx\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;\;\mathsf{let}\;z=c;\;d:{D}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{\mathbf{0}}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:A\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\mathstrut

let
1
-abort

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;y=\mathsf{abort}\;{b};\;b\approx\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;\mathsf{let}\;y=\mathsf{abort}\;{x};\;b:{B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A+B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{C}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{C}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,z:C\vdash_{\epsilon}d:{D}}\mathstrut

let
1
-case

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;z=(\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:a,\iota_{r}\;{y}:b\});\;d\mathstrut

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt\approx\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\mathsf{let}\;z=a;\;d,\iota_{r}\;{y}:\mathsf{let}\;z=b;\;d\}:D\mathstrut

Figure 15
.
Rewriting rules for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
unary
\mathsf{let}
expressions

Handling the other type constructors is a little simpler: by providing a “binding” rule, we
generally only need to specify how to interact with
\mathsf{let}_{1}
, as well as an
\eta
and
\beta

rule; interactions with the other constructors can then be derived. For example, consider the rules
for
\mathsf{let}_{2}
given in
16
; we have:

•

let
2
-
\eta
, which is the standard
\eta
-rule for binary
\mathsf{let}
-bindings

•

let
2
-pair
, which acts like a slightly generalized
\beta
-rule, since we can
derive
\beta
reduction as follows: given pure
\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A}
and

\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}b:{B}
, we have

(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=(a,b);\;c)\approx(\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;\mathsf{let}\;y=b;\;c)\approx([a/x](\mathsf{let}\;y=b;\;c))\approx([a/x][b/y]c)

We state the rule in a more general form to allow for impure
a
and
b
, as well as to simplify
certain proofs.

•

let
2
-bind
, which allows us to “pull” out the bound value of a binary

\mathsf{let}
-expression into its own unary
\mathsf{let}
-expression; operationally, this just says that
we execute the bound value before executing the binding itself.

This is enough to allow us to define our interactions with the other expression constructors: for
example, to show that we can lift an operation
f
out of a binary
\mathsf{let}
-binding, rather than
adding a separate rule, we can instead derive (types omitted for simplicity) it from

let
2
-bind
and
let
1
-op
as follows:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=f\;a;\;b)

\displaystyle\approx(\mathsf{let}\;z_{f}=f\;a;\;\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=z;\;b)

\displaystyle\approx(\mathsf{let}\;z_{a}=a;\;\mathsf{let}\;z_{f}=f\;z_{a};\;\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=z;\;b)

\displaystyle\approx(\mathsf{let}\;z_{a}=a;\;\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=f\;z_{a};\;b)

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}c:{C}}\mathstrut

let
2
-pair

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=(a,b);\;c\approx\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;\mathsf{let}\;y=b;\;c:{C}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A\otimes B}}\mathstrut

let
2
-
\eta

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;\;(x,y)\approx e:{A\otimes B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A\otimes B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}c:{C}}\mathstrut

let
2
-bind

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;\;c\approx\mathsf{let}\;z=e;\;\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=z;\;c:{C}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}c:{C}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}d:{C}}\mathstrut

case-inl

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{case}\;\iota_{l}\;{a}\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:c,\iota_{r}\;{y}:d\}\approx\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;c:{C}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}c:{C}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}d:{C}}\mathstrut

case-inr

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{case}\;\iota_{r}\;{b}\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:c,\iota_{r}\;{y}:d\}\approx\mathsf{let}\;y=b;\;d:{C}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A+B}}\mathstrut

case-
\eta

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\iota_{l}\;{x},\iota_{r}\;{y}:\iota_{r}\;{y}\}\approx e:{A+B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A+B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}c:{C}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}d:{C}}\mathstrut

case-bind

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:c,\iota_{r}\;{y}:d\}\approx\mathsf{let}\;z=e;\;\mathsf{case}\;z\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:c,\iota_{r}\;{y}:d\}:{C}\mathstrut

Figure 16
.
Rewriting rules for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
binary
\mathsf{let}
and
\mathsf{case}
expressions

Similarly, it is enough to give
\eta
,
\beta
, and binding rules for
case
expressions.
In particular, we have that

•

case-inl
and
case-inr
serve as
\beta
-reduction rules, telling us that

\mathsf{case}
-expressions given an injection as an argument have the expected operational behaviour.
Note that we reduce to a
\mathsf{let}
-expression rather than perform a substitution to allow for
impure discriminants.

•

case-
\eta
is the standard
\eta
-rule for
\mathsf{case}
-expressions.

•

case-bind
allows us to “pull” out the bound value of the discriminant into
it’s own
\mathsf{let}
-expression; again, operationally, this just says that we need to evaluate
the discriminant before executing the
\mathsf{case}
-expression.

It’s interesting that this is enough, along with the
let-case
rule and friends, to derive the
distributivity properties we would expect well-behaved
\mathsf{case}
-expressions to have. For example,
we have that

\displaystyle f(\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:a,\iota_{r}\;{y}:b\})

\displaystyle\approx(\mathsf{let}\;z=\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:a,\iota_{r}\;{y}:b\};\;f\;z)

\displaystyle\approx\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\mathsf{let}\;z=a;\;f\;z,\iota_{r}\;{y}:\mathsf{let}\;z=b;\;f\;z\}

\displaystyle\approx\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:f\;a,\iota_{r}\;{y}:f\;b\}

and likewise for more complicated distributivity properties involving, e.g.,
\mathsf{let}
-bindings.

The case for other the other constructors is even more convenient: no additional rules are required
at all to handle operations, pairs, and injections. For example, we can derive the expected
bind-rule for operations as follows:

\displaystyle f\;a\approx(\mathsf{let}\;y=f\;a;\;y)\approx(\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;\mathsf{let}\;y=f\;x;\;y)\approx(\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;f\;x)

This completes the equational theory for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
terms; in Section
5.5
, we
will show that this is enough to state a relatively powerful completeness theorem.

4.2.
Regions

We now come to the equational theory for regions, which is similar to that for terms, except that we
also need to support control-flow graphs. As before, we will split our rules into a set of

congruence rules
and, for each region constructor,
rewriting rules
based on that
constructor’s semantics. Our congruence rules, given in Figure
17
, are
quite standard; we have:

•

As for terms,
refl
,
trans
, and
symm
state that

\Gamma\vdash\cdot\approx\cdot\rhd{\mathsf{L}}
is an equivalence relation for all
\Gamma
,
\mathsf{L}
.

•

Similarly,
let
1
,
let
2
,
case
, and
cfg
state that

\Gamma\vdash\cdot\approx\cdot\rhd{\mathsf{L}}
is a congruence over the respective region constructors;

as well as
the equivalence relation on terms
\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\cdot\approx\cdot:{A}
.

•

initial
states that any context containing the empty type
\mathbf{0}
equates all
regions, by a similar reasoning to the rules for terms. Note that we do not require an analogue to
the
terminal
rule (for example, for regions targeting
\mathsf{L}=\ell(\mathbf{1})
),
since it will follow from the version for terms; this is good, since the concept of a “pure”
region has not yet been defined.

Our rewriting rules for unary
\mathsf{let}
-statements, given in Figure
18
, are
analogous to those for unary
\mathsf{let}
-expressions:

•

let
1
-
\beta
allows us to perform
\beta
-reduction of
pure
expressions
into regions; unlike for terms, we do not need an
\eta
-rule

•

Exactly like for
\mathsf{let}
-expressions,
let
1
-op
,
let
1
-let
1
,

let
1
-let
2
,
let
1
-abort
, and
let
1
-case
allow us to pull out nested
subexpressions of the bound value of a
\mathsf{let}
-statement into their own unary
\mathsf{let}
-statement

Similarly to expressions, binary
\mathsf{let}
-statements and
\mathsf{case}
-statements need only the obvious

\beta
rule and binding rule, with all the interactions with other constructors derivable; these
rules are given in Figure
19
. Note in particular that
\eta
-rules are
not necessary, as these are derivable from binding and the
\eta
-rules for expressions.

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

refl

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash r\approx r\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\approx s\rhd{\mathsf{L}}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\vdash s\approx t\rhd{\mathsf{L}}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash r\approx t\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\approx s\rhd{\mathsf{L}}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash s\approx r\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}}\mathstrut

1

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r\approx\mathsf{let}\;x=a^{\prime};r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A,y:B\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;r\approx\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e^{\prime};r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e\approx e^{\prime}:{A+B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:B\vdash s\approx s^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:r,\iota_{r}\;{y}:s\}\approx\mathsf{case}\;e^{\prime}\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:r^{\prime},\iota_{r}\;{y}:s^{\prime}\}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L},(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)_{i}}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}\vdash t_{i}\approx t_{i}^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j}}}\mathstrut

Rewriting rules for

binary
