-statements

Dealing with
\mathsf{where}
-blocks, on the other hand, is a little bit more complicated, as shown by the
number of rules in Figure
20
. One difficulty is that, unlike the other region
constructors, we will need an
\eta
-rule as well as
two

\beta
-rules. The latter are simple
enough to state:

•

For
\ell_{k}
defined in a
\mathsf{where}
-block,
cfg-
\beta_{1}
says that we can replace a
branch to
\ell_{k}
with argument
a
with a
\mathsf{let}
-statement binding
a
to the corresponding
body
t_{k}
’s argument
x_{k}
.

•

For
\kappa

not
defined in a
\mathsf{where}
-block,
cfg-
\beta_{2}
says that
a branch to
\kappa
within the
\mathsf{where}
-block has the same semantics as if the
\mathsf{where}
-block
was not there; hence, it can be removed.

To state our
\eta
-rule, however, we will need to introduce some more machinery. Given a mapping
from a set of labels
\ell_{i}
to associated regions
t_{i}
, we may define the
control-flow
graph substitution

\mathsf{cfgs}\;\{(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\}
pointwise as follows:

(7)

\mathsf{cfgs}\;\{(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\}\;\kappa\;a:=(\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;a\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})

In general, we may derive, for any label-context
\mathsf{L}
(assuming
\mathsf{cfgs}\;\{\cdot\}
acts uniformly
on the labels
\kappa
in
\mathsf{L}
as described above), the following rule:

(8)

\displaystyle{\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}\vdash t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j}}\mathstrut

cfgs

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{cfgs}\;\{(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\}:\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

Our
\eta
-rule,
cfg-
\eta
, says that any
\mathsf{where}
-block of the form

r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}
has the same semantics as the label-substitution

[\mathsf{cfgs}\;\{(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\}]r
, which in effect propagates the where-block to the
branches of
r
, if any. While we called this rule
cfg-
\eta
, it also functions similarly
to a binding rule in that it allows us to derive many of the expected commutativity properties of

\mathsf{where}
; for example, we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}\;y=a;\;r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{\mathsf{br}\;\ell_{j}\;a_{j}\},)_{i}

\displaystyle\approx[\mathsf{cfgs}\;\{(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{\mathsf{br}\;\ell_{j}\;a_{j}\},)_{i}\}](\mathsf{let}\;y=a;\;r)

\displaystyle\approx\mathsf{let}\;y=a;\;[\mathsf{cfgs}\;\{(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{\mathsf{br}\;\ell_{j}\;a_{j}\},)_{i}\}]r

\displaystyle\approx\mathsf{let}\;y=a;\;r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{\mathsf{br}\;\ell_{j}\;a_{j}\},)_{i}

One particularly important application of the
\eta
-rule for control-flow graphs is in validating
the rewrite

(9)

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A+B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash s\rhd\mathsf{L}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:B\vdash t\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

case2cfg

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{case}\;a\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:s,\iota_{r}\;{y}:t\}\approx(\mathsf{case}\;a\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;x,\iota_{r}\;{y}:\mathsf{br}\;\ell^{\prime}\;y\})\mathstrut

\displaystyle\hskip 160.00024pt\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{s\},\ell^{\prime}(y):\{t\}\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

However, on it’s own, it’s not quite enough to get rid of nested
\mathsf{where}
-blocks. To enable this,
it turns out that we only need to add as an axiom the ability to get rid of a single, trivially
nested
\mathsf{where}
-block; this is given as the rule
codiag
.

To be able to soundly perform equational rewriting, we will need the
uniformity
property,
which is described by the rule
uni
. In essence, this lets us commute pure expressions with
loop bodies, enabling rewrites like

(10)

\mathsf{loop}\;\{x=x+1;\mathsf{if}\;p\;3x\;\{\mathsf{ret}\;3x\}\}\qquad\approx\qquad y=3x;\mathsf{loop}\;\{y=y+3;\mathsf{if}\;p\;y\;\{\mathsf{ret}\;y\}\}

Note that substitution alone would not allow us to derive Equation
10
above,
since
x
and
y
change each iteration, and hence, in SSA, would need to become parameters as
follows:

(11)

\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;x\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{\mathsf{let}\;x^{\prime}=x+1;\;\mathsf{if}\;p\;3x^{\prime}\;\{\mathsf{ret}\;3x^{\prime}\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;x^{\prime}\}\}\\
\approx\mathsf{let}\;y=3x;\;\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;y\;\mathsf{where}\;\kappa(y):\{\mathsf{let}\;y^{\prime}=y+3;\;\mathsf{if}\;p\;y^{\prime}\;\{\mathsf{ret}\;y^{\prime}\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;y^{\prime}\}\}

The actual rule is quite complicated, so let’s break it down point by point. Assume we are given:

•

A region
\Gamma,y:B\vdash s\rhd\mathsf{L},\kappa(B)
taking “input”
y
of type

B
and, as “output,” jumping to a label
\kappa
with an argument of type
B
. We’ll
interpret branches to any other label as a (divergent) “side effect.”

•

A region
\Gamma,x:A\vdash t\rhd\mathsf{L},\ell(A)
taking “input”
x
of type

A
and, as “output,” jumping to a label
\ell
with an argument of type
A
.

•

A
pure
expression
\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\bot}e:{B}
parameterised by a value

x
of type
A

Suppose further that the following condition holds:

\Gamma,x:A\vdash[e/y]s\approx t\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;e\}\rhd{\mathsf{L},\kappa(B)}

That is, the following two programs are equivalent:

(a)

Given input
x
, evaluate
e
and, taking it’s output to be input
y
, evaluate
s
,
(implicitly) yielding as output a new value of
y
. In imperative pseudocode,

y=e;x=s

(b)

Given input
x
, evaluate
t
and, taking it’s output to be the
new
value of
x
,
evaluate
e
, (implicitly) yielding as output a new value
y
. In imperative pseudocode,

x=t;y=e

Then
, for any well-typed entry block
\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},\ell(A)
(which can produce
an appropriate input
x:A
at label
\ell
), we have that

\Gamma\vdash(r\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;e\})\;\mathsf{where}\;\kappa(y):\{s\}\approx r\;\mathsf{where}\;t\rhd{\mathsf{L}}

i.e., in imperative pseudocode,

(12)

\displaystyle x=r;y=e;\mathsf{loop}\;\{y=s\}

\displaystyle\approx x=r;\mathsf{loop}\;\{x=t\}

where
s
and
t
may branch out of the loop. The reason why we require
e
to be
pure
is
that impure expressions do not necessarily commute with infinite loops, even if they commute
with any finite number of iterations of the loop. For example, if
\mathsf{hi}
is some effectful
operation (say, printing “hello”), it is quite obvious that,

(13)

\displaystyle\mathsf{hi};x=x+1;\mathsf{if}\;x=y\;\{\mathsf{ret}\;y\}

\displaystyle\approx x=x+1;\mathsf{if}\;x=y\;\{\mathsf{hi};\mathsf{ret}\;y\};\mathsf{hi}

whereas

(14)

\displaystyle\mathsf{hi};\mathsf{loop}\{x=x+1;\mathsf{if}\;x=y\;\{\mathsf{ret}\;y\}\}

\displaystyle\not\approx\mathsf{loop}\{x=x+1;\mathsf{if}\;x=y\;\{\mathsf{hi};\mathsf{ret}\;y\}\};

since, in particular, we may have
y\leq x
, in which case the loop will never exit and hence

\mathsf{hi}
will never be executed.
Note that, due to
let
1
-
\beta
,
cfg-
\eta
, and
cfg-
\beta_{1}
, this is
equivalent to

(15)

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},\ell(A)}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash[e/y]s\approx[\ell(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;e]t\rhd{\mathsf{L},\kappa(B)}}\mathstrut

uni’

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(([\ell(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;e]r)\;\mathsf{where}\;\kappa(y):\{s\})\approx(r\;\mathsf{where}\;t)\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A_{k}}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}\vdash t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j}}\mathstrut

cfg-
\beta_{1}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{br}\;\ell_{k}\;a\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\approx(\mathsf{let}\;x_{k}=a;t_{k})\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}b:{B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{{\hskip 8.00003pt{\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}\vdash t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\mathsf{L}\;\kappa=B}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\kappa\notin\{(\ell_{i},)_{i}\}}\mathstrut

cfg-
\beta_{2}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;b\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\approx\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;b\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)_{i}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}\vdash t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j}}\mathstrut

cfg-
\eta

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\approx[\mathsf{cfgs}\;\{(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\}]r\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},\ell(A)}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:A\vdash s\rhd\mathsf{L},\ell(A),\kappa(A)}\mathstrut

codiag

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash r\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;x\;\mathsf{where}\;\kappa(y):\{s\}\}\approx r\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(y):\{[\ell/\kappa]s\}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},\ell(A)}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash\mathsf{let}\;y=e;\;s\approx t\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;e\}\rhd{\mathsf{L},\kappa(B)}}\mathstrut

uni

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(r\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;e\})\;\mathsf{where}\;\kappa(y):\{s\}\approx r\;\mathsf{where}\;t\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\text{where}\qquad{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\bot}e:{B}},\quad{\Gamma,y:B\vdash s\rhd\mathsf{L},\kappa(B)},\quad\text{and}\quad{\Gamma,x:A\vdash t\rhd\mathsf{L},\ell(A)}

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)_{i}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\vdash\sigma:(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)_{i}\rightsquigarrow(\kappa_{j}(B_{j}),)_{j}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}:B_{i}\vdash t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j}}\mathstrut

dinat

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash([{\sigma}^{\upharpoonleft}]r)\;\mathsf{where}\;(\kappa_{i}(x_{i}):\{[{\sigma}^{\upharpoonleft}]t_{i}\},)_{i}\approx r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{[{(\kappa_{j}(x_{j})\mapsto t_{j},)}^{\upharpoonleft}](\sigma_{i}\;x_{i})\},)_{i}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

Figure 20
.
Rewriting rules for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}

\mathsf{where}
-blocks

This format illuminates a very important potential use for uniformity; namely, formalizing rewrites
like those in Figure
7
. In particular, consider a program of the form

\Gamma\vdash([\ell(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;e]r)\;\mathsf{where}\;\kappa(y):\{[\ell(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;e]s\}\rhd\mathsf{L}

where
\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},\ell(A)
,
\Gamma,y:B\vdash s\rhd\mathsf{L},\ell(A)
, and

\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\bot}e:{B}
is pure. Then we have that

[e/y][\ell(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;(e)]s\\
\approx[\ell(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;[e/y](e)][e/y]s\\
\approx[\ell(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;(e)][e/y]s

and therefore that

\Gamma\vdash([\ell(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;e]r)\;\mathsf{where}\;\kappa(z):\{[\ell(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;e]s\}\\
\approx r\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{[e/y]s\}\\
\approx r\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{\mathsf{let}\;y=e;s\}

In particular, for example, we can then easily derive the rewrite from Figure
7(b)

to Figure
7(c)
by noting the
equalities
(an equivalence would be enough, of
course)

\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=(i_{0},a_{0});\;(x+1,y*x+1))\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{\mathsf{ret}(a_{0})\}\\
=[\mathsf{loop}(i_{0},a_{0})\mapsto\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=(i_{0},a_{0});\;(x+1,y*x+1)](\mathsf{if}\;i_{0}<n\;\{\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(i_{0},a_{0})\}\;\mathsf{else}\;\{\mathsf{ret}(a_{0})\})

and

\mathsf{let}\;n=10;\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=(0,1);(x+1,y*(x+1)))\\
=[\mathsf{loop}(i_{0},a_{0})\mapsto\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=(i_{0},a_{0});\;(x+1,y*x+1)](\mathsf{let}\;n=10;\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{loop}(0,1))

Rewrites like this are an instance of the principle we call
dinaturality
, which, for
structured control-flow, can be best expressed as an equivalence between the control-flow graphs
in Figure
21
. Unlike in the case of uniformity, however, this is true even
when the program fragment
P
is
impure
, since we do not commute
P
over an infinite number
of iterations; hence, we cannot simply use uniformity to derive this rewrite rule in its most
general form. We instead postulate our final rewriting rule,
dinat
, which generalises the
above rewrite from sequential composition on a structured control-flow graph to label substitution
on an arbitrary control-flow graph.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quad Q\quad}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
=
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quad Q\quad}

Figure 21
.

Dinaturality on a structured loop

It turns out that this being able to do this is essential, as it allows us to relate unary and

n
-ary control-flow graphs and, in particular, use this relationship to interconvert between
data-flow and control-flow. This means we now have enough machinery to justify the elimination of
arbitrary nested
\mathsf{where}
-blocks, and hence to justify the rule

(16)

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)_{i},(\kappa_{j}(B_{j}),)_{j}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}\vdash t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\forall i.\Gamma,y_{i}:B_{i}\vdash s_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j},(\kappa_{k}(B_{k}),)_{k}\mathstrut

cfg-fuse

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\kappa_{i}(y_{i}):\{s_{i}\}),)_{i}\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{j}(x_{j}):\{t_{j}\}),)_{j}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\hskip 80.00012pt\approx r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\kappa_{i}(y_{i}):\{s_{i}\},)_{i},(\ell_{j}(x_{j}):\{t_{j}\}),)_{j}\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

Also very convenient is the variant

(17)

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)_{i},\kappa(B)}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}\vdash t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j},\kappa(B)\mathstrut

\displaystyle\Gamma,y:B\vdash s\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j},\kappa(B),(\ell_{j}^{\prime}(A_{j}^{\prime}),)_{j},\mathstrut

\displaystyle\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}^{\prime}:A_{i}^{\prime}\vdash t_{i}^{\prime}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j},\kappa(B),(\ell_{j}^{\prime}(A_{j}^{\prime}),)_{j}\mathstrut

cfg-fuse
2

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i},\kappa(y):\{s\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime}):\{t_{i}^{\prime}\},)_{i}\}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\hskip 80.00012pt\approx r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i},\kappa(y):\{s\},(\ell_{i}^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime}):\{t_{i}^{\prime}\},)_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

Rather than justify these rules directly, it turns out to be much more convenient to do so using our
denotational semantics, which, due to our completeness result in Section
5.5
, is
sound. A proof can be found in Lemma
B.7
in the appendix.

There are some other basic rules we may want to use which turn out to be
derivable from our existing set. For example, while re-ordering labels in a
\mathsf{where}
-block looks
like a no-op in our named syntax, to rigorously justify the following rule actually requires
dinaturality (with the permutation done via a label-substitution):

(18)

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)_{i}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}\vdash t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\sigma\;\text{permutation}}\mathstrut

perm-cfg

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\approx r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{\sigma_{i}}(x_{\sigma_{i}}):\{t_{\sigma_{i}}\},)_{i}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

Note the implicit use of the fact that if some region
r
typechecks in some label-context
\mathsf{L}
,
then it typechecks in any permutation of
\mathsf{L}
, which is again proven by label-substitution.

4.3.
Metatheory

We can now begin to investigate the metatheoretic properties of our equational theory. As a first
sanity check, we can verify that weakening, label-weakening, and loosening of effects all respect
our equivalence relation, as stated in the following lemma:

Lemma 4.1 (Weakening (Rewriting)).

Given
\Gamma\leq\Delta
,
\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{K}
, and
\epsilon\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
, we have that

(a)

\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{A}\implies\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon^{\prime}}a\approx a^{\prime}:{A}

(b)

\Delta\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\implies\Gamma\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}^{\prime}}

Proof.

These are formalized as:

(a)

Term.InS.wk_congr
and
Term.InS.wk_eff_congr
in

Rewrite/Term/Setoid.lean

(b)

Region.InS.vwk_congr
and
Region.InS.lwk_congr
in

Rewrite/Region/Setoid.lean

∎

In particular, note that this lemma uses an equivalence relation on substitutions and
label-substitutions: this is just the obvious pointwise extension of the equivalence relation on
terms and regions respectively. We give the rules for this relation in
Figure
22
in the interests of explicitness. It is straightforward to verify
that these are indeed equivalence relations. In fact, it turns out that substitution and
label-substitution both respect these equivalences, in the following precise sense:

Lemma 4.2 (Congruence (Substitution)).

Given
\gamma\approx\gamma^{\prime}:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta
, we have that

(a)

\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{A}\implies\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}[\gamma]a\approx[\gamma^{\prime}]a^{\prime}:{A}

(b)

\Delta\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\implies\Gamma\vdash[\gamma]r\approx[\gamma^{\prime}]r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}

(c)

\rho\approx\rho^{\prime}:\Delta\mapsto\Xi\implies[\gamma]\rho\approx[\gamma^{\prime}]\rho^{\prime}:\Gamma\mapsto\Xi

(d)

\sigma\approx\sigma^{\prime}\vdash\Delta:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}\implies[\gamma]\sigma\approx[\gamma^{\prime}]\sigma^{\prime}\vdash\Gamma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}

Proof.

These are formalized as:

(a)

Term.InS.subst_congr
in
Rewrite/Term/Setoid.lean

(b)

Region.InS.vsubst_congr
in
Rewrite/Region/Setoid.lean

(c)

Term.Subst.InS.comp_congr
in
Rewrite/Term/Setoid.lean

(d)

Region.Subst.InS.vsubst_congr
in
Rewrite/Region/LSubst.lean

∎

Lemma 4.3 (Congruence (Label Substitution)).

Given
\sigma\approx\sigma^{\prime}\vdash\Gamma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}
, we have that

(a)

\Gamma\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\implies\Gamma\vdash[\sigma]r\approx[\sigma^{\prime}]r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{K}}

(b)

\kappa\approx\kappa^{\prime}\vdash\Gamma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{J}\implies[\sigma]\kappa\approx[\sigma^{\prime}]\kappa^{\prime}\vdash\Gamma:\mathsf{K}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{J}

Proof.

These are formalized as:

(a)

Region.InS.lsubst_congr
in
Rewrite/Region/LSubst.lean

(b)

Region.LSubst.InS.comp_congr
in
Rewrite/Region/LSubst.lean

∎

This means, in particular, that, substitution and label-substitution are well-defined operators on
equivalence classes of terms, which will come in handy later as we set out to prove completeness
in Section
5.5
.

sb-nil

\displaystyle\cdot\approx\cdot:\Gamma\mapsto\cdot\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\gamma\approx\gamma^{\prime}:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta}\mathstrut

sb-id

\displaystyle\gamma\approx\gamma^{\prime}:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\sigma\approx\sigma^{\prime}\vdash\Gamma:\mathsf{L},\ell(A)\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K},\ell(A)}\mathstrut

ls-id

\displaystyle\sigma\approx\sigma^{\prime}\vdash\Gamma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}\mathstrut

.
Rules for the equivalence relation on
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
substitutions and label-substitutions

4.4.
Strict SSA

The relaxation of SSA to
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
allows us to state our equational theory and handle
substitution more conveniently. However, this approach may lead readers to question whether we have
truly provided a type-theoretic presentation of SSA as it is used in practice. To address this
concern, we introduce a subset of
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
regions called
strict regions
. We make the
following claims about these strict regions:

(1)

Every
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
region can be converted to an equivalent strict region

(2)

Every strict region can be erased to a well-formed SSA program by removing
“
\mathsf{where}
”

(3)

Every well-formed SSA program can be typed as an strict region purely by adding
“
\mathsf{where}
”

(4)

Two strict regions which erase to the same SSA program are equivalent

We assert that strict regions correspond exactly to SSA, with the
\mathsf{where}
-bracketing serving as
syntactic sugar to make our typing rules syntax-directed. Specifically,
\mathsf{where}
-blocks can be
added algorithmically by computing dominance relationships between basic blocks.

Converting directly to strict SSA can be unwieldy. Therefore, we do
this in two stages, first converting
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
into to a subset
corresponding to A-normal form (ANF) extended with mutually recursive

\mathsf{where}
-bindings, and then converting the ANF regions into strict
regions.

4.4.1.
From
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
to A-Normal Form

Our first step is to extend the work of
Chakravarty et al. (2003)
, by providing an
algorithm for converting between
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
and ANF in an equivalence preserving way. We begin by
defining a typing judgement
\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}^{\mathsf{anf}}a:{A}
for
atomic expressions
in
Figure
23
. An atomic expression is either a single variable, a function application,
an injection, a pair of variables, or the unit expression. We specifically forbid

\mathsf{case}
-expressions and
\mathsf{let}
-bindings at this level; they are required to appear only at the
region level. It is obvious that
\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}^{\mathsf{anf}}a:{A}\implies\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}
.

We now present typing rules for ANF regions in Figure
24
. These rules mirror those
for regions but with additional constraints: each
\mathsf{let}
-binding in a region must bind an atomic
expression, and case-statements and branches must operate on variables. This ensures that each
binding corresponds to a single SSA instruction. It is similarly obvious that

\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}
. We can now define a syntactic function to convert an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}

region to ANF inductively as follows:

(19)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}(\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;a)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,a,\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;x)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}(\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,a,\mathsf{ANF}(r))

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;\;r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(z,a,\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=z;\mathsf{ANF}(r))

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}(\mathsf{case}\;a\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:r,\iota_{r}\;{y}:s\})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(z,a,\mathsf{case}\;z\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\mathsf{ANF}(r),\iota_{r}\;{y}:\mathsf{ANF}(s)\})

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}(r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ANF}(r)\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{\mathsf{ANF}(t_{i})\},)_{i}

where we define
\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,a,r)
by induction on expressions
a
as follows

(20)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,a,r)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r)\hskip 80.00012pt\text{if}\;a\;\text{atomic}

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,f\;e,r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(y,e,\mathsf{let}\;x=f\;y;r)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,(\mathsf{let}\;y=e;\;a),r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(y,e,\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,a,r))

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,(e_{1},e_{2}),r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(y_{1},e_{1},\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(y_{2},e_{2},\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,(y_{1},y_{2}),r)))

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,(\mathsf{let}\;(y,z)=e;\;a),r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(w,e,(\mathsf{let}\;(y,z)=w;\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,a,r)))

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,\iota_{l}\;{e},r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(y,e,(\mathsf{let}\;x=\iota_{l}\;{y};r))

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,\iota_{r}\;{e},r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(y,e,(\mathsf{let}\;x=\iota_{r}\;{y};r))

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{y}:a,\iota_{r}\;{z}:b\},r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(w,e,

\displaystyle\mathsf{case}\;w\;\{\iota_{l}\;{y}:\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,a,r),\iota_{r}\;{z}:\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,b,r)\})

\displaystyle\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,\mathsf{abort}\;{e},r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(y,e,(\mathsf{let}\;x=\mathsf{abort}\;{y};r))

We state the correctness of these functions in the following lemma:

Lemma 4.4 (name=A-normalization, restate=anfconversion).

For all
\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}
, we have that that
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}\mathsf{ANF}(r)\rhd\mathsf{L}
and

\Gamma\vdash r\approx\mathsf{ANF}(r)\rhd{\mathsf{L}}
. Furthermore, given
\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}
, we
have that

•

\Gamma,x:A\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}\implies\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r\approx\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,a,r)\rhd{\mathsf{L}}

•

\Gamma,x:A\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}\implies\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}\mathsf{ANF}_{\mathsf{let}}(x,a,r)\rhd\mathsf{L}

Proof.

See Appendix
B.1

∎

\boxed{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}^{\mathsf{anf}}a:{A}}

\displaystyle{\Gamma\;x\leq(A,\epsilon)}\mathstrut

var

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}^{\mathsf{anf}}x:{A}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{f\in\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}(A,B)}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\;x\leq(A,\epsilon)}\mathstrut

op

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}^{\mathsf{anf}}f\;x:{B}\mathstrut

unit

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}^{\mathsf{anf}}():{\mathbf{1}}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\;x\leq(A,\epsilon)}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma\;y\leq(B,\epsilon)}\mathstrut

pair

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}(x,y):{A\otimes B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\;x\leq(A,\epsilon)}\mathstrut

inl

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\iota_{l}\;{x}:{A+B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\;y\leq(B,\epsilon)}\mathstrut

inr

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\iota_{r}\;{y}:{A+B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\;x\leq(\mathbf{0},\epsilon)}\mathstrut

abort

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{abort}\;{x}:{A}\mathstrut

Figure 23
.
Typing rules for atomic expressions

\boxed{\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}}

\displaystyle{\Gamma\;x\leq(A,\epsilon)}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\mathsf{L}\;\ell=A}\mathstrut

br

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;x\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}^{\mathsf{anf}}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

let
1
-r

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}^{\mathsf{anf}}e:{A\otimes B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A,y:B\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

let
2
-r

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;r\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\;x\leq(A+B,\epsilon)}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:A\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,z:B\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}s\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

case-r

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}\mathsf{case}\;x\;\{\iota_{l}\;{y}:r,\iota_{r}\;{z}:s\}\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}r\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)_{i}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j}}\mathstrut

cfg

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

.
Typing rules for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
ANF regions

4.4.2.
From ANF to SSA

We now wish to give typing rules for strict regions. Since SSA consists of
basic blocks
, that is,
linear sequences of instructions followed by a terminator, we will introduce two mutually
recursive judgements:

•

\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{t}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}
, which states that
r
is a terminator, which we model
as a tree of case-statements with unconditional branches at the leaves

•

\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}
, which states that
r
is a basic block
plus
the blocks
which are its children in the dominator tree. The basic block itself is a linear
sequence of unary and binary let-bindings followed by a terminator, which is wrapped in a where-block
containing the child blocks.

A strict region, then, is a basic block, with the block itself (made up of its component
let-bindings followed by its terminator) the distinguished
entry block
. We give typing rules
for these judgements in Figure
25
. Note in particular that we require every block
to have an associated
\mathsf{where}
-block of dominated blocks; if there are no blocks dominated, then
we simply use the empty
\mathsf{where}
-block. This hints at the natural inductive representation

⬇

1

struct

BasicBlock

{

2

instructions

:

List

LetBinding
,

3

terminator

:

Terminator
,

4

children

:

List

BasicBlock

5

}

In particular, given a strict region
r
, we can define the following functions to extract the
region’s entry block and a list of its children as follows

(21)

\displaystyle\mathsf{entry}(\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\mathsf{entry}(r))

\displaystyle\mathsf{entry}(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;r)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;\mathsf{entry}(r))

\displaystyle\mathsf{entry}(r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})

\displaystyle=r

(22)

\displaystyle\mathsf{children}(\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{children}(r)

\displaystyle\mathsf{children}(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{children}(r)

\displaystyle\mathsf{children}(r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})

\displaystyle=[\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{\mathsf{children}(t_{i})\},]_{i}

We can similarly define a function to construct a strict region from an entry block and a list
of children as follows:

(23)

\displaystyle\mathsf{bb}(\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r,G)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\mathsf{bb}(r,G)

\displaystyle\mathsf{bb}(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;r,G)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;\mathsf{bb}(r,G)

\displaystyle\mathsf{bb}(r,(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})

\displaystyle=r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}

It is easy to see that these functions are mutually inverse: for any strict region
r
, we have

(24)

r=\mathsf{bb}(\mathsf{entry}(r),\mathsf{children}(r))

Some other useful facts about
\mathsf{bb}(\cdot,\cdot)
include:

•

It is a congruence: if
r\approx r^{\prime}
and each
t_{i}\approx t_{i}^{\prime}
,

\mathsf{bb}(r,(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})\approx\mathsf{bb}(r^{\prime},(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}^{\prime}\},)_{i})

•

It is invariant up to permutations

\sigma
:
\mathsf{bb}(r,(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})\approx\mathsf{bb}(r,(\ell_{\sigma_{i}}(x_{\sigma_{i}}):\{t_{\sigma_{i}}\},)_{i})

•

Similarly,
if both sides of the equation are well-typed
, i.e.,

–

All
t_{i}
do not use
y
or any of the variables defined in
s

–

All branches to
\ell_{i}
come from
\kappa
or
\ell_{j}
(and not from either
r
or
G
)

(25)

\mathsf{bb}(r,(G,\kappa(y):\{s\},(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)))\approx\mathsf{bb}(r,(G,\kappa(y):\{s\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)\}))

and hence

(26)

\mathsf{bb}(r,(G,\kappa(y):\{s\},(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)))\approx\mathsf{bb}(r,(G,\kappa(y):\{\mathsf{bb}(s,(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},))\}))

In particular, we may apply this rule twice to obtain

(27)

\mathsf{bb}(r,(G,\kappa(y):\{\mathsf{bb}(s,G^{\prime})\},(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)))\approx\mathsf{bb}(r,(G,\kappa(y):\{\mathsf{bb}(s,G^{\prime},(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},))\}))

Now that we have defined strict regions, we can give a function
\mathsf{SSA}(r)
to convert a region
r

to a strict SSA as follows:

(28)

\displaystyle\mathsf{SSA}(r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{SSA}_{\mathsf{a}}(\mathsf{ANF}(r),\cdot)

\displaystyle\mathsf{SSA}_{\mathsf{a}}(t,(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})

\displaystyle=t\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\qquad\text{where}\;t\;\text{is a terminator}

\displaystyle\mathsf{SSA}_{\mathsf{a}}((\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r),G)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\mathsf{SSA}_{\mathsf{a}}(r,G))

\displaystyle\mathsf{SSA}_{\mathsf{a}}((\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;r),G)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;\mathsf{SSA}_{\mathsf{a}}(r,G))

\displaystyle\mathsf{SSA}_{\mathsf{a}}((\mathsf{case}\;a\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:s,\iota_{r}\;{y}:t\}),G)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{case}\;a\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\mathsf{br}\;\ell_{l}\;x,\iota_{r}\;{y}:\mathsf{br}\;\ell_{r}\;y\})

\displaystyle\mathsf{where}\;G,\ell_{l}(x):\{\mathsf{SSA}(s)\},\ell_{r}(y):\{\mathsf{SSA}(t)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{SSA}_{\mathsf{a}}((r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}),G)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{SSA}_{\mathsf{a}}(r,(G,(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{\mathsf{SSA}(t_{i})\},)_{i}))

We may state its correctness as follows:

Lemma 4.5 (name=SSA conversion, restate=ssaconversion).

If
\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}
, then
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}\mathsf{SSA}(r)\rhd\mathsf{L}
and

\Gamma\vdash r\approx\mathsf{SSA}(r)\rhd{\mathsf{L}}
. In particular, given
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{anf}}r\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)_{i}
and
\forall i,\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)_{i}
, we have that

\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}\mathsf{SSA}_{\mathsf{a}}(r,(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})\rhd\mathsf{L}
and
\Gamma\vdash(r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})\approx\mathsf{SSA}_{\mathsf{a}}(r,(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})\rhd{\mathsf{L}}

Proof.

See Appendix
B.1

∎

\boxed{\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{t}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}}

\displaystyle{\Gamma\;x\leq(A,\epsilon)}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\mathsf{L}\;\ell=A}\mathstrut

br

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{t}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;x\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\;x\leq(A+B,\epsilon)}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:A\vdash^{\mathsf{t}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,z:B\vdash^{\mathsf{t}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}s\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

case-t

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{t}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}\mathsf{case}\;x\;\{\iota_{l}\;{y}:r,\iota_{r}\;{z}:s\}\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\boxed{\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}}

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{t}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}r\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)_{i}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}\vdash^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j}}\mathstrut

cfg

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}^{\mathsf{anf}}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

let
1
-c

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}^{\mathsf{anf}}e:{A\otimes B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A,y:B\vdash^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

let
2
-c

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{ssa}}\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;r\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

Figure 25
.
Typing rules for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
terminators and strict regions

We now come to the final part of our argument: that strict regions are equivalent to standard SSA.
We begin by giving a grammar for standard SSA in Figure
26
. It is easy to see
that removing
\mathsf{where}
-blocks from a strict region, gives us a function
\mathsf{cfg}(\cdot)
from
strict regions
r
to control-flow graphs
G
defined as follows:

(29)

\mathsf{cfg}(r)=\mathsf{entry}(r),(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{\mathsf{cfg}(t_{i})\},)_{(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\})\in\mathsf{children}(r)}

where

(30)

G,\ell(x):\{\beta,G^{\prime}\}:=G,\ell(x):\{\beta\},G^{\prime}

An SSA program is
well-formed
if:

•

All variable uses are well-typed

•

All variable uses respect dominance-based scoping

It is easy to see that any erased program is well-formed, since our typing rules guarantee every
expression is well-typed, while our lexical scoping for values ensures that variables are only
visible in the children of the block
\beta
in which they are defined, which the lexical scoping of
labels guarantees are dominated by
\beta
. On the other hand, we may give an algorithm to convert
any well-formed SSA program
G
into a well-typed strict region
r=\mathsf{reg}(G)
as follows:

(1)

Compute the dominance tree of
G
, rooted at its entry block
\beta
.

(2)

For each child
\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{\beta_{i}\}
of
\beta
, let
G_{i}
denote the CFG
composed of the descendants of
\beta_{i}
(with
\beta_{i}
as entry block), given in the order they
appear in
G
. Recursively compute
r_{i}=\mathsf{reg}(G_{i})
.

(3)

Return the program
\mathsf{bb}(\beta,(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{r_{i}\},)_{i})

We will write
G\simeq G^{\prime}
to mean “
G
is a permutation of
G^{\prime}
” (in particular,
G
and
G^{\prime}

must have the same entry block!). It is easy to see that this algorithm gives a strict SSA region
which erases to a permutation of
G
, i.e.,
\mathsf{cfg}(\mathsf{reg}(G))\simeq G
, as desired. To complete
our argument, it hence suffices to show that, given strict regions
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}
,

\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}r^{\prime}\rhd\mathsf{L}
, such that
\mathsf{cfg}(r)\simeq\mathsf{cfg}(r^{\prime})
, we have that

\Gamma\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}
. We break this down into two lemmas:

Lemma 4.6 (name=Permutation Invariance, restate=cfgperminvar).

If
G\simeq G^{\prime}
and
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{reg}(G)\rhd\mathsf{L}
, then

\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{reg}(G^{\prime})\rhd\mathsf{L}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{reg}(G)\approx\mathsf{reg}(G^{\prime})\rhd{\mathsf{L}}

Proof.

See Appendix
B.1

∎

Lemma 4.7 (name=CFG Conversion, restate=cfgconversion).

If
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}
, then
\Gamma\vdash r\approx\mathsf{reg}(\mathsf{cfg}(r))\rhd{\mathsf{L}}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
B.1

∎

We may now conclude that, given
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}
and
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}r^{\prime}\rhd\mathsf{L}
, if

\mathsf{cfg}(r)\simeq\mathsf{cfg}(r^{\prime})
, we have that
\Gamma\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}
, since in particular

(31)

r\approx\mathsf{reg}(\mathsf{cfg}(r))\approx\mathsf{reg}(\mathsf{cfg}(r^{\prime}))\approx r^{\prime}

<
a,b,c,e
> ::=
x

|
f\;x

|
()

|
(x,y)

|
\iota_{l}\;{x}

|
\iota_{r}\;{y}

|
\mathsf{abort}\;{x}

<
\tau
> ::=
\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;x

|
\mathsf{case}\;x\;\{\iota_{l}\;{y}:\tau,\iota_{r}\;{z}:\tau^{\prime}\}

<
\beta
> :=
\tau

|
\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\beta

|
\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;\beta

<
G
> ::=
\beta
|
G;\ell(x):\{\beta\}

Figure 26
.
Grammar for standard SSA, parametrized over an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
signature. A standard SSA
program is made up of
basic blocks

\beta
, and can be thought of as a

control-flow graph

G
(with a distinguished entry block
\beta
).

4.5.
Records and Enums

For simplicity, we defined our language to have only binary products and sums. However, we would
like to support records (named
n
-ary products) and enums (named
n
-ary sums). We will implement
these on top of our language using contexts; the resulting machinery will turn out to be crucial in
proving the Böhm-Jacopini theorem in Section
4.6
and completeness in
Section
5.5
. We begin by defining
packing
of variable contexts as
follows:

(32)

\langle\cdot\rangle=\mathbf{1}\qquad\langle\Gamma,x:{A}^{\epsilon}\rangle=\langle\Gamma\rangle\otimes A

While in general we destructure pairs using binary
\mathsf{let}
-bindings, it will be more convenient
to start defining operations on records in terms of projections. We begin by defining projections
on pairs in the obvious manner:

(33)

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A\otimes B}}\mathstrut

\pi_{l}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\pi_{l}\;e:=(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;\;x):{A}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A\otimes B}}\mathstrut

\pi_{r}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\pi_{r}\;e:=(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;\;y):{A}\mathstrut

We may then define projections on records as follows:

(34)

\pi_{(\Delta,x:{A}^{\epsilon}),y}\;e=\pi_{\Delta,y}(\pi_{l}\;e)\qquad\pi_{(\Delta,x:{A}^{\epsilon}),x}\;e=\pi_{r}\;e

with typing rule

(35)

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{\langle\Delta\rangle}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Delta(x)=A}\mathstrut

\pi_{\mathsf{rec}}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\pi_{\Delta,x}\;e:{A}\mathstrut

We will omit
\Delta
when it is clear from context. We can define a term

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{eff}(\Gamma)}\mathsf{packed}(\Gamma):{[\Gamma]}
to “pack” up the current context
into a record as follows:

(36)

\mathsf{packed}(\cdot)=()\qquad\mathsf{packed}(\Gamma,x:{A}^{\epsilon})=(\mathsf{packed}(\Gamma),x)

where we define the
effect
of a context
\Gamma
as follows

(37)

\mathsf{eff}(\cdot)=\bot\qquad\mathsf{eff}(\Gamma,x:{A}^{\epsilon})=\mathsf{eff}(\Gamma)\sqcup\epsilon

In particular, we say
\Gamma
is
pure
if
\mathsf{eff}(\Gamma)=\bot
. We may now define
packing and unpacking substitutions

\mathsf{pack}_{y}^{\otimes}(\Gamma):\Gamma\mapsto y:\langle\Gamma\rangle
,

\mathsf{unpack}_{y}^{\otimes}(\Gamma):y:\langle\Gamma\rangle\mapsto\Gamma
as follows:

(38)

\mathsf{pack}_{y}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)=y\mapsto\mathsf{packed}(\Gamma)\qquad\mathsf{unpack}_{y}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)=(x\mapsto\pi_{\Gamma,x}\;y)_{x\in\Gamma}

It turns out that our equational theory is sufficient to show that the pack and unpack substitutions
are inverses of each other
for pure contexts
, i.e.

(39)

\displaystyle[\mathsf{unpack}_{y}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)]\mathsf{pack}_{y}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\approx\mathsf{id}_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\mapsto\Gamma

\displaystyle[\mathsf{pack}_{y}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)]\mathsf{unpack}_{y}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\approx\mathsf{id}_{y:[\Gamma]}:y:\Gamma\mapsto y:\Gamma

Similarly, we may define a “packing” operation
\langle\cdot\rangle
on label-contexts as follows:

(40)

\langle\cdot\rangle=\mathbf{0}\qquad\langle\mathsf{L},\ell(A)\rangle=\langle\mathsf{L}\rangle+A

We may then define injections on enums as follows:

(41)

\iota_{(\mathsf{L},\ell(A)),\kappa}\;e=\iota_{\mathsf{L},\kappa}(\iota_{l}\;e)\qquad\iota_{(\mathsf{L},\ell(A)),\ell}\;e=\iota_{r}\;e

with typing rule

(42)

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\mathsf{L}(\ell)=A}\mathstrut

\iota_{\mathsf{enum}}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\iota_{\mathsf{L},\ell}\;e:{\langle\mathsf{L}\rangle}\mathstrut

Similarly, we can define
n
-ary case-statements on enums as follows:

(43)

\displaystyle\mathsf{case}_{\cdot}\;e\;\{\}

\displaystyle=\infty

\displaystyle\mathsf{case}_{\mathsf{L},\kappa(A)}\;e\;\{(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):t_{i})_{i},\kappa(y):s\}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\mathsf{case}_{\mathsf{L}}\;e\;\{(\ell_{i}(A_{i}):t_{i})_{i}\},\iota_{r}\;{y}:s\}

with typing rule

(44)

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{[\mathsf{L}]}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\forall i.\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}\vdash t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{K}}\mathstrut

\mathsf{case}_{\mathsf{enum}}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{case}_{\mathsf{L}}\;e\;\{(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):t_{i})_{i}\}\rhd\mathsf{K}\mathstrut

where

(45)

\infty

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\infty:=\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;()\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;x\}\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

We may now define “packing” and “unpacking” label-substitutions

\cdot\vdash\mathsf{pack}_{\kappa}^{+}(\mathsf{L}):\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\kappa([\mathsf{L}])
,

\cdot\vdash\mathsf{unpack}_{\kappa}^{+}(\mathsf{L}):\kappa([\mathsf{L}])\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{L}
as follows:

(46)

\mathsf{pack}_{\kappa}^{+}(\cdot)=\cdot\qquad\mathsf{pack}_{\kappa}^{+}(\mathsf{L},\ell(A))=[\kappa(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;\iota_{l}\;x]\mathsf{pack}_{\kappa}^{+}(\mathsf{L}),\ell(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\kappa\;\iota_{r}\;x

(47)

\mathsf{unpack}_{\kappa}^{+}(\mathsf{L})=\kappa(x)\mapsto\mathsf{unpack}^{+}(\mathsf{L})\;x

where we have

(48)

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{\mathsf{L}}}\mathstrut

unpack
+

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{unpack}^{+}(\mathsf{L})\;e\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\infty

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\infty:=\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;()\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;x\}\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

defined as follows:

(49)

\mathsf{unpack}^{+}(\cdot)\;e=\infty\qquad\mathsf{unpack}^{+}(\mathsf{L},\ell(A))\;e=\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\mathsf{unpack}^{+}(\mathsf{L})\;x,\iota_{r}\;{y}:\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;y\}

We can further show that, in this case for all label contexts
\mathsf{L}
, these substitutions are
mutually inverse, i.e., that

(50)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash[\mathsf{unpack}_{\kappa}^{+}(\mathsf{L})]\mathsf{pack}_{\kappa}^{+}(\mathsf{L})

\displaystyle\approx\mathsf{id}_{\mathsf{L}}:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{L}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash[\mathsf{pack}_{\kappa}^{+}(\mathsf{L})]\mathsf{unpack}_{\kappa}^{+}(\mathsf{L})

\displaystyle\approx\mathsf{id}_{\kappa([\mathsf{L}])}:\kappa([\mathsf{L}])\rightsquigarrow\kappa([\mathsf{L}])

Finally, fixing a distinguished variable
\square
, it will be very useful to define
a “
variable packing
” operation on expressions and regions as follows:

(51)

\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}\implies\square:[\Gamma]\vdash\langle r\rangle^{\otimes}:=[\mathsf{unpack}_{\square}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)]r\rhd\mathsf{L}

In particular, for
\Gamma
pure, the packing operation
[\cdot]
is an injection on expressions and
regions w.r.t. the equational theory, since
\mathsf{unpack}_{\square}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
has an inverse.
Similarly, fixing a distinguished label
\blacksquare
, we may define a “label packing” operation on
regions as follows:

(52)

\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}\implies\Gamma\vdash\langle r\rangle^{+}:=[\mathsf{pack}_{\blacksquare}^{+}(\mathsf{L})]r\rhd\blacksquare([\mathsf{L}])

Since the packing substitution has an inverse, it similarly follows that label packing is an
injection w.r.t. the equational theory, i.e.

(53)

\Gamma\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\iff\Gamma\vdash\langle r\rangle^{+}\approx\langle r^{\prime}\rangle^{+}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}

Finally, we can define a “packing” operation on regions to be given by label-packing followed by
variable-packing, or vice versa (since it turns out the operations commute), as follows:

(54)

\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}\implies\square:[\Gamma]\vdash\langle r\rangle:=\langle\langle r\rangle^{+}\rangle^{\otimes}=\langle\langle r\rangle^{\otimes}\rangle^{+}\rhd\blacksquare(\mathsf{L})

We similarly have that this is an injection for
\Gamma
pure, i.e.

(55)

\Gamma\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\iff\Gamma\vdash\langle r\rangle\approx\langle r^{\prime}\rangle\rhd{\mathsf{L}}

4.6.
Böhm-Jacopini for SSA

Now that we have given our equational theory, we want to show it is “good enough” to reason about
the properties inherent to all SSA-based languages. In particular, we
wish to show that we have enough power to reason about interconversion between data-flow and
control-flow. For example, a state machine can be implemented either as a switch on a state value,
or as a set of mutually-tail-recursive functions (i.e., the state can be encoded in the program
counter). We demonstrate this by using some of the machinery from the previous section to state and prove a form
of the Böhm-Jacopini theorem
(
Böhm and Jacopini, 1966
)
for SSA.

The Böhm-Jacopini theorem states that every general control-flow graph program can be rewritten to
an equivalent program which uses only structured control-flow: i.e., it can be rewritten to a program
using only conditional branching, sequencing, and loops. To adapt this result to SSA, we
need to express branching, sequencing and loops as SSA regions
\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}
,
so that we can build up an inductive set of structured regions. We will be maximally strict, and
allow branching to an exit label in
\mathsf{L}
only as the terminal statement in a structured program
(and, in particular, not from within a loop!). It is obvious that we can represent conditional
branching using
\mathsf{case}
-statements, but we need convenient primitives for sequencing and looping.
Sequencing can be expressed using a
\mathsf{where}
-block as follows:

(56)

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\blacksquare(A)}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,\square:A\vdash s\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

seq

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{seq}(r,s):=([\blacksquare(x)\mapsto\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;x]r\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(\square):\{s\})\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

where “
\square
" is a distinguished input variable, and “
\blacksquare
” is a distinguished output
label. Another, equivalent, way of writing sequencing is:

(57)

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{seq}(r,s)\approx[\blacksquare(\square)\mapsto s]r\rhd{\mathsf{L}}

That is, when we exit
r
, we jump to (a copy of)
s
.

Expressing structured looping is a bit more complicated, since we do not have
mutable variables and hence cannot directly express while loops. If we recall that loops
can be expressed as tail-recursive procedures which carry the loop state in the argument,
then we can define a “functional do-while loop” as follows:

(58)

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,\square:A\vdash r\rhd\blacksquare(B+A)}\mathstrut

loop

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{loop}(e,r):=(\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;e\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(\square):\{\mathsf{seq}(r,\mathsf{case}\;\square\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\mathsf{br}\;\blacksquare\;x,\iota_{r}\;{y}:\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;y\})\})\rhd\blacksquare(B)\mathstrut

We can now define the inductive predicate

\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}
, which says that
r
is a structured
region with input variables
\Gamma
and output labels
\mathsf{L}
, as in
Figure
27
. Note that this defines a subset of the
well-typed regions: every region
r
such that

\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}
is also well-typed as
\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}
.

It now remains to give an algorithm to convert a region
r
targeting label-context
\mathsf{L}
into an
equivalent structured region
\mathsf{WH}_{\mathsf{L}}(r)
. In particular, we define

(59)

\mathsf{WH}_{\mathsf{L}}(r)=[\mathsf{unpack}^{+}_{\blacksquare}(\mathsf{L})]\mathsf{PW}_{L}(r)

where we define

(60)

\displaystyle\mathsf{PW}_{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;a)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pack}^{+}(\mathsf{L})_{\ell}(a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{PW}_{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\mathsf{PW}_{\mathsf{L}}(r)

\displaystyle\mathsf{PW}_{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;r)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=a;\mathsf{PW}_{\mathsf{L}}(r)

\displaystyle\mathsf{PW}_{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:r,\iota_{r}\;{y}:s\})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\mathsf{PW}_{\mathsf{L}}(r),\iota_{r}\;{y}:\mathsf{PW}_{\mathsf{L}}(s)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{PW}_{\mathsf{L}}(r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{seq}(\mathsf{PW}_{\mathsf{L}}(r),\mathsf{case}\;\mathsf{ua}\;\square\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\mathsf{br}\;\blacksquare\;x

\displaystyle,\iota_{r}\;{y}:\mathsf{loop}(y,\mathsf{case}_{\mathsf{R}}\;\square\;\{\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\mathsf{seq}(\mathsf{PW}_{\mathsf{L}}(t_{i}),\mathsf{br}\;\blacksquare\;(\mathsf{ua}\;\square)\})\})

\displaystyle\qquad\text{where}\qquad\mathsf{R}=(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)

and

(61)

\mathsf{ua}_{\mathsf{L},\cdot}\;e=e\qquad\mathsf{ua}_{\mathsf{L},(\mathsf{R},\ell(A))}\;e=\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\mathsf{case}\;\mathsf{ua}_{\mathsf{L},\mathsf{R}}\;x\;\{\iota_{l}\;{z}:\iota_{l}\;z,\iota_{r}\;{w}:\iota_{r}\;\iota_{l}\;w\},\iota_{r}\;{y}:\iota_{r}\;(\iota_{r}\;y)\}

The
\mathsf{PW}_{\mathsf{L}}(r)
function does the actual transformation, and we can see that most of the
cases are trivial except for the
r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}
case. In this case, we replace the set of labels bound
by the
\mathsf{where}
clause with a tag and a while loop containing a case statement branching on the
tag. (This uses the
\mathsf{ua}
function, which implements associativity of n-ary coproducts. That is,
given
\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}e:{[L,R]}
, we have that

\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}\mathsf{ua}_{\mathsf{L}}\;e:{[L]+[R]}
.)

The Böhm-Jacopini theorem for SSA can then
be written:

Theorem 4.8 (name=Böhm-Jacopini for SSA, restate=bohmjacopini).

For all
\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}
,
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{WH}_{\mathsf{L}}(r)\rhd\mathsf{L}
is structured,
and
\Gamma\vdash r\approx\mathsf{WH}_{\mathsf{L}}(r)\rhd{\mathsf{L}}
. In particular, we have that

\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{PW}_{\mathsf{L}}(r)\rhd\blacksquare(\langle\mathsf{L}\rangle)
is structured, and

\Gamma\vdash\langle r\rangle^{+}\approx\mathsf{PW}_{r}(\rhd){\blacksquare(\langle\mathsf{L}\rangle)}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
B.2

∎

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\mathsf{L}\;\ell=A}\mathstrut

s-br

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;a\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

s-let
1
-r

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A\otimes B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A,y:B\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

s-let
2
-r

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;r\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A+B}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle{\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,x:A\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}r\rhd\mathsf{L}}}\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,y:B\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}s\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

s-case-r

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:r,\iota_{r}\;{y}:s\}\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}r\rhd\blacksquare(A)}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,\square:A\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}s\rhd\mathsf{L}}\mathstrut

s-seq

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{seq}(r,s)\rhd\mathsf{L}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{\blacksquare(A)}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\Gamma,\square:A\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}r\rhd\blacksquare(B+A)}\mathstrut

s-loop

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{s}}\mathsf{loop}(e,r)\rhd\blacksquare(B)\mathstrut

Figure 27
.
Typing rules for structured regions

5.
Denotational Semantics

5.1.
Freyd Elgot Categories

Moggi (1991)
showed that the Kleisli category of a strong
monad over a CCC interprets effectful higher-order functional
programs. There are two mismatches in using this as a semantics for
SSA. On one hand, SSA has features not necessarily supported by Moggi
models such as arbitrary, unstructured cyclic control-flow. On the
other hand Moggi models support features SSA (as a first-order
language) does not have, such as higher-order functions and
first-class computation values.

Given that we want to model SSA with some category
\mathcal{C}
, we hence have to think about what
structure we need
\mathcal{C}
to possess so that it has exactly our desired features.
Obviously, we need a way to take the product of two objects, to be able to model contexts as well as
pairs. The usual way to do this is via
monoidal categories
. However, monoidal categories
typically have too many
equations: computations operating on independent data must always commute
(this is the “sliding rule”), which is obviously not true for effects such as printing, since

\mathsf{print}(x);\mathsf{print}(y)\neq\mathsf{print}(y);\mathsf{print}(x)

Instead, we will only require that our category be
premonoidal
: “monoidal, without
sliding.” Indeed, the Kleisli category of a strong monad on a CCC is not always monoidal, as
demonstrated by the writer monad on
\mathsf{Set}
(which exposes
\mathsf{print}
), but
is
always
premonoidal. We define a premonoidal category as follows:

Definition 5.1 (Symmetric Premonoidal Category).

We define a
binoidal category
to be a category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a binary operation

\otimes:|\mathcal{C}|\times|\mathcal{C}|\to|\mathcal{C}|
on the objects of
\mathcal{C}
and, for each
A,B\in|\mathcal{C}|
, functors
A\otimes-,-\otimes B:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
. We say a morphism
f:A\to A^{\prime}
in a binoidal category is
central
if, for all
g:B\to B^{\prime}
, it satisfies

sliding
:

f\otimes B;A^{\prime}\otimes g=A\otimes g;f\otimes B^{\prime}\qquad B\otimes f;g\otimes A^{\prime}=g\otimes A;B^{\prime}\otimes f

in which case we may write these morphisms as
f\otimes g:A\otimes B\to A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}
and
g\otimes f:B\otimes A\to B^{\prime}\otimes A^{\prime}
respectively. A
premonoidal category
is, then, a
binoidal category equipped with:

•

An
identity
object
I\in|\mathcal{C}|

•

For each triple of objects
A,B,C\in|\mathcal{C}|
, a central, natural isomorphism

\alpha_{A,B,C}:(A\otimes B)\otimes C\to A\otimes(B\otimes C)
, the
associator

•

For each object
A
, central, natural isomorphisms
\lambda_{A}:A\otimes I\to A
and

\rho_{A}:I\otimes A\to A
, the
left
and
right unitors

satisfying the
triangle
and
pentagon identity

\alpha_{A,I,B};A\otimes\lambda_{B}=\rho_{A}\otimes B\qquad\alpha_{A\otimes B,C,D};\alpha_{A,B,C\otimes D}=\alpha_{A,B,C}\otimes D;\alpha_{A,B\otimes C,D};A\otimes\alpha_{A,B,C}

We say a premonoidal category is
symmetric
if it is also equipped with a central, natural
involution
\sigma_{A,B}:A\otimes B\to B\otimes A
, the
symmetry
, satisfying the

hexagon identity

\alpha_{A,B,C};\sigma_{A,B\otimes C};\alpha_{B,C,A}=\sigma_{A,B}\otimes C;\alpha_{B,A,C};B\otimes\sigma_{A,C}

We say a premonoidal category is
monoidal
if every morphism is central.

One important theorem about premonoidal categories is
coherence
:

Theorem 5.2.

The subcategory
\mathcal{A}
generated by associators, unitors, and their tensor products is an
equivalence relation, i.e., for all
A,B:|\mathcal{A}|
, if

f,g:A\to B
can be constructed using only identity, composition, associators, unitors, and
their tensor products, then:

(a)

f=g

(b)

f,g
are isomorphisms in
\mathcal{A}

In particular, as a syntactic convenience, we will often simply write “
\alpha
" for the (unique)
morphism between objects
A
and
B
, when it exists, satisfying the requirements of
Theorem
5.2
. For example, given
f:A\to B\otimes((C\otimes I)\otimes D)
and
g:((B\otimes(I\otimes C))\otimes D)\to E
, we have

f;\alpha;g:=f;B\otimes(\lambda_{C}\otimes D);\alpha_{B,C,D}^{-1};(B\otimes\rho_{C}^{-1})\otimes D;g

Just like for higher-order functional languages, we can interpret types
A
as objects
\llbracket{A}\rrbracket:|\mathcal{C}|
. Similarly, we can interpret variable contexts
\Gamma
by taking products of objects, as
follows:

\boxed{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket:|\mathcal{C}|}\qquad\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket=I\qquad\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{A}\rrbracket

We would like to interpret an expression-in-context
\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}
as a morphism in

\mathcal{C}
from
\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket
to
\llbracket{A}\rrbracket
. However, in standard SSA, it is possible for a variable
to be unused, or used multiple times. Our premonoidal structure, however, does not give us any way
to
project
out of a product type, making it impossible to interpret expressions-in-context
like

x:A,y:B\vdash x:{A}
.
In order to project out individual variables, we need Cartesian structure, but a premonoidal
category can only interpret
linear
expressions, that is, those which
use every variable exactly once. However, it is too much to require the whole premonoidal category to be Cartesian, because that would validate the sliding rule, which is what we set out to avoid.

In the case of the Kleisli category over a CCC, the Cartesian structure of the CCC becomes the premonoidal structure of the Kleisli category. This lets us use the Cartesian structure to project out the variables, while the product in the Kleisli category still does not satisfy sliding. By analogy with this case, we can suppose that there is a subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{\bot}
of the premonoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, which has the property that the premonoidal structure in
\mathcal{C}
behaves like a Cartesian product in
\mathcal{C}_{\bot}
.

This is called a
Freyd category
, and is defined as follows:

Definition 5.3 (Freyd category).

A
Freyd category
is a premonoidal category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a wide subcategory

\mathcal{C}_{\bot}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
of
pure
morphisms such that

•

\mathcal{C}_{\bot}
contains all associators, unitors, and symmetries

•

I
is a terminal object in
\mathcal{C}_{\bot}

•

For each
A,B
,
A\otimes B
is a cartesian product of
A,B
in
\mathcal{C}_{\bot}

•

For pure morphisms
f,g
,
f\otimes g=\langle\pi_{l};f,\pi_{r};g\rangle

Alternatively, it is equivalent to require

•

\mathcal{C}_{\bot}
contains all associators, unitors, and symmetries

•

I
is a terminal object in
\mathcal{C}_{\bot}

•

For each
A
, there exists a pure morphism
{\Delta}_{A}:A\to A\otimes A
forming a
comonoid with the terminal morphism
{!}_{A}:A\to I
, i.e:
{\Delta}_{A};{!}_{A}\otimes A;\rho={\Delta}_{A};A\otimes{!}_{A};\lambda=\mathsf{id}_{A}

•

For every pure morphism
f:A\to_{\bot}B
,
f;{\Delta}_{B}={\Delta}_{A};f\otimes f

and
f;!_{B}=!_{A}
.

In both cases, we have that
\pi_{l}=A\otimes{!}_{B};\lambda
,
\pi_{r}={!}_{A}\otimes B;\rho
,
\langle f,g\rangle={\Delta}_{A};f\otimes g
, and
{\Delta}_{A}=\langle\mathsf{id}_{A},\mathsf{id}_{A}\rangle

For convenience, given
f:A\to B
in a Freyd category, we will define the notation

(62)

\mathsf{let}(f):={\Delta}_{A};A\otimes f:A\to A\otimes B

Note that this is pure if and only if
f
is. This has the following useful properties:

•

\mathsf{let}(f);\pi_{r}=f
and, for
f
pure,
\mathsf{let}(f);\pi_{l}=\mathsf{id}

•

\mathsf{let}(f;g)=\mathsf{let}(f);A\otimes g
and
\mathsf{let}(\mathsf{id}_{A})={\Delta}_{A}

•

\mathsf{let}(\mathsf{let}(f))=\mathsf{let}(f);{\Delta}_{A}\otimes B;\alpha_{A,A,B}
, and, therefore, given

g:A\otimes B\to C
, we have
\mathsf{let}(\mathsf{let}(f);g)=\mathsf{let}(f);\mathsf{let}(g);\pi_{l}\otimes C

We now have everything we need to model effectful first-order expressions. For reasoning about
substitution, we will also demand that the denotation of “
pure
" expressions

\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A}
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\bot}(\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket,\llbracket{A}\rrbracket)
; in general, we will write
morphisms in
\mathcal{C}_{\bot}
as
A\to_{\bot}B
.

At this point, we still have no way to interpret control-flow, i.e.
\mathsf{case}
-expressions.
Furthermore, if we want to model regions as morphisms, we need some way of modelling label-contexts

\mathsf{L}
. At first glance, it seems sufficient for branching control-flow to require the existence
of coproducts, and indeed, assuming the existence of all coproducts and an initial object, we may
model label-contexts as follows:

\boxed{\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket:|\mathcal{C}|}\qquad\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket=\mathbf{0}\qquad\llbracket{\mathsf{L},\ell(A)}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket+\llbracket{A}\rrbracket

Regions can now be interpreted as morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
from
\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket
to
\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket
, as
desired. Just like for products, we will write “
\alpha^{+}
” for the (unique) morphism between
objects
A
and
B
, when it exists, satisfying the requirements of
Theorem
5.2
where coproducts are taken as the monoidal structure; we will
sometimes also write
\alpha^{+}_{B}
for clarity.

It turns out that our coproducts must be
distributive
to allow us to use variables in scope
before a branch. In particular, we define a
distributive
premonoidal category as follows:

Definition 5.4 (Distributive category).

A premonoidal category
\mathcal{C}
with all coproducts is
distributive
if, for all
A,B,C
,
the obvious morphism

\delta_{A,B,C}=[(A+\iota_{l}),(A+\iota_{r})]:(A\otimes B)+(A\otimes C)\to A\otimes(B+C)

has an inverse
\delta^{-1}
.
We will say a Freyd category
\mathcal{C}
is distributive if it has all coproducts and the subcategory
of pure morphisms
\mathcal{C}_{\bot}
is distributive (which implies, in particular, that
\mathcal{C}
is
distributive when taken as a premonoidal category).

We note in particular that every cartesian closed category with coproducts is a distributive Freyd
category, as is the Kleisli category of a monad over a CCC with coproducts.
For any finite coproduct
\Sigma_{i}B_{i}
, we will introduce the notation
\delta_{\Sigma}:\Sigma_{i}(A\otimes B_{i})\to A\otimes\Sigma_{i}B_{i}
and
\delta_{\Sigma}^{-1}:A\otimes\Sigma_{i}B_{i}\to\Sigma_{i}(A\otimes B_{i})
to denote the obvious morphisms.
The last piece of the puzzle, allowing us to model loops in regions, is the existence of an

iteration operator
, since we currently cannot represent any nonterminating computations. We
would further like this operator to be
strong
; that is, compatible with the distributor, so
that we can soundly access variables in scope in the loop body. Formally, we define a
(strong)
Conway iteration operator
as follows:

Definition 5.5 (Conway iteration).

A category
\mathcal{C}
with all coproducts is said to have a
iteration operator
if we can
define an operator
(-)^{\dagger}
taking every morphism
f:A\to B+A
to a morphism
f^{\dagger}:A\to B
, the
fixpoint
of
f
, with the following property: given
f:A\to B+A
, we
have
f^{\dagger}=f;[\mathsf{id},f^{\dagger}]
. We say this operator is a
Conway iteration
operator
if it additionally satisfies the following properties:

•

Naturality:
given
f:A\to B+A
and
g:B\to C
, we have

(f;g+\mathsf{id})^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger};g:A\to C

•

Dinaturality:
given morphisms
g:A\to B+C
and
h:C\to B+A
, we have that

(g;[\iota_{l},h])^{\dagger}=g;[\mathsf{id}_{B},(h;[\iota_{l},g])^{\dagger}]

•

Codiagonal:
given
f:A\to(B+A)+A
, we have

(f^{\dagger})^{\dagger}=(f;[\mathsf{id},\iota_{r}])^{\dagger}:A\to B

If
\mathcal{C}
is distributive, we say this operator is
strong
if

\forall f:A\to B+A,(C\otimes f;\delta^{-1})^{\dagger}=C\otimes f^{\dagger}

Given a wide subcategory
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
, we say
\mathcal{C}
is
\mathcal{K}
-uniform
if,
for all
h:A\to_{\mathcal{K}}B
,
f:B\to C+B
, and
g:A\to C+A
, we have that

h;f=g;C+h\implies h;f^{\dagger}=g^{\dagger}

We will call Freyd categories equipped with an appropriate Elgot structure
strong Elgot
categories
. In particular, we define

Definition 5.6 ((Strong) Elgot structure).

A Freyd category
\mathcal{C}
with all coproducts is said to have an
Elgot structure
if it has
a Conway iteration operator which is
\mathcal{C}_{\bot}
uniform. If
\mathcal{C}
is distributive, we say

\mathcal{C}
is strong Elgot if its iteration operator is strong. In particular, we say a monad is
Elgot if its Kliesli category has an Elgot structure. Similarly, we say a
strong
monad is
strong Elgot if its Kliesli category has a strong Elgot structure.

It turns out that, to check something is a strong Elgot category, we do not need to
explicitly verify dinaturality. In particularly, we may verify the following:

Proposition 5.7.

If
(-)^{\dagger}
is an iteration operator which satisfies naturality and codiagonal and is

\mathcal{K}
-uniform for
\mathcal{K}
co-Cartesian, then it also satisfies dinaturality.

Proof.

See Lemma 31 of
Goncharov and Schröder (2018)

∎

Since in a distributive Freyd category
\mathcal{C}_{\bot}
must be distributive (and hence co-cartesian),
dinaturality follows from the other axioms of an Elgot category.
Given a distributive Freyd category equipped with a (strong) Elgot structure, we will often want to
consider the fixpoint of a morphism
f:R\otimes A\to B+A
, where our “context”
R
does not
change between iterations. To do this, we first need to build up a morphism

(63)

\mathsf{rcase}(f):=\mathsf{let}(f);\pi_{l}\otimes(B+A);\delta^{-1}:R\otimes A\to R\otimes B+R\otimes A

which computes
f
and then distributes a copy of the read-only state
R
to each branch of the
result. We may then define the fixpoint

(64)

\mathsf{rfix}(f):=(\mathsf{rcase}(f))^{\dagger};\pi_{r}:R\otimes A\to B

We consider some more properties of
\mathsf{rcase}(f)
and
\mathsf{rfix}(f)
in Appendix
A
.

5.2.
String Diagrams

String diagrams
provide a graphical calculus for reasoning about (symmetric) monoidal
categories, which allows us to succinctly express complex morphisms and rewrites. Since both
cartesian and co-cartesian categories are monoidal (with the product and coproduct as tensor,
respectively), we can use string diagrams to reason about both. In the co-cartesian case, string
diagrams behave much like control-flow diagrams, with boxes representing sub-programs, input wires
entry points, and output wires exit points. In particular, a
region
is just a box with a
single input wire. Continuing this analogy, we draw the codiagonal morphism
[\mathsf{id}_{A},\mathsf{id}_{A}]

as joining two wires, and the zero morphism as a wire coming from nowhere, as in
Figure
28
.

subprogram 1
subprogram 2
subprogram 3
subprogram 4
Zero morphism
Region
Codiagonal morphism

Figure 28
.

A string diagram using the coproduct as symmetric monoidal structure, interpreted as a CFG

A string diagram using the coproduct as symmetric monoidal structure, showing how to
draw the codiagonal morphism and zero morphism, as well as showing what the analogue to a
subprogram and region might look like.

The power of string diagrams comes from the fact that many syntactically distinct ways to write
equal values are obviously graphically equivalent by
isotopy
: essentially, moving boxes and
wires around. String diagrams also give us an elegant way to represent and reason about Elgot
structures. It turns out that Elgot structures induce a
trace
on the coproduct

(
Hasegawa, 2002
)
: given
f:A+C\to B+C
, we can define

(65)

\mathsf{Tr}_{A,B}^{C}(f)=\iota_{l};[f;B+\iota_{r}]^{\dagger}=\iota_{l};f;[\mathsf{id},(\iota_{l};f)^{\dagger}]:A\to B

Since this satisfies the axioms of a trace over a symmetric monoidal category, we can draw it, and
therefore the Elgot operator, as in Figure
29
. Continuing with the
control-flow diagram analogy, such traces can be interpreted as
loops
, with the Elgot axioms,
now drawn as diagrams in Figure
30
.

Unfortunately, unmodified string diagrams do not work for premonoidal categories, and hence for
Freyd categories. The reason is because, since not all morphisms are central, premonoidal categories
do not in general validate
sliding
. However, this is easy enough to fix: we can postulate a
“state” wire, drawn in red, which all impure morphisms require as an input and output, as in
Figure
31
. Since the state wire linearly threads through all
impure boxes, it establishes a unique order in which they must be executed; this construction is
shown to be sound in
Román (2023)
. Pure morphisms do not have a state wire, so a diagram
representing a pure morphism will simply have a red “stripe” on the side. This gives us a
convenient way to distinguish between string diagrams using the monoidal structure induced by the
coproduct and those using the premonoidal structure induced by the tensor product in a category
having both (such as a distributive premonoidal category): the latter will have a state wire, while
the former will not.

\mathbb{N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot+2}
print
print
\neq
\mathbb{N}
print
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot+2}
print

Figure 31
.

A string diagram in a premonoidal category, demonstrating the necessity of using a state wire

A string diagram in a premonoidal category, using a red state wire

5.3.
Semantics

We now have all the ingredients we need to give a semantics to
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
expressions and regions.
In particular, an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
expression model consists of:

•

A premonoidal category
\mathcal{C}

•

A complete lattice
E
, and a continuous function
\epsilon\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
from

E
to wide subcategories of
\mathcal{C}
, such that
\mathcal{C}_{\top}=\mathcal{C}
, and
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\bot})
is a distributive Freyd category. Furthermore, we ask that each
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
is
closed under tensor products and coproducts, and in particular that injections and (inverse)
distributors are pure and therefore contained in every
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
.

If an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
expression model additionally has an Elgot structure on
\mathcal{C}
(taken as a
Freyd category with pure morphisms
\mathcal{C}_{\bot}
), we will refer to it simply as an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}

model.

We will write morphisms
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}(A,B)
as
A\to_{\epsilon}B
, and morphisms
\mathcal{C}(A,B)

as
A\to B
. Note that, whenever
\mathcal{C}
is a distributive Elgot Freyd category, we can trivially
obtain an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model by choosing
E=\{\top,\bot\}
; hence, this re-framing does not add
any additional generality, but simplifies bookkeeping. We may also choose
E=\{*\}
if and only if

\mathcal{C}
is in fact Cartesian.
Fixing an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
expression model, we can model types in the obvious manner:

•

The unit type
\mathbf{1}
is modelled as the monoidal unit
I

•

The empty type
\mathbf{0}
is modelled as the initial object
\mathbf{0}

•

Products
A\otimes B
are modelled as tensor products
\llbracket{A}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{B}\rrbracket

•

Sum types
A+B
are modelled as coproducts
\llbracket{A}\rrbracket+\llbracket{B}\rrbracket

We’ll say an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model
interprets
a signature
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{I})
if we have

•

For every base type
X\in\mathcal{T}
, an object
\llbracket{X}\rrbracket:|\mathcal{C}|

•

For every primitive instruction
f\in\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}(A,B)
, a morphism
\llbracket{f}\rrbracket:\llbracket{A}\rrbracket\to_{\epsilon}\llbracket{B}\rrbracket
.

This gives us all the ingredients to interpret
contexts
and
label contexts
, which, as
in Figure
32
, we will interpret as tensor products and coproducts of the
denotations of their parameters, respectively.

\boxed{\llbracket{A}\rrbracket:|\mathcal{C}|}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\mathbf{1}}\rrbracket=I\qquad\llbracket{A\otimes B}\rrbracket=\llbracket{A}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{B}\rrbracket\qquad\llbracket{\mathbf{0}}\rrbracket=\mathbf{0}\qquad\llbracket{A+B}\rrbracket=\llbracket{A}\rrbracket+\llbracket{B}\rrbracket

\boxed{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket:|\mathcal{C}|}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket=I\qquad\llbracket{\Gamma,x:{A}^{\epsilon}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{A}\rrbracket

\boxed{\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket:|\mathcal{C}|}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket=0\qquad\llbracket{\mathsf{L},\ell(A)}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket+\llbracket{A}\rrbracket

\boxed{\llbracket{\Gamma\leq\Delta}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\to_{\bot}\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\cdot\leq\cdot}\rrbracket=\mathsf{id}_{I}\qquad\llbracket{\Gamma,x:{A}^{\epsilon}\leq\Delta}\rrbracket=\pi_{l};\llbracket{\Gamma\leq\Delta}\rrbracket\qquad\llbracket{\Gamma,x:{A}^{\epsilon}\leq\Delta,x:{A}^{\epsilon}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\leq\Delta}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{A}\rrbracket

\boxed{\llbracket{\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket\to_{\bot}\llbracket{\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\cdot\leq\cdot}\rrbracket=\mathsf{id}_{\mathbf{0}}\qquad\llbracket{\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{K},\ell(A)}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket;\iota_{\ell}\qquad\llbracket{\mathsf{L},\ell(A)\leq\mathsf{K},\ell(A)}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket+\llbracket{A}\rrbracket

Figure 32
.
Denotational semantics for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
types, contexts, and weakenings

We can now interpret
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
expressions
\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}
over a given
signature in a model interpreting that signature as morphisms
\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\to_{\epsilon}\llbracket{A}\rrbracket

using the rules in Figure
33
. Up to this point, both our syntax and semantics
are quite standard; in particular:

•

Variables
x
are modelled as projections from the appropriate index in the context’s
denotation
\pi_{\Gamma,x}:\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\to_{\bot}\llbracket{A}\rrbracket

•

Applications of primitive instructions
f\;a
are modelled as
\llbracket{f}\rrbracket
precomposed with the
denotation of
a
.

•

Unary
\mathsf{let}
-bindings are modelled by:

–

Duplicating the context using the diagonal morphism
\Delta_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket}

–

Passing the right copy of the context through
\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket
to
get an input of type
\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{A}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A}\rrbracket

–

Passing the result of this through
\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\rrbracket
to get
the final result of type
\llbracket{B}\rrbracket

•

Pairs are modelled by passing the result of the diagonal morphism
\Delta_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket}
to

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket\ltimes\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\rrbracket

i.e., first
passing the left copy through
\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket
and then the right copy
through
\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\rrbracket
. By the axioms of a Freyd category, for pure
morphisms, this is the same as simply taking the product

\langle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket,\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\rrbracket\rangle
.

•

Binary
\mathsf{let}
-bindings are modelled similarly to unary
\mathsf{let}
-bindings, except that after
passing the right copy of the context through
\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A\otimes B}}\rrbracket
, we
re-associate
\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes(\llbracket{A}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{B}\rrbracket)
to
(\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{A}\rrbracket)\otimes\llbracket{B}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A,y:B}\rrbracket
before passing the entire result through

\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}c:{C}}\rrbracket
.

•

The unit value
()
is modelled as the terminal morphism
1_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket}
, while

\mathsf{abort}\;a
is modelled as the denotation of
a
postcomposed with the zero morphism

0_{\llbracket{A}\rrbracket}
. Injections, similarly, are simply modelled as the appropriate coproduct injections.

•

A
\mathsf{case}
-expression is modelled by

–

Duplicating the context using the diagonal morphism
\Delta_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket}

–

Using the right copy of the context to compute the discriminant using

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A+B}}\rrbracket

–

Applying the inverse distributor
\delta^{-1}
to obtain a coproduct

\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{A}\rrbracket+\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{B}\rrbracket

–

Computing
\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{C}}\rrbracket
on the right
branch and
\llbracket{\Gamma,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{C}}\rrbracket
on the left branch

\boxed{\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\to_{\epsilon}\llbracket{A}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}x:{A}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\pi_{\Gamma,x}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}f\;a:{B}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket{f}\rrbracket\circ\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;b:{B}}\rrbracket
