\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}(\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket);\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}(a,b):{A\otimes B}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket};\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket\ltimes\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;\;c:{C}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}(\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A\otimes B}}\rrbracket);\alpha;\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}c:{C}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}():{\mathbf{1}}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=1_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\iota_{l}\;{a}:{A+B}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket;\iota_{l}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\iota_{r}\;{b}:{A+B}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\rrbracket;\iota_{r}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:a,\iota_{r}\;{y}:b\}:{C}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}(\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A+B}}\rrbracket);\delta^{-1}_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket};

\displaystyle\qquad[\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{C}}\rrbracket,\llbracket{\Gamma,y:B\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{C}}\rrbracket]

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{abort}\;{a}:{A}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{\mathbf{0}}}\rrbracket;0_{\llbracket{A}\rrbracket}

\text{where}\quad\boxed{\pi_{\Gamma,x}:\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\to_{\bot}\llbracket{A}\rrbracket}\qquad\pi_{(\Gamma,x:A),x}=\pi_{r}\qquad\pi_{(\Gamma,y:B),x}=\pi_{l};\pi_{\Gamma,x}

Figure 33
.
Denotational semantics for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
expressions
Denotational semantics for isotope-SSA expressions

Similarly, if we in fact have an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model, we can interpret
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
regions

\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}
as morphisms
\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\to\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket
; note that we don’t assume
anything about the effect of these morphisms. As
\mathsf{L}
is a coproduct, we can view the result
object of a region
r
as encoding both
data
and
control-flow
information. In
particular, we interpret a branch
\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;a
as simply the injection of the (pure) expression

\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A}
, our
data
, into the element of the coproduct corresponding to

\ell
, which encodes the point in control-flow the rest of the program should jump to next. This is
in contrast to
expressions
, which purely encode data, with no particular instructions on how
to use it afterwards. Our interpretation of
\mathsf{let}
-statements and
\mathsf{case}
-statements, given in
Figure
34
, is exactly the same as that of the corresponding expressions.

Finally, we come to the interpretation of
\mathsf{where}
-statements, which is where the Elgot structure
comes in. The semantics of a
\mathsf{where}
-block

\Gamma\vdash r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L}
can be broken down into two
major components:

•

The
terminator

\mathsf{esem}_{\Gamma,\mathsf{L}}(r):\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\to\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket+\Sigma_{i}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket
,
which, given as input the context
\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket
, executes
r
and then re-associates the output.
The output type
\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket+\Sigma_{i}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket
expresses that
r
may either:

–

Via the left injection, return immediately, jumping to an enclosing label in
\mathsf{L}

–

Via the right injection, jump to the
i^{th}
basic block
t_{i}
in the

\mathsf{where}
-statement by returning a value in
\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket

•

The “
loop
”
\mathsf{lsem}_{\Gamma,\mathsf{L}}((\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}):\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\Sigma_{i}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket\to\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket+\Sigma_{i}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket
which, given as input
the context
\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket
and an input
\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket
for the
i^{th}
basic block
t_{i}
, executes

t_{i}
and then re-associates the output. The output type
\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket+\Sigma_{i}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket
again
expresses that control-flow may either exit the
\mathsf{where}
-block (via
\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket
) or jump to
some other basic block (via
\Sigma_{i}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket
).

We glue these together in the obvious manner to get the semantics for a where-block:

•

Compute
\mathsf{let}(\mathsf{esem}_{\Gamma,\mathsf{L}}(r))
, which, given as input the context

\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket
, copies the context, executes
\mathsf{esem}_{r,,}(
) and then returns the copied context
and the output as
\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes(\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket+\Sigma_{i}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket)

•

Distribute the context into each branch of the coproduct, yielding

\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket+\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\Sigma_{i}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket

•

If we are in the left branch, project out the result to yield
\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket
, and return
immediately

•

Otherwise, compute
\mathsf{lsem}_{\Gamma,\mathsf{L}}((\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})
in a loop,
passing in a fresh copy of the context each time. This is implemented with
\mathsf{rfix}
.

This simplifies significantly in the case of a
\mathsf{where}
-block defining just a single label,
yielding

(66)

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash r\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{s\}\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket=\mathsf{let}(\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},\ell(A)}\rrbracket);\delta^{-1};[\pi_{r},\mathsf{rfix}(\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A\vdash s\rhd\mathsf{L},\ell(A)}\rrbracket)]

In particular, it is a consequence of label weakening (Lemma
5.8
) that, in the case where

s
does not call
\ell
, this simplifies further to

(67)

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash r\;\mathsf{where}\;\ell(x):\{s\}\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket=\mathsf{let}(\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},\ell(A)}\rrbracket);\delta^{-1};[\pi_{r},\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A\vdash s\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket]

\boxed{\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\to\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{br}\;\ell\;a\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A}}\rrbracket;\iota_{\mathsf{L},\ell}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}(\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket);\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=e;r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}(\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A\otimes B}}\rrbracket);\alpha;\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A,y:B\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{case}\;e\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:r,\iota_{r}\;{y}:s\}\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}(\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A+B}}\rrbracket);\delta^{-1};

\displaystyle\quad\;[\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket,\llbracket{\Gamma,y:B\vdash s\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket]

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash r\;\mathsf{where}\;(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}(\mathsf{esem}_{\Gamma,\mathsf{L}}(r));\delta^{-1};[\pi_{r},\mathsf{rfix}(\mathsf{lsem}_{\Gamma,\mathsf{L}}((\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}))]

where

\displaystyle\boxed{\iota_{\mathsf{L},\ell}:\llbracket{A}\rrbracket\to_{\bot}\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket}\qquad\qquad\qquad\iota_{(\mathsf{L},\ell(A)),\ell}=\iota_{r}\qquad\iota_{(\mathsf{L},\kappa(B)),x}=\iota_{l};\iota_{\mathsf{L},\ell}

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathsf{esem}_{\Gamma,\mathsf{L}}(r):\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\to\llbracket{L}\rrbracket+\Sigma_{i}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathsf{esem}_{\Gamma,\mathsf{L}}(r)=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{i}(A_{i}),)_{i}}\rrbracket;\alpha^{+}_{\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket+\Sigma_{i}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathsf{lsem}_{\Gamma,\mathsf{L}}(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i}):\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\Sigma_{i}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket\to\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket+\Sigma_{i}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathsf{lsem}_{\Gamma,\mathsf{L}}(\ell_{i}(x_{i}):\{t_{i}\},)_{i})=\delta^{-1}_{\Sigma};[\llbracket{\Gamma,x_{i}:A_{i}\vdash t_{i}\rhd\mathsf{L},(\ell_{j}(A_{j}),)_{j}}\rrbracket,]_{i};\alpha^{+}_{\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket+\Sigma_{i}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket}

Figure 34
.
Denotational semantics for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
regions
Denotational semantics for isotope-SSA regions

5.4.
Metatheory

We can now begin to state the metatheoretic properties of our denotational semantics. Before we do so, we establish the convention that whenever we have an equation involving the interpretation of a derivation (e.g.,
\llbracket{\mathcal{D}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\rrbracket
), we assume that all the derivations (e.g.,
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
) exist and are well-formed.

We begin with
weakening: as shown in Figure
32
, weakenings are modelled, essentially, as
projections from a larger product
\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket
to a smaller product
\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket
, while
label-weakenings are modelled as injections from a smaller coproduct
\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket
to a larger
coproduct
\llbracket{\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket
; in particular, in both cases, the morphisms are pure. A simple induction
can then be used to derive the following weakening lemmas:

Lemma 5.8 (name=(Label) Weakening, restate=weakeninglem).

Given
\Gamma\leq\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{L}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{L}
,
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{K}
, we have

(a)

\llbracket{\Gamma\leq\Delta}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\leq\Gamma^{\prime}}\rrbracket;\llbracket{\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Delta}\rrbracket

(b)

\llbracket{\mathsf{L}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\mathsf{L}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket;\llbracket{\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket

(c)

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\leq\Gamma^{\prime}}\rrbracket;\llbracket{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket

(d)

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\leq\Gamma^{\prime}}\rrbracket;\llbracket{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}^{\prime}}\rrbracket;\llbracket{\mathsf{L}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket

(e)

\llbracket{\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\leq\Gamma^{\prime}}\rrbracket;\llbracket{\gamma:\Gamma^{\prime}\mapsto\Delta}\rrbracket

(f)

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\leq\Gamma^{\prime}}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket;\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}\rrbracket;\llbracket{\mathsf{K}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket

Proof.

See Appendix
B.3
.
∎

Our first proper semantic theorem is that
rewriting
is sound: if two (potentially impure!)
substitutions have pointwise equal semantics, then substituting by either yields a result with
the same semantics. That is, value-substitution is a well-defined operation when quotienting by the
semantics, or, more formally:

Theorem 5.9 (Soundness (Rewriting)).

Given substitutions
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta
such that, for all

x:{A}^{\epsilon}\in\Delta
,
\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\gamma\;x:{A}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\gamma^{\prime}\;x:{A}}\rrbracket
, we have

(a)

Given
\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}
,

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}[\gamma]a:{A}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}[\gamma^{\prime}]a:{A}}\rrbracket

(b)

Given
\rho:\Delta\mapsto\Xi
,
\llbracket{[\gamma]\rho:\Gamma\mapsto\Xi}\rrbracket=\llbracket{[\gamma^{\prime}]\rho:\Gamma\mapsto\Xi}\rrbracket

(c)

Given
\Delta\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}
,
\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash[\gamma]r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash[\gamma^{\prime}]r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket

(d)

Given
\Delta\vdash\rho:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}
,

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash[\gamma]\rho:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash[\gamma^{\prime}]\rho:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket

Proof.

By a trivial induction, since our semantics is compositional.
∎

This result allows us to safely perform peephole rewrites which we can independently prove
semantically valid, including on potentially impure operations. However, for pure substitutions, it
is possible to relate the semantics of the substituted term to the original more precisely. By

pure
substitutions, we mean substitutions which do not replace variables with impure terms.
More precisely, we can define the
effect
of a context using the lattice structure on effects
as follows

(68)

\mathsf{eff}(\cdot)=\bot\qquad\mathsf{eff}(\Gamma,x:{A}^{\epsilon})=\mathsf{eff}(\Gamma)\sqcup\epsilon

We can now give a denotational semantics to substitutions in which a substitution

\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta
is interpreted as a morphism from
\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket
to

\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket
with effect
\leq\mathsf{eff}(\Delta)
, as in Figure
35
. Such a
substitution is
pure
if the effect of each of its components is
\bot
, i.e., we have

(69)

\gamma\;\mathsf{pure}\iff\forall x,\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\gamma\;x:{\Delta\;x}}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\to_{\bot}\llbracket{\Delta\;x}\rrbracket

In particular, we have that

(1)

\mathsf{eff}(\Delta)=\bot\implies\gamma\;\mathsf{pure}

(2)

\gamma\;\mathsf{pure}\implies{\gamma}^{\upharpoonleft}\;\mathsf{pure}
and
{\gamma}^{\upharpoonright}\;\mathsf{pure}
;
in particular, the identity substitution is obviously pure.

We can now state the soundness of variable substitution as follows:

Theorem 5.10 (name=Soundness (Substitution), restate=soundnesssubst).

Given
\llbracket{\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\to\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket
pure, we have that

(a)

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}[\gamma]a:{A}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta}\rrbracket;\llbracket{\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket

(b)

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash[\gamma]r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta}\rrbracket;\llbracket{\Delta\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket

(c)

\llbracket{[\gamma]\rho:\Delta\mapsto\Xi}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta}\rrbracket;\llbracket{\rho:\Delta\mapsto\Xi}\rrbracket

(d)

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash[\gamma]\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta}\rrbracket;\llbracket{\Delta\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket

Proof.

See Appendix
B.3
.
∎

In particular, this implies that, when
\gamma
is pure, the semantics of substitution composition

[\gamma]\rho
is just composition of the denotations of
\gamma,\rho
; note in general that this is

not
true in general if only the
\rho
is pure, as can trivially be seen from the example

\gamma=x\mapsto\mathsf{print};x
,
\rho=x\mapsto()
. Note also that we can derive
rewriting (Theorem
5.9
) for pure substitutions from soundness of substitution, but
not vice versa; however, rewriting also covers
impure
substitutions; this is similar to the
distinction between
uniformity
(which only holds for pure operations) and
dinaturality

(which holds in general, but has a more restricted form). We can derive the following important
corollary:

Corollary 5.11 (Soundness (Single Substitution)).

Given
\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A}
, we have

(1)

Given
\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\bot}b:{B}
,

(70)

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}[a/x]b:{B}}\rrbracket=\mathsf{let}(\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A}}\rrbracket);\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{B}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;x=a;\;b:{B}}\rrbracket

(2)

Given
\Gamma,x:A\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}
, we have

(71)

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash[a/x]r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket=\mathsf{let}(\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A}}\rrbracket);\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\;x=a;r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket

Proof.

Follows immediately from the fact that

(72)

\llbracket{{x\mapsto a}^{\upharpoonleft}:\Gamma\mapsto\Gamma,x:A}\rrbracket=\Delta_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket};\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A}}\rrbracket=\mathsf{let}(\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\bot}a:{A}}\rrbracket)

∎

\boxed{\llbracket{\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\to_{\mathsf{eff}(\Delta)}\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\cdot:\Gamma\mapsto\cdot}\rrbracket={!}_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket}\qquad\llbracket{\gamma,x\mapsto e:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta,x:{A}^{\epsilon}}\rrbracket={\Delta}_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket};\llbracket{\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta}\rrbracket\ltimes\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A}}\rrbracket

\boxed{\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\kappa:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket\to\llbracket{\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\cdot:\cdot\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket={!}_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket}\otimes\mathbf{0};\lambda;0_{\mathsf{K}}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\kappa,\ell(x)\mapsto r\vdash\Gamma:\mathsf{L},\ell(A)\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket=\delta;[\llbracket{\kappa\vdash\Gamma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket,\llbracket{\Gamma,x:A\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket]

Figure 35
.
Denotational semantics for
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
(label) substitutions

We can now move on to stating the metatheoretic properties of label-substitutions, which,
thankfully, turn out to be a little bit simpler. In particular, in Figure
35
,
we interpret label substitutions
\Gamma\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}
as morphisms taking
a copy of the context
\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket
and an element of the coproduct
\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket
to an element
of the coproduct
\llbracket{\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket
, with an arbitrary effect. Label substitution is then sound in
general, as stated in the following theorem:

Theorem 5.12 (name=Soundness (Label Substitution), restate=soundnesslsubst).

Given
\Gamma\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}
, we have

(a)

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash[\sigma]r\rhd\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket=\mathsf{let}(\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket);\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket

(b)

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash[\sigma]\sigma^{\prime}:\mathsf{M}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket={\Delta}_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket}\otimes\llbracket{\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket;\alpha;\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\sigma^{\prime}:\mathsf{M}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket;\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket

Proof.

See Appendix
B.4

∎

5.5.
Equational Theory

Using the metatheory in the previous section, our goal is now to prove the equational theory given
in Section
4
sound with respect to any valid
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model. Stated more
precisely, we have the following:

Theorem 5.13 (name=Soundness (Equational Theory), restate=soundnesseqn).

We have that

(a)

\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx a^{\prime}:{A}\implies\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a^{\prime}:{A}}\rrbracket

(b)

\Gamma\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\implies\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash r^{\prime}\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket

(c)

\gamma\approx\gamma^{\prime}:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta\implies\llbracket{\gamma:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\gamma^{\prime}:\Gamma\mapsto\Delta}\rrbracket

(d)

\sigma\approx\sigma^{\prime}\vdash\Gamma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}\implies\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\sigma:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\sigma^{\prime}:\mathsf{L}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket

Proof.

See Appendix
B.5

∎

Now that we’ve proved the
soundness
of our equational theory, what remains is to prove that
it is
complete
, i.e., that every equation which holds in all
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
models can be
derived from it, or, stated more categorically, that our syntax quotiented by the equational theory
forms an initial
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model.
Our strategy for doing this is as follows:

(1)

We begin by constructing a category of expressions
\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
and a category
of regions
\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})
quotiented by our equational theory, and constructing a
functor from the former to the latter.

(2)

We then show that the category of expressions and the category of regions have the structure
of an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
expression model and
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model, respectively, and hence that
expressions may be interpreted in the former and both expressions and regions in the latter.

To do so, we will first need some notation to talk about the behaviour of the equivalence classes
of quotients. Suppose
S
and
T
are sets of terms. Then we will write

\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}S:{A}
to mean that for every
a\in S
, we have

\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}
, and similarly we will write
\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}S\approx T:{A}

when for all
a\in S
and
b\in T
, we have
\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}a\approx b:{A}
. We generalize in
the obvious fashion to regions as well as the case when only one side of an equivalence is a set
of terms.

Then, it will turn out that, for a distinguished variable
\square
and label
\blacksquare
,

(73)

\begin{gathered}\Gamma,\square:\langle\Delta\rangle\vdash_{\epsilon}\llbracket{\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)}:{A}\\
\Gamma,\square:\langle\Delta\rangle\vdash\llbracket{\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}\rhd\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(A)\\
\Gamma,\square:\langle\Delta\rangle\vdash\llbracket{\Delta\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}\rhd\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(\langle\mathsf{K}\rangle)\end{gathered}

where packing of contexts is defined as in Section
4.5
.

(3)

Finally, we refine this result to show that

(74)

\begin{gathered}\Gamma,\square:[\Delta]\vdash_{\epsilon}\llbracket{\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)}\approx\langle a\rangle:{A}\\
\Gamma,\square:[\Delta]\vdash\llbracket{\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}\approx\mathsf{ret}\;\langle a\rangle\rhd{\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(A)}\\
\Gamma,\square:[\Delta]\vdash\llbracket{\Delta\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}\approx\langle r\rangle\rhd{\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(\langle\mathsf{K}\rangle)}\end{gathered}

where
\mathsf{ret}\;a:=\mathsf{br}\;\blacksquare\;a
. Since the packing operator
\langle\cdot\rangle
on terms and
regions from Section
4.5
is injective for pure contexts
\mathsf{eff}(\Gamma)=\bot
, and hence in particular for
\Gamma=\cdot
,
\mathsf{L}=\cdot
, it follows that in this
case the category of expressions and the category of regions are the initial distributive

\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
expression model and
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model respectively.

5.6.
Expressions

We’ll begin by going over the entire proof of completeness for expressions, which is the simpler
case. In particular, we may define the category
\mathsf{Th}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
of expressions
with effect
\epsilon
as follows:

•

Objects
|\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)|
types
A,B,C

•

Morphisms
\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)_{\epsilon}(A,B)=\{e\mid\Gamma,\square:A\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{B}\}
quotiented by
\Gamma,\square:A\vdash_{\epsilon}e\approx e^{\prime}:{B}

•

Identity
(\Gamma,\square:A\vdash_{\bot}\square:{A})\in\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)_{\epsilon}(A,A)

•

Composition
e;e^{\prime}=(\mathsf{let}\;\square=e;e^{\prime})
, which satisfies

\Gamma,\square:A\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{B},\quad\Gamma,\square:B\vdash_{\epsilon}e^{\prime}:{C}\qquad\implies\qquad\Gamma,\square:A\vdash_{\epsilon}e;e^{\prime}:{C}

We may verify that this satisfies the axioms of a category w.r.t. our equational theory

In general, the category of expressions
\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
is simply then given by

\mathsf{Th}_{\top}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
, which can be viewed as the union of all

\mathsf{Th}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
.

We now need to equip
\mathsf{Th}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
with the structure of a premonoidal
category. Obviously, we wish to define the tensor product of types
A
and
B
to be simply
A\otimes B
; we can then begin by defining projections

(75)

\Gamma,\square:A\otimes B\vdash_{\epsilon}\pi_{l}:=\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=\square;x:{A}\qquad\Gamma,\square:A\otimes B\vdash_{\epsilon}\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=\square;y:{B}

By simply using the pair constructor as a cartesian product
\langle a,b\rangle=(a,b)
, this
can be shown to endow
\mathsf{Th}_{\bot}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
with the structure of a cartesian category,
allowing us to define the associators, symmetries, and unitors in the natural manner. If we then
define tensor functors

(76)

-\otimes X:e\mapsto\mathsf{let}\;(\square,x)=\square;(e;(\square,x))\qquad X\otimes-:e\mapsto\mathsf{let}\;(x,\square)=\square;(e;(x,\square))

we find that
\mathsf{Th}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
and hence in particular
\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
is
endowed with the structure of a Freyd category with pure subcategory
\mathsf{Th}_{\bot}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
.

Similarly, we wish to show that
A+B
is the coproduct of
A
and
B
in

\mathsf{Th}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
. Since we already have obvious injection morphisms

(77)

\Gamma,\square:A\vdash_{\epsilon}\iota_{l}:=\iota_{l}\;\square:{A+B}\qquad\Gamma,\square:B\vdash_{\epsilon}\iota_{r}:=\iota_{r}\;\square:{A+B}

we can define the coproduct of morphisms
\Gamma,\square:A\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{C}
and

\Gamma,\square:B\vdash_{\epsilon}b:{C}
to be simply given by

(78)

\Gamma,\square:A+B\vdash_{\epsilon}[a,b]:=\mathsf{case}\;\square\;\{\iota_{l}\;{\square}:a,\iota_{r}\;{\square}:b\}:{C}

It is straightforward to verify that this indeed induces a coproduct on

\mathsf{Th}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
and hence on
\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
All that remains is to
show that
\mathsf{Th}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)
is in fact a
distributive
Freyd category. To do
so, we may define an inverse distributor morphism

(79)

\Gamma,\square:A\otimes(B+C)\vdash_{\epsilon}\delta^{-1}:=\mathsf{let}\;(x,y)=\square;\mathsf{case}\;y\;\{\iota_{l}\;{z}:\iota_{l}(x,z),\iota_{r}\;{z}:\iota_{r}(x,z)\}:{A\otimes B+A\otimes C}

which can easily be shown to be an inverse to the obvious distributor morphism. We may now note that

(80)

\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)}=\mathbf{1},\quad\llbracket{\Delta,x:{A}^{\epsilon}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)}=\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)}\otimes A\qquad\implies\qquad\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)}=\langle\Gamma\rangle

Therefore, it follows that, as expected, that

\Gamma,\square:\langle\Delta\rangle\vdash_{\epsilon}\llbracket{\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)}:{A}

and it remains to show that we in fact have

\Gamma,\square:\langle\Delta\rangle\vdash_{\epsilon}\llbracket{\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\Gamma)}\approx[a]:{A}

which can be done by a relatively straightforward induction, implying, since
[\cdot]
is injective
w.r.t. our equational theory for pure contexts, the following theorem:

Theorem 5.14 (name=Completeness (Expressions), restate=completenessexpr).

We have that, for all pure
\mathsf{eff}(\Gamma)=\bot
,

\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e\approx e^{\prime}:{A}\iff\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e:{A}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\cdot)}=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash_{\epsilon}e^{\prime}:{A}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}(\cdot)}

In particular, this implies that
\mathsf{Th}(\cdot)
is the initial
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
expression model

Proof.

See Appendix
B.6

∎

5.7.
Regions

We define the category
\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})
of regions as follows:

•

Objects
|\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})|
types
A,B,C

•

Morphisms
\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})(A,B)=\{r\mid\Gamma,\square:A\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(B)\}

quotiented by
\Gamma,\square:A\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(B)}

•

Identity
\Gamma,\square:A\vdash\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{ret}\;\square\rhd\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(A)

where
\mathsf{ret}\;a:=\mathsf{br}\;\blacksquare\;a

•

Composition
r;r^{\prime}=[{(\blacksquare(\square)\mapsto r^{\prime})}^{\upharpoonleft}]r

In particular, we may view
\mathsf{ret}
as an identity-on-objects functor

\mathsf{Th}^{\otimes}_{\bot}(\Gamma)\to\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})
with action on morphisms given by given
by

(81)

\Gamma,\square:A\vdash_{\bot}e:{B}\qquad\mapsto\qquad\Gamma,\square(A)\vdash\mathsf{ret}\;e\rhd\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(B)

We will use this to equip
\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})
with the structure of a Freyd category.
In particular, taking our subcategory of pure morphisms to be the image of
\mathsf{ret}
in

\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})
, we may define the obvious tensor functors

(82)

-\otimes X:r\mapsto\mathsf{let}\;(\square,x)=\square;(r;\mathsf{ret}\;(\square,x))\qquad X\otimes-:r\mapsto\mathsf{let}\;(x,\square)=\square;(r;\mathsf{ret}\;(x,\square))

Our premonoidal structure is then completely described by requiring that
\mathsf{ret}
preserves all
relevant structure, i.e., that we have

(83)

\alpha=\mathsf{ret}\;\alpha\qquad\lambda=\mathsf{ret}\;\lambda\qquad\rho=\mathsf{ret}\;\rho\qquad\sigma=\mathsf{ret}\;\sigma\qquad\Delta=\mathsf{ret}\;\Delta

Just like for expressions, we can write the coproduct of

\Gamma,\square:A\vdash s\rhd\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(C)
and

\Gamma,\square:B\vdash t\rhd\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(C)

in
\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})
as

(84)

\Gamma,\square:A+B\vdash[s,t]:=\mathsf{case}\;\square\;\{\iota_{l}\;{\square}:s,\iota_{r}\;{\square}:t\}\rhd\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(C)

with the obvious injections
\iota_{l}:=\mathsf{ret}\;\iota_{l}
and
\iota_{r}=\mathsf{ret}\;\iota_{r}
. It
turns out that in this case
\mathsf{ret}
preserves coproducts as well, and we can therefore easily
conclude that our category is distributive by taking inverse distributor
\delta^{-1}=\mathsf{ret}\;\delta^{-1}
.

All that remains now is to take
\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})
from an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
expression model
to an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model by giving it an Elgot structure. We do so by defining the fixpoint
of a morphism
\Gamma,\square:A\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(B+A)
as follows:

(85)

\Gamma,\square:A\vdash r^{\dagger}:=\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{go}\;\square\;\mathsf{where}\;\mathsf{go}(\square:A):\{r;\mathsf{case}\;\square\;\{\iota_{l}\;{x}:\mathsf{ret}\;x,\iota_{r}\;{y}:\mathsf{br}\;\mathsf{go}\;y\}\}\rhd\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(B)

where
\mathsf{go}
is an (arbitrary) fresh label. We can verify this indeed satisfies the axioms of
an Elgot structure through a somewhat tedious calculation. We may now note that

(86)

\displaystyle\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1},

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Delta,x:{A}^{\epsilon}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}\otimes A

\displaystyle\qquad\implies\qquad\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}

\displaystyle=\langle\Gamma\rangle

\displaystyle\llbracket{\cdot^{+}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle\llbracket{\mathsf{K},\ell(A)}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}+A

\displaystyle\qquad\implies\qquad\llbracket{\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathsf{K}\rangle

and hence that

\Gamma,\square:\langle\Delta\rangle\vdash\llbracket{\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}\rhd\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(A)\qquad\Gamma,\square:\langle\Delta\rangle\vdash\llbracket{\Delta\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}\rhd\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(\langle\mathsf{K}\rangle)

as expected. It is relatively easy to derive that

\Gamma,\square:\langle\Delta\rangle\vdash\llbracket{\Delta\vdash_{\epsilon}a:{A}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}\approx\mathsf{ret}\;\langle a\rangle\rhd{\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(A)}

by a relatively straightforward induction. A much more tedious induction is required to prove that

\Gamma,\square:\langle\Delta\rangle\vdash\llbracket{\Delta\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{K}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\Gamma,\mathsf{L})}\approx[r]\rhd{\mathsf{L},\blacksquare(\langle\mathsf{K}\rangle)}

since the case for
\mathsf{where}
-statements is particularly complex. With a little bit more book-keeping (which can be found in the mechanization), we can state the completeness theorem as follows:

Theorem 5.15 (name=Completeness (Regions), restate=completenessregions).

We have that, for all pure
\mathsf{eff}(\Gamma)=\bot
,

\Gamma\vdash r\approx r^{\prime}\rhd{\mathsf{L}}\iff\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash r\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\cdot,\cdot)}=\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash r^{\prime}\rhd\mathsf{L}}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Th}(\cdot,\cdot)}

In particular, this implies that
\mathsf{Th}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the initial
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model.

Proof.

See Appendix
B.6

∎

6.
Concrete Models

6.1.
Monads and Monad Transformers

As previously stated, every strong monad over a CCC, by virtue of providing a
model of higher-order effectful programming,
also
provides a model of
first-order effectful programming, i.e., (acyclic) SSA. In particular, the Kleisli
category
\mathsf{C}_{\mathsf{T}}
of every such strong monad
\mathsf{T}
is a premonoidal category and
induces a Freyd category with pure morphisms given by

(87)

(\mathsf{C}_{\mathsf{T}})_{\bot}(A,B)=\{f;\eta_{B}:A\to\mathsf{T}B\mid f:A\to B\}\subseteq\mathsf{C}(A,\mathsf{T}B)=\mathsf{C}_{\mathsf{T}}(A,B)

It follows that every strong Elgot monad over a CCC with all coproducts is an

\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model (since, in particular, every CCC with coproducts is
distributive). This allows us to very quickly amass a large collection of

\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
models from the literature on strong Elgot monads.

Probably the simplest example of an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model is given by the

option monad

\mathsf{Option}\;A=A+\mathbf{1}
, with fixpoint operation

f^{\dagger}=\mathsf{some}\;b\quad\text{if}\quad\exists n,f^{n}\;a=\mathsf{some}\;(\iota_{l}\;b)\qquad f^{\dagger}=\mathsf{none}\quad\text{otherwise}

where

f^{0}\;a=\mathsf{some}\;(\iota_{r}\;a)\qquad f^{n+1}\;a=\mathsf{bind}\;(f^{n}\;a)\;[(\mathsf{pure};\iota_{l}),f]

This model allows us to interpret potentially divergent but otherwise purely functional programs.
Another very important
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model is generated by the
powerset monad

\mathcal{P}
over

\mathsf{Set}
, which has fixpoint operation

f^{\dagger}\;a=\bigcup_{n}\{b\mid\iota_{l}\;b\in f^{n}\;a\}

We can use this monad to interpret
nondeterministic
potentially divergent functional
programs. We can further expand our repertoire of
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
models by considering
monad
transformers
, many of which preserve Elgot-ness. For example, if
\mathsf{T}
is a strong Elgot over

\mathsf{Set}
,

•

For all types
R
, the
reader transformer

\mathsf{ReaderT}\;R\;\mathsf{T}\;A=R\to\mathsf{T}\;A
is strong Elgot
with monad operations

(88)

\eta\;a=\lambda-.\eta_{\mathsf{T}}\;a\qquad\mathsf{bind}\;a\;f=\lambda r.\mathsf{bind}_{\mathsf{T}}\;(a\;r)\;(\lambda a.f\;a\;r)

and fixpoint

(89)

f^{\dagger}=\lambda r.(\lambda a.f\;a\;r)^{\dagger_{\mathsf{T}}}

•

For all monoids
W
, the
writer transformer

\mathsf{WriterT}\;W\;\mathsf{T}\;A=\mathsf{T}\;(A\times W)
is strong Elgot
with monad operations

(90)

\eta\;a=\eta_{\mathsf{T}}\;(a,1)\qquad\mathsf{bind}\;a\;f=\mathsf{bind}_{\mathsf{T}}\;a\;(\lambda(a,w).\mathsf{bind}_{\mathsf{T}}\;(f\;a)\;(\lambda(b,w^{\prime}).(b,w\cdot w^{\prime})))

and fixpoint

(91)

f^{\dagger}\;a=(\lambda(a,w).\mathsf{bind}_{\mathsf{T}}\;(f\;a)\;(\lambda(b,w^{\prime}).\eta_{\mathsf{T}}([(\lambda c.\,(c,w\cdot w^{\prime})),(\lambda c.\,(c,w\cdot w^{\prime}))]\;b)))^{\dagger_{\mathsf{T}}}(a,1)

•

For all types
S
, the
state transformer

\mathsf{StateT}\;S\;\mathsf{T}\;A=S\to\mathsf{T}(A\times S)
is strong Elgot
with monad operations

(92)

\eta\;a=\lambda s.\eta_{\mathsf{T}}\;(a,s)\qquad\mathsf{bind}\;a\;f=\lambda s.\mathsf{bind}_{\mathsf{T}}\;(a\;s)\;(\lambda(a,s^{\prime}).f\;a\;s^{\prime})

and fixpoint

(93)

f^{\dagger}=\lambda s.(\lambda(a,s^{\prime}).\mathsf{bind}_{\mathsf{T}}\;(f\;a\;s^{\prime})\;(\lambda(b,s^{\prime}).\eta_{\mathsf{T}}([(\lambda c.\,(c,s^{\prime})),(\lambda c.(c,s^{\prime}))]\;b)))^{\dagger_{\mathsf{T}}}

One other important source of
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
models is via
Elgot submonads

of monads on
\mathsf{Set}
, which we define as follows:

Definition 6.1 (Elgot submonad).

A monad
(\mathsf{S},\eta^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is a submonad of
(\mathsf{T},\eta,\mu)
if

\eta=\eta^{\prime}
,
\forall A,\mathsf{S}\;A\subseteq\mathsf{T}\;A
, and, on the set

\mathsf{S}\;(\mathsf{S}\;A)\subseteq\mathsf{T}\;(\mathsf{T}\;A)
,
\mu=\mu^{\prime}
. We say

\mathsf{S}
is
strong Elgot
if
\mathsf{T}
is strong Elgot and, for all
f\in A\to\mathsf{S}\;(A+B)
,
f^{\dagger}\in A\to\mathsf{S}\;B
(i.e. the fixpoint
operation sends the Kleisli category of
\mathsf{S}
to itself).

One useful property this definition has is that the intersection of arbitrarily many (Elgot)
submonads forms an (Elgot) submonad, i.e., the (Elgot) submonads form a complete lattice.

6.2.
Trace Models

A particularly important class of
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
models are what we will call

trace models
, which can be viewed as programs which either diverge or
produce a result, while also producing (potentially nondeterministic,
potentially infinite) traces of events
\epsilon\in\mathcal{E}
.

Definition 6.2 ((Monoidal) Stream Action).

A
stream action

(M,I,\cdot,\Sigma)
is a set
M
of
finite
effects
and set
I
of
divergent effects
equipped with a binary
function (“action”)
\cdot:M\times I\to I
and a function
\Sigma:M^{\omega}\to I

mapping infinite streams of
M
to an element of
I
satisfying
\Sigma\sigma=\sigma_{0}\cdot\Sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}
, where
\Sigma_{i}\sigma_{i}=\Sigma(\lambda i.\sigma_{i})
. If
M
is a monoid and
(M,I,\cdot)
a monoid
action, we say we have a
monoidal
stream action

The most important example of a monoidal stream action is the
free

monoidal stream action over a set
E
, called the set of
events
, where:

•

M=E^{*}
is the free monoid over a set of “events"
\epsilon\in E
, i.e., the
monoid of finite lists of events

•

I=E^{\leq\omega}
is the set of
potentially
infinite
streams of events
\epsilon\in E

•

m\cdot i
is defined in the obvious manner, i.e. by prepending the (finite) list
m
to
the (potentially infinite) stream
i

•

\Sigma\;\sigma
is defined as the supremum
\bigsqcup_{i}t_{i}
, where
t\leq t^{\prime}
iff
t
is a prefix of
t^{\prime}
,
t_{0}=\bot
(the empty stream), and

t_{i+1}=t_{i}\cdot\sigma_{i}

Other important examples include:

•

The free stream action over a set of events
E
, where
M=E
,
I=E^{\omega}
(the set of infinite streams of events
\epsilon
),
m\cdot i
is
merely prepending
m
to the stream
i
, and
\Sigma\sigma=\sigma

•

The terminal stream action over an arbitrary set
M
, where
I=\mathbf{1}
. This is always a monoidal stream action if
M
is a monoid

Definition 6.3 (Trace Monad Transformer).

Given a monoidal stream action
(M,I,\cdot,\Sigma)
, we can define the
trace
monad transformer
over
\mathsf{Set}
as follows:

\mathsf{TraceT}\;\Sigma\;\mathsf{T}\;A=\mathsf{T}\;(A\times M+I)

with monad operations

\eta=(\lambda a.\iota_{l}(a,0));\eta_{\mathsf{T}}\qquad\mathsf{bind}\;a\;f=\mathsf{bind}_{\mathsf{T}}\;a\;[\lambda(a,m).m\cdot fa,\lambda i.\eta_{\mathsf{T}}(\iota_{r}\;i)]

This yields a family of useful Elgot monads, including

•

\mathsf{Traces}?\;\Sigma=\mathsf{TraceT}\;\Sigma\;\mathcal{P}
, with the fixpoint of

f:A\to B+A
given by

(94)

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}\;a

\displaystyle=f^{\dagger}_{\infty}\;a\cup\bigcup_{n}f^{\dagger}_{n}\;a\qquad\qquad\text{where}

(95)

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}_{n}\;a

\displaystyle=\{\iota_{l}\;(b,m)\mid\iota_{l}(\iota_{l}\;b,m)\in[\mathsf{id}_{B},f]^{n}(\iota_{r}\;a)\}\cup\{\iota_{r}\;i\mid\iota_{r}\;i\in[\mathsf{id}_{B},f]^{n}(\iota_{r}\;a)\}

(96)

\displaystyle f^{\infty}\;a

\displaystyle=\{\iota_{r}\Sigma\sigma\mid\exists\alpha:A^{\omega},\alpha_{0}=a\land\forall i,\iota_{l}\;(\iota_{r}\;\alpha_{i+1},\sigma_{i})\in f\;\alpha_{i}\}

We say a morphism
f:A\to\mathsf{Traces}?\;\Sigma\;B
is
total
if all
f\;a
are
nonempty, and
deterministic
if all
f\;a
are subsingletons.

•

\mathsf{Traces}\;\Sigma=\mathsf{TraceT}\;\Sigma\;\mathcal{P}^{+}
, which can be viewed as an Elgot
submonad of
\mathsf{Traces}?\;\Sigma
since the fixpoint of a total function is total

•

\mathsf{Trace}?\;\Sigma=\mathsf{TraceT}\;\Sigma\;\mathsf{Option}
, which can be viewed as an Elgot
submonad of
\mathsf{Traces}?\;\Sigma
since the fixpoint of a deterministic function is
deterministic

•

\mathsf{Trace}\;\Sigma=\mathsf{TraceT}\;\Sigma\;\mathsf{Id}
, which can be viewed as an Elgot
submonad of
\mathsf{Traces}?\;\Sigma
, and in particular the intersection

\mathsf{Trace}?\;\Sigma\cap\mathsf{Traces}\;\Sigma
.

As a simple, concrete example of this type of model this construction lets us define, consider the

nondeterministic printing monad
, which is simply defined as
\mathsf{Print}\;A\equiv\mathsf{Traces}\;\Sigma
, where
\Sigma:(\mathsf{byte}^{*})^{\omega}\to\mathsf{byte}^{\leq\omega}
denotes
concatenation of bytestrings into a (potentially infinite) bytestream. This gives us us an

\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model supporting effectful instructions
\mathsf{print}:\mathsf{byte}^{*}\to\mathbf{1}
and

\mathsf{nondet}:\mathbf{1}\to A
(for
A
nonempty), with semantics
\llbracket{\mathsf{print}}\rrbracket=\lambda b.\{\iota_{0}((),b)\}
,
\llbracket{\mathsf{nondet}}\rrbracket=\lambda().\{\iota_{0}(a,[])\mid a\in A\}
.

We can make things a little more interesting by adding a heap to the mix, defining our monad

\mathsf{Comp}=\mathsf{StateT}\;\mathsf{Heap}\;\mathsf{Print}
, where
\mathsf{Heap}=\mathbb{N}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{N}
is simply a partial function with finite support. The Kleisli category of this monad is also
a Freyd category and inherits an Elgot structure from that on
\mathsf{Set}_{\mathsf{Print}}
; we
therefore have an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model supporting the instructions
\mathsf{set}:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{1}
,
\mathsf{get}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
,
\mathsf{alloc}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N})
, and
\mathsf{free}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{1}
. We can assign semantics to
\mathsf{set}
in the standard fashion, with
\llbracket{\mathsf{set}}\rrbracket=\lambda(p,v)\;h.\{\iota_{0}((),[p\mapsto v]h,[])\}
.
\mathsf{get}
is a bit more tricky, since
it is unclear what to do when we try to access uninitialized memory: one option is simply to return
an arbitrary value, with
\llbracket{\mathsf{get}}\rrbracket=\lambda p\;h.\{\iota_{0}(v,h,[])\mid v=h\;p\lor p\notin h\}
. Finally,
\llbracket{\mathsf{alloc}}\rrbracket=\lambda v\;h.\{\iota_{0}\;(p,h^{\prime},[])\mid h^{\prime}=h\sqcup p\mapsto v\}
simply fills a random empty heap cell with the provided value, returning a pointer to
the cell, while
\llbracket{\mathsf{free}}\rrbracket=\lambda p\;h.\{\iota_{0}\;((),h\setminus p,[])\}
.

As we can see, the trace monad is a very powerful tool for quickly constructing families of

\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
models, at least for relatively simple side-effects. It turns out, however, that the
same techniques can be used to tackle much more complex side-effects; to demonstrate this, we will
show how to build a simple model of TSO weak memory based on that given in
Kavanagh and Brookes (2018)
.

6.3.
TSO weak memory

To start, we need to construct an appropriate stream action monoid to take our traces over. For
simplicity, we will consider an infinite set of named locations
x,y,z\in\mathsf{Loc}
which are
subject to concurrent modification by all threads via TSO reads, writes, and fences. We begin with
the following definitions:

Definition 6.4 (Pomset).

A
pomset

\alpha
over a set of actions
\mathcal{A}
with a distinguished null action
"
tick
"
\delta\in\mathcal{A}
is a
nonempty
partially-ordered
carrier set

P
such that every
p\in P
has finitely many predecessors equipped with a mapping
\alpha:P\to\mathcal{A}
. A pomset is
finite
if its carrier set is. We will quotient pomsets over the
removal of arbitrarily many copies of
\delta
as long as infinite sets are only equated to other
infinite pomsets. We will write unordered pomsets using multiset notation, e.g.
\{a,a,b\}
,
and linearly ordered pomsets using list notation, e.g.
[a,a,b]
.

(Finite) pomsets form a monoid under sequential composition
\alpha;\beta
, which is defined by
the function
[\alpha,\beta]:\mathsf{trim}(P+Q)\to\mathcal{A}
, where
P+Q
is given the
lexicographic ordering and
\mathsf{trim}(R)
removes all elements of
R
with infinitely many
predecessors. They also form a monoid under parallel composition, defining
\alpha||\beta=[\alpha,\beta]:P+Q\to\mathcal{A}
where
P+Q
is given the standard partial ordering (with
elements of
P
and
Q
incomparable). Note in both cases the monoidal unit is
\{\delta\}
,
since we only consider nonempty pomsets but allow the removal of finitely many ticks. Given any
partially ordered set
N
and a family of pomsets
\alpha_{n}
for
n\in N
, we can define
their
sum

\Sigma_{n}\alpha_{n}
to be given by the function
(n,a)\mapsto\alpha_{n}\;a:\mathsf{trim}(\Sigma_{n}P_{n})\to\mathcal{A}
, where the dependent product
\Sigma_{n}P_{n}
is given the
lexicographic order. In particular, choosing
N=\mathbb{N}
makes
\Sigma:\mathsf{Pom}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{\omega}\to\mathsf{Pom}
into a stream action monoid w.r.t the sequential
composition monoid on finite pomsets.

We define a
program order pomset
to be a pomset with
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{PO}}=\mathcal{A}_{w}\cup\mathcal{A}_{r}\cup\{\delta\}
, where
\mathcal{A}_{r}
consists of
reads
of the form
x=v
and

\mathcal{A}_{w}
consists of
writes
of the form
x:=v
for locations
x
and values
v\in\mathsf{Word}
. We define the
program order monad

\mathsf{PO}\;A=\mathsf{Traces}\;\Sigma\;A
,
where
\Sigma
is taken as a stream action on finite program order pomsets. This yields an

\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
model with support for concurrent read and write operations
\mathsf{read}_{x}:\mathcal{I}^{\varnothing}_{0}(\mathbf{1},\mathsf{Word})
,
\mathsf{write}_{x}:\mathcal{I}^{\varnothing}_{0}(\mathsf{Word},\mathbf{1})

with semantics

\llbracket{\mathsf{read}_{x}}\rrbracket=R_{x}^{\mathsf{PO}}=\lambda().\{(v,\{x=v\})|v\in\mathsf{Word}\}
,

\llbracket{\mathsf{write}_{x}}\rrbracket=W_{x}^{\mathsf{PO}}=\lambda v.\{((),\{x:=v\})\}
.
The semantics of
\mathsf{read}
in particular give a hint as to how this model works: rather than
tracking the state of the heap, we simply
emit
a pomset of the events that would have been
generated by a given execution (in the case of a read, a read event
x=v
whenever the read
returns
v
, and in the case of a write, the single write event
x:=v
for a write of
v
),
and later post-filter to obtain a set of valid executions. By doing so, our semantics remains
compositional, allowing us to reason about each program fragment individually, while still allowing
other program fragments to perform concurrent operations affecting our potential executions. On that
note, we can define the
parallel execution
of two morphisms as follows:

(97)

\displaystyle f_{0}||f_{1}=\lambda(a_{0},a_{1}).

\displaystyle\{\iota_{0}((b_{0},b_{1}),\alpha_{0}||\alpha_{1})\mid\iota_{0}(b_{i},\alpha_{i})\in f_{i}\;a_{i}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{\iota_{1}(\alpha_{0}||\alpha_{1})\mid(\iota_{0}(b_{0},\alpha_{0})\in f_{0}\;a\lor\iota_{0}\;\alpha_{0}\in f_{0}\;a_{0})\land\iota_{1}\alpha_{1}\in f_{1}\;a_{1}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{\iota_{1}(\alpha_{0}||\alpha_{1})\mid\iota_{1}\;\alpha_{0}\in f_{0}\;a_{0}\land\iota_{0}(a_{1},\alpha_{1})\in f_{1}\;a_{1}\}:\mathsf{Set}_{\mathsf{PO}}(A\otimes A^{\prime},B\otimes B^{\prime})

It’s tempting to try to use this to define a tensor product of morphisms to obtain a monoidal
(rather than premonoidal) category, but unfortunately, sliding still fails:
(W_{x}^{\mathsf{PO}}||\mathsf{id});(\mathsf{id}||W_{y}^{\mathsf{PO}})
emits the pomset
[x:=a,y:=b]
, while
(\mathsf{id}||W_{y}^{\mathsf{PO}});(W_{x}^{\mathsf{PO}}||\mathsf{id})
emits the pomset
[y:=b,x:=a]
.

To graduate from sequential consistency to a genuine, if maximally simple, weak memory model, we
introduce
load buffering
of write actions. We will implement TSO ordering by buffering all
our writes, in which case they are only visible to the local thread. On the other hand, read events
will first attempt to read from the buffer, and, if there is no corresponding write in the buffer,
will read an arbitrary value, in both cases pushing an event to the global pomset. At any point, we
may choose to
flush
some of the buffered writes. We introduce the set
\mathcal{A}_{b}=\{(\overline{x}:=v)\}
of
buffer write actions
, where an action
\overline{x}:=v
by a
thread denotes adding a write
x:=v
to the thread’s write buffer. We may then define the set of
TSO actions
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{TSO}}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{PO}}\cup\mathcal{A}_{b}
; a pomset over this set is called
a
TSO pomset
. A buffer will be defined to be a list of write actions
\mathsf{Buf}=\mathcal{A}_{b}^{*}
, which we will interpret as linear pomsets over
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{TSO}}
ordered by index
(with the empty list corresponding to
\{\delta\}
). In particular, we define the monad
\mathsf{TSO}=\mathsf{StateT}\;\mathsf{Buf}\;(\mathsf{Trace}\;\Sigma)
, where
\Sigma
is taken as a the stream action of
finite TSO pomsets on TSO pomsets. We can view
\mathsf{PO}
as a submonad of this monad.

Given a buffer
\mathsf{Buf}
, we can define the result of reads
[\cdot]_{x}:\mathsf{Buf}\to\mathsf{Word}\sqcup\{\bot\}
from the buffer by induction to be:

(L;\{\overline{x}:=v\})[x]=v,
,

(L;\{\overline{y}:=v\})[x]=L[x]
,

[][x]=\bot
.
Note that writes at the
end
of the buffer are prioritized, since later writes overwrite
earlier ones!

The semantics of reads and writes can then be given by

(98)

\displaystyle\llbracket{\mathsf{read}_{x}}\rrbracket=R_{x}^{\mathsf{TSO}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pflush};(\lambda()\;L.\{(v,L,\{x=v\})\mid L[x]=v\lor L[x]=\bot\});\mathsf{pflush}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\mathsf{write}_{x}}\rrbracket=W_{x}^{\mathsf{TSO}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pflush};(\lambda v\;L.\{(v,(L;\{\overline{x}:=v\}),\{x:=v\})\});\mathsf{pflush}

where buffer flushing is implemented via the morphism

\mathsf{pflush}_{A}=\lambda a\;L.\{(a,R,\alpha)|L=\alpha;R\}:\mathsf{Set}_{\mathsf{TSO}}(A,A)

(called
\mathsf{split}
in
(
Kavanagh and Brookes, 2018
)
). To be able to perform synchronization, we will also need
to introduce a
\mathsf{fence}:\mathcal{I}^{\varnothing}_{0}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})
instruction, which simply causes
all actions before the fence to be observed before any actions after the fence. For
\mathsf{TSO}
,
implementing this is as simple as flushing the buffer, i.e., we define

\llbracket{\mathsf{fence}}\rrbracket=\lambda()\;L.\{((),[],L;\{\delta\})\}

We can now define the parallel composition of morphisms in a "fork-join" style as follows: we first
flush the buffer completely (i.e., taking it and sticking it at the beginning of our pomset), then
execute
f
and
g
in parallel with separate buffers,
filtering out executions which
completely flush the buffer
. Since both threads end up with an empty buffer, the resulting joined
buffer is also empty, giving us the following definition:

(99)

\displaystyle f_{0}||f_{1}=\lambda(a_{0},a_{1})\;L.

\displaystyle\{\iota_{0}((b_{0},b_{1}),[],L;(\alpha_{0}||\alpha_{1}))\mid\iota_{0}(b_{i},[],\alpha_{i})\in f_{i}\;a_{i}\;[]\}

\displaystyle\cup\{\iota_{1}(\alpha_{0}||\alpha_{1})\mid(\iota_{0}(b_{0},[],\alpha_{0})\in f_{0}\;a\lor\iota_{0}\;\alpha_{0}\in f_{0}\;a_{0}\;[])\land\iota_{1}\alpha_{1}\in f_{1}\;a_{1}\;[]\}

\displaystyle\cup\{\iota_{1}(\alpha_{0}||\alpha_{1})\mid\iota_{1}\;\alpha_{0}\in f_{0}\;a_{0}\;[]\land\iota_{0}(a_{1},[],\alpha_{1})\in f_{1}\;a_{1}\;[]\}

One issue with this model is that our category currently has “too many” operations besides the
ones we want: for example, it is a perfectly valid morphism to simply add an event into the buffer
without any flushing. Of course, one thing we could do is consider the smallest Elgot subcategory
generated by the atomic instructions we want to support, such as
\mathsf{read}_{x}
,
\mathsf{write}_{x}
,
and
\mathsf{fence}
, which can be a perfectly valid approach, but is somewhat unwieldy. Another
approach, however, is to think about what “valid” morphisms might look like in general; this
also has the benefit of letting us write down specifications that may not be trivially implementable
in terms of actual instructions.

There are many potential properties we might want “valid” morphisms to have; in general, the less
valid morphisms we admit, the more equations we have to work with when reasoning. One simple
property we might consider is that a “valid” morphism must have at least one execution in which
the buffer is completely flushed, regardless of initial state. This is a perfectly reasonable
property to impose, since it ensures that we do not encounter degenerate cases where, for example,
the parallel composition of two processes has an empty trace set even though both processes have a
nonempty trace set (since neither empties the buffer). We might even want to go a little bit further
and, representing the fact that a buffer flush can happen at any time between instructions, require
that a nondeterministic buffer flush before or after an instruction does not change its semantics,
i.e., that
\mathsf{pflush}_{A};f;\mathsf{pflush}_{B}=f
. Unfortunately, this does
not
hold for
the identity, since

(100)

\mathsf{pflush};\mathsf{id};\mathsf{pflush}=\mathsf{pflush};\mathsf{pflush}=\mathsf{pflush}\neq\mathsf{id}

so this would not give us a subcategory. While one possible solution is to simply add the identity
back in (and this would give us a valid subcategory), we have the issue that, for example, morphisms
like the associator and unitor would still be excluded. Instead, we can take into account the fact
that all
\mathsf{pflush}_{A}
are idempotent and define a new category
\mathsf{PTSO}
with objects sets and
morphisms of the form

(101)

\mathsf{PTSO}(A,B)=\{\mathsf{pflush}_{A};f;\mathsf{pflush}_{B}\mid f\in\mathsf{Set}_{\mathsf{TSO}}(A,B)\}

In this category,
\mathsf{pflush}_{A}
is the identity on
A
, since

(102)

\displaystyle\mathsf{pflush}_{A};\mathsf{pflush}_{A};f;\mathsf{pflush}_{B}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pflush}_{A};f;\mathsf{pflush}_{B}

\displaystyle\mathsf{pflush}_{A};f;\mathsf{pflush}_{B};\mathsf{pflush}_{B}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pflush}_{A};f;\mathsf{pflush}_{B}

This is especially useful since it frees us from needing to remember to sprinkle
\mathsf{pflush}

everywhere when specifying things (and, of course, when that level of control is desired, we can
specify in
\mathsf{Set}_{\mathsf{TSO}}
and then lift to
\mathsf{PTSO}
).

It turns out that this is a general construction we can do, which gives us another useful way to
build up
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
models that can often be quite difficult to express as simply monads.
In particular, we have the following definition

Definition 6.5 (Idempotent envelope category).

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and
d
be a family of morphisms for each
A\in|\mathcal{C}|
such that
each
d_{A}:\mathcal{C}(A,A)
is idempotent, i.e.,
d_{A};d_{A}=d_{A}
. We may then define the
subsemicategory
\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)
of
\mathcal{C}
to be given by

(103)

\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)(A,B)=\{f\in\mathcal{C}(A,B)\mid f=d_{A};f;d_{B}\}=\{d_{A};f;d_{B}\mid f\in\mathcal{C}(A,B)\}

Note that
\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)
is
not
a subcategory unless
d_{A}=\mathsf{id}_{A}
for all
A
,
in which case
\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)=\mathcal{C}
. However,
\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)
can be

independently
viewed as a category with identity
d_{A}
at
A
.

We have that, if
\mathcal{C}
has coproducts and
d_{A+B}=d_{A}+d_{B}
, then
\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)

inherits coproducts from
\mathcal{C}
with injections

(104)

\displaystyle d_{A};\iota_{l}

\displaystyle=\iota_{l};d_{A+B}

\displaystyle=d_{A};\iota_{l};d_{A+B}

\displaystyle\in\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)(A,A+B)

\displaystyle d_{B};\iota_{r}

\displaystyle=\iota_{r};d_{A+B}

\displaystyle=d_{B};\iota_{r};d_{A+B}

\displaystyle\in\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)(B,A+B)

since, for
f\in\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)(A,C)
,
g\in\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)(A,C)
, we have that

(105)

d_{A+B};[f,g];d_{C}=d_{A}+B;[f,g];d_{C}=[d_{A};f;d_{C},d_{B};g;d_{C}]=[f,g]

In particular, it follows that
\mathsf{Ide}_{d}
preserves coproducts. If
\mathcal{C}
in addition has an
Elgot structure, then
\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)
also inherits this structure (and therefore
\mathsf{Ide}_{d}

preserves it), as for
f\in\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)(A,B+A)
, we have

(106)

f^{\dagger}=(d_{A};f;d_{B}+d_{A})^{\dagger}=(d_{A};f;d_{B}+d_{A})^{\dagger};d_{B}=d_{A};(f;d_{B}+(d_{A};d_{A}))^{\dagger};d_{B}=d_{A};f^{\dagger};d_{B}

On the other hand, if
\mathcal{C}
is equipped with a (symmetric) premonoidal structure and we have that

(107)

d_{A\otimes B}=d_{A}\ltimes d_{B}=d_{A}\rtimes d_{B}\qquad d_{A\otimes I}=d_{A}\otimes I\qquad d_{I\otimes A}=I\otimes d_{A}

(note that
d
does
not
have to be central!), it follows that
\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)
has (symmetric) premonoidal structure as well, with inherited tensor product functors and
associators, unitors, and symmetries given by

(108)

\begin{gathered}d_{(A\otimes B)\otimes C};\alpha_{A,B,C}=\alpha_{A,B,C};d_{A\otimes(B\otimes C)}=d_{(A\otimes B)\otimes C};\alpha_{A,B,C};d_{A\otimes(B\otimes C)}\\
d_{A\otimes I};\lambda_{A}=\lambda_{A};d_{A}=d_{A\otimes I};\lambda_{A};d_{A}\qquad d_{I\otimes A};\rho_{A}=\rho_{A};d_{A}=d_{I\otimes A};\rho_{A};d_{A}\\
d_{A\otimes B};\sigma_{A,B}=\sigma_{A,B};d_{B\otimes A}=d_{A\otimes B};\sigma_{A,B};d_{B\otimes A}\end{gathered}

respectively. It follows that if
\mathcal{C}
is distributive, so is
\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)
, since we can
verify that the distributor
[d_{A}\otimes(d_{B};\iota_{l}),d_{A}\otimes(d_{C};\iota_{r})]
has the
expected inverse

(109)

d_{A\otimes(B+C)};\delta^{-1};d_{(A\otimes B)+(A\otimes C)}

If we in fact have that
d_{A\otimes B}=A\otimes d_{B}=d_{A}\otimes B
for all
A,B
(which implies Equation
107
) and
\mathcal{C}
is equipped with the structure
of a Freyd category, then so is
\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)
, with projections and diagonal given by

(110)

\begin{gathered}d_{A\otimes B};\pi_{l}=\pi_{l};d_{A}=d_{A\otimes B};\pi_{l};d_{A}\qquad d_{A\otimes B};\pi_{r}=\pi_{r};d_{B}=d_{A\otimes B};\pi_{r};d_{B}\\
d_{A};\Delta=\Delta;d_{A\otimes A}=d_{A};\Delta;d_{A\otimes A}\end{gathered}

Hence, it follows that if
\mathcal{C}
is an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
(expression) model,
d_{A}
is idempotent,

d_{A+B}=d_{A}+d_{B}
, and
d_{A\otimes B}=A\otimes d_{B}=d_{A}\otimes B
, then

\mathsf{Ide}(\mathcal{C},d)
is an
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
(expression) model as well. It turns out that all these
properties hold for
d=\mathsf{pflush}
, giving us our final categorical model for TSO weak memory,

\mathsf{PTSO}=\mathsf{Ide}(\mathsf{Set}_{\mathsf{TSO}},\mathsf{pflush})
.

7.
Discussion and Related Work

7.1.
SSA, FP and IRs

Static Single Assignment (SSA) form was first introduced as a compiler intermediate representation
by
Alpern et al. (1988)
and
Rosen et al. (1988)
, with the goal of facilitating
effective reasoning about program variable equivalence. To perform optimizations like common
subexpression elimination (CSE) effectively, we need to determine which expressions are equal

at a given point in time
. By transforming the program into SSA form, we introduce unique
variables for each assignment and use
\phi
-nodes to merge variable values at points where control
paths converge. Since each variable then corresponds to a unique value at a specific point in the
program’s execution, SSA unlocks the ability to perform
algebraic reasoning
about variable
values over time. As a result, analyses like CSE become a matter of simple algebraic rewriting based
on variable names.

Cytron et al. (1991)
provided the first efficient algorithm for converting 3-address code
programs to SSA form using a minimal number of
\phi
-nodes. They observed that a
\phi
-node only
needs to be introduced at the earliest point where a variable may have different values based on
control flow. This point is computable via the graph-theoretic notion of a
dominance
frontier
, which identifies where control paths merge in the program’s control flow graph. Since
then, SSA has become the intermediate representation of choice for most production-grade
compiler toolchains.

One of the most famous optimizations enabled by SSA’s support for effective algebraic reasoning is

sparse conditional constant propagation (SCCP)
, introduced in
Wegman and Zadeck (1991)
. This
algorithm leverages the SSA property to reason about the possible set of values each variable may
take using a lattice-based data-flow analysis, which models variable values in terms of abstract
states like
unknown
,
constant
, or
overdefined
. Without SSA, one could naively
consider all variable definitions, which would likely detect fewer constants due to imprecision.
Alternatively, achieving more precise results would require a complex and computationally intensive
reaching-definitions analysis.

However, while SSA is an excellent representation for implementing compilers, the semantics of

\phi
-nodes can be quite unintuitive due to their lack of an obvious operational interpretation,
making them challenging to reason about formally.
Kelsey (1995)
establishes a correspondence
between SSA and a subset of
continuation-passing style (CPS)
, a common intermediate
representation for functional compilers. In particular, while not all CPS programs can be directly
converted to SSA form—those using non-local returns like
longjmp
or
call/cc
—the
typical outputs of CPS transformations avoid such features. Kelsey observed that many optimizations
requiring flow analysis in CPS could be performed directly in SSA, often dramatically simplifying
them.

Appel (1998)
builds on this work by informally showing that the functional subset of CPS
programs “hidden inside" SSA is, in fact, simply nested, mutually tail-recursive functions,
with each function corresponding to a basic block. He makes the key observation that the
dominance-based scoping of SSA corresponds to the lexical scoping of functions. For a variable to be
visible in a function, it must appear in the lexical scope of that variable’s definition and
therefore be dominated by it; otherwise, there would be no way to call the function.

In fact, the subset of functional programs identified by
Appel (1998)
corresponds to

A-normal form (ANF)
, another functional intermediate representation advocated by

Flanagan et al. (1993)
.
Chakravarty et al. (2003)
formalizes this correspondence
giving an algorithm to convert SSA programs to ANF, and then showing how SSA optimizations such as
SCCP can be written to operate on ANF programs. The authors highlight that the semantic rigor of
their notation, combined with the well-defined semantics of ANF, make their presentation of SCCP
significantly more amenable to formal analysis.

Going in the other direction,
Leißa et al. (2015)
introduce the Thorin intermediate representation,
which consists of CPS extended with SSA-style dominance-based scoping. As SSA is a first-order
language, it is often difficult to represent programs using closures effectively, and consequently,
difficult to optimize them well. The authors give the example of LLVM, which often needs to generate
a new struct and a large amount of boilerplate code for every closure, which, even after
optimization, is often not significantly reduced. By contrast, Thorin retains the advantages of CPS
for representing functional programs, while enabling SSA-like graph-based use-def analysis by
prohibiting variable shadowing.

The primary focus of the works we have covered so far is establishing the correspondence between
SSA, an imperative intermediate representation, and widely used functional intermediate
representations. However, to advance beyond these correspondences and directly study the
optimization and verification of SSA programs themselves, we require an equational theory
underpinned by formal semantics. One approach found in the literature (and which we also use) is
to relax SSA into forms that support richer substitution principles and well-defined operational
semantics, which can then be used to reason about SSA itself. Two papers which exemplify this
approach are
Benton and Kennedy (1999)
and
Garbuzov et al. (2018)
.

One of the difficulties of working with ANF as an intermediate representation is that it does not,
in general, satisfy
substitution
: replacing a variable
x
with a compound expression like a
function call can take a program out of the ANF-fragment. The
monadic intermediate language
(MIL)
introduced by
Benton and Kennedy (1999)
for use in the MLj compiler for Standard ML can be
viewed as a relaxation of ANF (and hence, of SSA) to get a nicer substitution principle. Benton and
Kennedy then use this flexibility to build up an equational theory justified by their operational
semantics. One interesting feature is that, unlike Moggi’s equational metalanguage

(
Moggi, 1991
)
(and like our
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
calculus), MIL enforces a stratification of

values
and
computations
, without supporting “computations of computations”
(corresponding to nested monad types
\mathsf{T}(\mathsf{T}(A)))
). This hints at Freyd categories, rather
than general Kleisli categories, being a natural model of MIL-like intermediate representations.

Similarly,
Garbuzov et al. (2018)
exhibit a correspondence between an operational
semantics for SSA and an operational semantics for call-by-push-value (CBPV)
(
Levy, 2004
)
. They then
use the normal form bisimulations of
Lassen and Levy (2007)
to derive an equational theory for use in
justifying optimizations. In particular, we can view their paper as interpreting CBPV as a
relaxation of SSA more suited for developing an equational theory and for semantics work; in
particular, to be able to take advantage of CBPV to give a
structural
operational semantics
for (unstructured) SSA programs. The semantics of CBPV are widely studied, and hint at a large
variety of potential models for SSA, but the formalization in
(
Garbuzov et al., 2018
)

does not support any effects other than nontermination.

7.2.
Formalizations of SSA

7.2.1.
Other SSA type systems

Several attempts have been made to provide a type-theoretic treatment of SSA. The work most similar
to ours is by
Rigon et al. (2020)
, who present a typed
translation from SSA into the lambda calculus using a type-and-effect system, observing that the
algorithm for converting programs to SSA form may also be viewed as a mechanism for transforming
programs in a functional language with unstructured control flow into equivalent expressions.

Similarly to
\lambda_{\mathsf{SSA}}
, they extend the lambda calculus with a mutually recursive

where
-binding, which allows them to directly translate unstructured SSA control flow.
However, their calculus uses only a single syntactic category of expressions, whereas we attempt to
model (generalized) SSA directly by distinguishing between expressions and regions. Their language
also includes support for effect handlers, which are beyond the scope of our current study but
represent an interesting direction for future work. While the authors do not provide an equational
theory or a semantics for their language, we believe that our equational theory could be adapted to
the fragment of their language without effect handlers.

An interesting alternative approach is demonstrated by
Matsuno and Ohori (2006)
, who give
a type theory for what appear to be ordinary three-address code programs. However, every well-typed
program can be placed into SSA-form by inserting
\phi
-nodes in a fully type-directed way. This
lets them model SSA without any
\phi
-nodes, letting them use the standard semantics for
three-address code.

Menon et al. (2006)
give a type-safe formalization of SSA, along with an operational semantics
and formal definitions of dominance, definition/use points, and the SSA property for 3-address
code. By augmenting SSA with first-class proof variables, they aim to give a representation which
allows aggressive optimizations to preserve safety information. Their type system requires checking
the SSA property separately from well-typedness, but is proven sound if the SSA property holds.

Hua et al. (2010)
give another type system and direct operational semantics for standard
SSA, and prove type safety for it.

Many operational semantics for SSA have arisen from compiler verification efforts.

Barthe et al. (2014)
give an operational semantics as part of the CompCertSSA project,
and give a semantics-preserving translation from three-address code into SSA.

Herklotz et al. (2023)
formalise "gated SSA" and give semantics-preserving translations between
it and ordinary SSA. Going beyond CompCertSSA,
Zhao et al. (2012)
have studied the semantics of the
LLVM IR itself as part of the Vellvm project. There has been much less work on denotational
semantics for SSA, or directly on its equational theory.
Pop et al. (2009)
give an unusual
denotational model of SSA in terms of the iteration structure of a program, which they use to better
understand the loop-closing
\phi
-nodes found both in the gated SSA representation as well as
practical compilers such as GCC.

7.2.2.
Mechanizations of SSA

CompcertSSA
(
Barthe et al., 2012
)
is an attempt to extend the CompCert verified compiler

(
Leroy, 2009
)
with an SSA-based middle-end. This is achieved by generating a pruned SSA
IR from CompCert’s RTL format. Instead of verifying the RTL-to-SSA translation within Coq, this
translation is performed by unverified code, and a separate
translation validation
stage uses
a verified checker to ensure correctness. After performing (verified) SSA optimizations, the IR is
naively lowered back to RTL for the rest of CompCert’s machinery to work on.
Demange et al. (2015)
build on this work by providing realistic, verified implementations of Sparse Conditional Constant Propagation (SCCP)
(
Wegman and Zadeck, 1991
)
, Common Subexpression Elimination (CSE), and Global Value Numbering (GVN)
(
Rosen et al., 1988
)
within a general framework of flow-insensitive static analysis for CompCertSSA.

While CompCert is a verified implementation of a new compiler in Coq, the Vellvm project

(
Zhao et al., 2012
)
attempts to mechanize a subset of the LLVM intermediate representation (IR),
covering the LLVM type system, operational semantics, and the well-formedness and structural
properties of valid LLVM IR. The authors adopt a memory model for LLVM based on CompCert’s

(
Leroy, 2009
)
, allowing them to leverage significant portions of CompCert’s Coq
infrastructure. Similarly,
Bhat et al. (2024)
attempt to mechanize a well-defined subset
of the Multi-Level Intermediate Representation (MLIR) in the Lean 4 theorem prover. Their
formalization features a user-friendly front end to convert MLIR syntax into their calculus and
scaffolding for defining and verifying peephole rewrites using tactics. Their framework has been
tested on bitvector rewrites from LLVM, structured control flow, and fully homomorphic encryption;
however, as of publication, only structured control flow was fully supported.

7.3.
Compositional (Relaxed) Concurrency

The most natural way to think about concurrency is often in terms of an operational semantics on an
abstract machine, in which concurrent threads interleave and interact. Such semantics, naturally,
are designed to reason about an entire program at a time.
Batty (2017)
argues that
a compositional semantics of concurrency is necessary to be able to reason about properties of large
software systems effectively, particularly in the presence of complicating factors such as compiler
optimizations and weak memory semantics. However, it is generally very challenging to reason about a
small component of a concurrent system in isolation since its behaviour may be drastically affected
by other components running in parallel.

One natural approach to constructing denotational models of concurrency is to consider the
extension of an abstract machine’s behavior. Each thread performs a sequence of atomic actions,
and so from the outside, the machine can produce any interleaving of the atomic actions of each
thread. Just by itself, considering sets of possible traces is not sufficiently abstract, and so

Brookes (1996)
takes the closure of these sets under semantics-preserving
transformations, such as stuttering (introducing extra identity steps) and mumbling (which fuse
sequential atomic operations, thereby hiding implementation details), to obtain a model with good
equational properties such as associativity
(
\llbracket{\alpha;(\beta;\gamma)}\rrbracket=\llbracket{(\alpha;\beta);\gamma}\rrbracket
) and the expected identities for
branches and loops.

If the machine model is
sequentially consistent
– i.e., all allowed behaviors arise from
interleavings of sequences of atomic events – then this style of trace semantics is sufficient.
However, real hardware often exhibits
relaxed behaviour
, in which additional behaviours
which do not correspond to the interleaving of parallel threads are allowed. Even more relaxed
behaviours arise from fundamental compiler optimizations, such as re-ordering of independent reads
and writes. These are perfectly valid in a single-threaded context but can introduce new
behaviours in multithreaded programs — for example, another thread could distinguish the order of
writes. In general, we need tools to reason about interactions with a system that is not

linearizable
(i.e., in which concurrent operations cannot be reduced to interleaved atomic
operations on a single thread), of which actual hardware is only one example. There are two major
approaches to this problem.

One idea we might have is to augment traces with additional structure, which we can then quotient
away to maintain extensionality by using an appropriate closure operator. What’s nice about this
method is that we can often use structures analogous to the additional state in the machine model
which leads to the relaxed behaviour in the first place. For example, in

Jagadeesan et al. (2012)
, the authors extend
Brookes (1996)
with
additional state corresponding to the contents of a thread-local buffer, and then take the closure
of their trace-set with respect to buffer operations such as nondeterministic flushing. This gives
them a model of TSO weak memory with good equational properties and a monadic structure, which
remains intuitive, since it has a connection to the original TSO model which can also be framed in
terms of the abstract machine having thread-local buffers.
Dvir et al. (2024)
use this approach
to derive a trace-based semantics for release-acquire atomics inspired by their operational
semantics, showing the viability of this technique even for very complex memory models.

The idea of augmenting traces leads naturally to
games
, which we can view one variant of as
sequential traces of moves between a
proponent
and an
opponent
. Game semantics were
first used with great success to give a semantics to
sequential computation
; for example,

Abramsky and McCusker (1996)
give an adequate denotational semantics for sequential Algol using
Hyland-Ong games
(
Hyland and Ong, 2000
)
.
Ghica and Murawski (2008)
observe that the sequentiality of Hyland-Ong
games corresponds to a highly constrained, deterministic form of interleaving between concurrent
processes. By generalizing away many of the rules of Hyland-Ong games, and in particular

alternation
(i.e., the proponent and the opponent must take turns), the authors obtain a form
of game-semantics for concurrent programs, which they use to build a fully abstract semantics for

fine-grained concurrency
(in contrast to
Brookes (1996)
, which
implements
coarse-grained
concurrency using the somewhat unrealistic
\mathsf{await}
primitive).

In general, generalizing traces to represent true concurrency leads us to another approach:
replacing sets of
linear
traces with (sets of)
partially ordered
structures that
directly represent the concurrency of their component operations. This approach directly captures
the idea that executing two operations concurrently (i.e., without specifying an order between them)
is fundamentally different from executing them in some particular order. The most basic such data
structure is a
partially ordered multiset
, or
pomset
, which we describe in
Section
6.3
of our paper. Pomsets can naturally model sequential consistency, and, like
traces, can be augmented with additional structure to model relaxed memory models such as TSO weak
memory, as in
Kavanagh and Brookes (2018)
.

One issue with this approach is that it can be very challenging to determine the appropriate
structures to augment pomsets with in order to model more advanced memory models. For example,

Jagadeesan et al. (2020)
introduce
pomsets with preconditions
(PwP), which augment pomsets
with logical formulae, to model weak memory behaviors. However,
Jeffrey et al. (2022)
demonstrate
that sequential composition (the semicolon operator) in PwP is not associative. This lack of
associativity undermines many common program optimizations and breaks the monadic structure
necessary for compositional reasoning. To address this issue,
Jeffrey et al. (2022)
propose

pomsets with predicate transformers
(PwT), which restore the associativity of sequential
composition. Despite this improvement, their semantics still do not support loops, and developing a
monadic structure based on PwT that fully supports recursion and iterative constructs remains future
work.

Another potential generalization of pomsets, inspired by game semantics, is the use of
event
structures
, which introduce a
conflict relation
to represent many potentially conflicting
executions within a single mathematical object.
Castellan (2016)
show how to use event
structures to represent concurrent executions with respect to a memory model. They study a simple
language supporting parallel composition of
n
linear programs defined as lists of loads, stores,
and arithmetic operations, for which event structures provide a denotational semantics.

Paviotti et al. (2020)
use the event structure approach to give denotational
semantics for relaxed memory concurrency in a more realistic language, including semantics for
branches and loops. However, they employ step indexing in their semantics, and as a result, loop
unrolling is only a refinement rather than an equation. Since event structures satisfy the axioms of
axiomatic domain theory
(
Fiore, 1994
)
, it should be possible to modify this semantics into one
where loop unrolling is an equation, at which point it would be a model of our calculus.
